### array
--idea
An array is a row of boxes of equal size, stored side by side in memory and numbered from 0. Because every box has the same size, the computer finds box number `i` by simple arithmetic (start address plus `i` times the box size), so reading or writing any position takes the same tiny time no matter how long the row is. A string is just an array of characters.
The price is that the row is fixed. Putting something in the middle means sliding everything after it one box over, and growing past the end means asking for a bigger row and copying. Almost every other data structure is built by deciding how to get around one of those two limits.
--build
1. Decide the size `n` and the type of each item. Create the row: all positions start at a default value (0, empty, false).
2. Read position `i` with `a[i]`. Valid positions are `0` to `n - 1`. Anything else is a bug (an "index out of range" error, or worse, silent garbage in C++).
3. Write position `i` with `a[i] = v`. Both read and write are O(1).
4. To walk everything, loop `i` from 0 to `n - 1`. Keep a running answer (`sum`, `best`) instead of building new rows when you can.
5. To insert at position `p`, move items `p..n-1` one step right (go from the back so you do not overwrite), then write. To delete, move items `p+1..n-1` one step left.
6. For a growable array (`ArrayList`, Python list, `vector`), keep `size` and `capacity`. When `size == capacity`, allocate a row of double the capacity and copy. Doubling makes the average cost of appending O(1).
--think
- Signal: nearly every problem starts here. The input is a list of numbers or a string, and you need to scan it, count in it, or reorder it.
- Decide first whether you need one pass (running sum, running max), two ends moving inward (two pointers), or a window that slides (sliding window). Many "hard" array problems are one of these three shapes.
- If you will ask many "sum of range" questions, build prefix sums once instead of looping every time. If you need to look things up by value, move to a hash table. If the list must stay sorted while changing, an array is the wrong tool.
- Strings: in Java and Python a string cannot be changed in place. Building a result with `+=` in a loop is O(n^2). Collect pieces in a `StringBuilder` or a list and join once.
- Pitfalls: off-by-one at the last index, reading `a[i + 1]` when `i` is the last index, forgetting that inserting in the middle is O(n), using the same row for input and output while still reading it, and integer overflow when summing many large values (use `long`).
--cost
Read and write O(1). Insert or delete in the middle O(n). Appending to a growable array is O(1) on average. Space O(n).
--java
// insert value v at position p in a[0..n-1]; returns the new length (caller guarantees room)
static int insertAt(int[] a, int n, int p, int v) {
    for (int i = n; i > p; i--) a[i] = a[i - 1];   // shift right, from the back
    a[p] = v;
    return n + 1;
}

// delete position p; returns the new length
static int deleteAt(int[] a, int n, int p) {
    for (int i = p; i < n - 1; i++) a[i] = a[i + 1];   // shift left
    return n - 1;
}

// one pass: largest sum of any run of items (Kadane)
static long maxRun(int[] a) {
    long best = a[0], cur = a[0];
    for (int i = 1; i < a.length; i++) {
        cur = Math.max(a[i], cur + a[i]);   // extend the run or restart here
        best = Math.max(best, cur);
    }
    return best;
}
--py
def insert_at(a, p, v):
    a.append(0)                      # make room
    for i in range(len(a) - 1, p, -1):
        a[i] = a[i - 1]              # shift right, from the back
    a[p] = v

def delete_at(a, p):
    for i in range(p, len(a) - 1):
        a[i] = a[i + 1]              # shift left
    a.pop()

def max_run(a):
    """Largest sum of any non-empty run of items (Kadane)."""
    best = cur = a[0]
    for x in a[1:]:
        cur = max(x, cur + x)        # extend the run or restart here
        best = max(best, cur)
    return best
--cpp
// insert v at position p in a (vector grows by one)
void insertAt(vector<int>& a, int p, int v) {
    a.push_back(0);                          // make room
    for (int i = (int)a.size() - 1; i > p; i--) a[i] = a[i - 1];
    a[p] = v;
}

void deleteAt(vector<int>& a, int p) {
    for (int i = p; i + 1 < (int)a.size(); i++) a[i] = a[i + 1];
    a.pop_back();
}

// one pass: largest sum of any non-empty run (Kadane)
long long maxRun(const vector<int>& a) {
    long long best = a[0], cur = a[0];
    for (size_t i = 1; i < a.size(); i++) {
        cur = max<long long>(a[i], cur + a[i]);   // extend the run or restart here
        best = max(best, cur);
    }
    return best;
}

### matrix
--idea
A matrix is a grid of items with rows and columns, like a spreadsheet or a chessboard. You reach a cell with two numbers, `grid[r][c]`. Grids show up both as real pictures (maps, boards, mazes, images) and as tables of numbers (distances between every pair of places).
Under the hood a grid is a row of rows, or one long row where cell `(r, c)` sits at position `r * cols + c`. Most grid problems come down to two habits: stay inside the borders, and describe the four (or eight) neighbours with small offset lists so you do not write the same code four times.
--build
1. Store `rows` and `cols`. Create `grid` with `rows` rows, each of `cols` cells.
2. Use `inBounds(r, c, rows, cols)` (code piece `u-idx`): it is true when `0 <= r < rows` and `0 <= c < cols`. Call it before every neighbour read. The same piece has `idx(r, c, cols)`, the position of a cell in a flat row.
3. Keep direction lists `dr = {-1, 1, 0, 0}` and `dc = {0, 0, -1, 1}`. The neighbours of `(r, c)` are `(r + dr[k], c + dc[k])` for each `k`. Add the four diagonals for eight-way movement.
4. Visit every cell with two nested loops, rows outside and columns inside. This follows the memory order, so it is fast.
5. To rotate 90 degrees clockwise into a new grid: `res[c][rows - 1 - r] = grid[r][c]`. In place: transpose (swap `[r][c]` with `[c][r]`), then reverse each row.
6. To multiply an `n x m` grid by an `m x p` grid: `res[i][j] += a[i][k] * b[k][j]` for all `i, j, k`.
--think
- Signal: the input is a board, a map of walls and floor, an image, or "n lines of n numbers". Words like island, path, flood, neighbour, spiral, diagonal.
- Decide first what a cell means and what counts as a neighbour. Then decide whether you walk the grid once (counting) or search through it (BFS for shortest steps, DFS for regions). Search on a grid is just graph search where cells are nodes.
- Diagonals: cells on one diagonal share `r - c`, cells on one anti-diagonal share `r + c`. This turns many chess problems into counting in two arrays.
- A table of 2D prefix sums answers "sum of any sub-rectangle" in O(1). If cells also change, use a 2D Fenwick tree.
- Pitfalls: mixing up `rows` and `cols` in a non-square grid, swapping `r` and `c` in `grid[c][r]`, reading outside the border, deep recursion on a big flood fill (use an explicit stack or queue), and modifying the grid while still reading the old values (rotate into a copy, or use the in-place trick).
--cost
Access O(1). Visiting all cells O(rows * cols). Matrix multiplication O(n * m * p). Space O(rows * cols).
--java
static final int[] DR = {-1, 1, 0, 0}, DC = {0, 0, -1, 1};

// number of land cells ('1') connected to (r, c), marking them visited with an explicit stack
static int floodFill(char[][] g, int r, int c) {
    int count = 0;
    java.util.ArrayDeque<int[]> st = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
    g[r][c] = '0'; st.push(new int[]{r, c});
    while (!st.isEmpty()) {
        int[] cur = st.pop(); count++;
        for (int k = 0; k < 4; k++) {
            int nr = cur[0] + DR[k], nc = cur[1] + DC[k];
            if (inBounds(nr, nc, g.length, g[0].length) && g[nr][nc] == '1') {
                g[nr][nc] = '0';            // mark when pushing, so no cell is pushed twice
                st.push(new int[]{nr, nc});
            }
        }
    }
    return count;
}

// rotate an n x n grid 90 degrees clockwise in place
static void rotate(int[][] a) {
    int n = a.length;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        for (int j = i + 1; j < n; j++) { int t = a[i][j]; a[i][j] = a[j][i]; a[j][i] = t; }  // transpose
    for (int[] row : a)
        for (int i = 0, j = n - 1; i < j; i++, j--) { int t = row[i]; row[i] = row[j]; row[j] = t; }  // reverse rows
}
--py
DR = (-1, 1, 0, 0)
DC = (0, 0, -1, 1)

def flood_fill(g, r, c):
    """Count the land cells ('1') connected to (r, c); marks them visited."""
    rows, cols = len(g), len(g[0])
    g[r][c] = '0'
    stack, count = [(r, c)], 0
    while stack:
        cr, cc = stack.pop()
        count += 1
        for k in range(4):
            nr, nc = cr + DR[k], cc + DC[k]
            if in_bounds(nr, nc, rows, cols) and g[nr][nc] == '1':
                g[nr][nc] = '0'          # mark when pushing, so no cell is pushed twice
                stack.append((nr, nc))
    return count

def rotate(a):
    """Rotate a square grid 90 degrees clockwise, in place."""
    n = len(a)
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            a[i][j], a[j][i] = a[j][i], a[i][j]      # transpose
    for row in a:
        row.reverse()                                  # then reverse each row
--cpp
const int DR[4] = {-1, 1, 0, 0}, DC[4] = {0, 0, -1, 1};

// count land cells ('1') connected to (r, c); marks them visited
int floodFill(vector<string>& g, int r, int c) {
    int rows = g.size(), cols = g[0].size(), count = 0;
    vector<pair<int,int>> st = {{r, c}};
    g[r][c] = '0';
    while (!st.empty()) {
        auto [cr, cc] = st.back(); st.pop_back();
        count++;
        for (int k = 0; k < 4; k++) {
            int nr = cr + DR[k], nc = cc + DC[k];
            if (inBounds(nr, nc, rows, cols) && g[nr][nc] == '1') {
                g[nr][nc] = '0';            // mark when pushing, so no cell is pushed twice
                st.push_back({nr, nc});
            }
        }
    }
    return count;
}

// rotate an n x n grid 90 degrees clockwise in place
void rotate(vector<vector<int>>& a) {
    int n = a.size();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        for (int j = i + 1; j < n; j++) swap(a[i][j], a[j][i]);   // transpose
    for (auto& row : a) reverse(row.begin(), row.end());          // then reverse each row
}
--uses
u-idx

### hash
--idea
A hash table stores key and value pairs and finds any key in about one step, without searching. Picture a wall of numbered mailboxes. A "hash function" turns a key (a name, a number) into a mailbox number, so you walk straight to it instead of checking every box. The classic use is counting: "how many times has each word appeared?" becomes one lookup and one add per word.
Two different keys can land in the same mailbox. That is called a collision, and it is normal. The table handles it by letting a mailbox hold a short chain of entries, or by trying the next free mailbox. As long as keys spread out evenly and the table is resized when it gets full, each mailbox holds only a few entries, so lookups stay fast.
--build
1. Make an array `buckets` of size `cap` (start small, like 16). Each bucket is an empty list of `(key, value)` pairs.
2. Hash: `h = hash(key) mod cap`, made non-negative. This is the bucket number.
3. `get(key)`: scan bucket `h` for the pair with that key. Return its value, or "missing".
4. `put(key, value)`: scan bucket `h`. If the key is there, replace the value. Otherwise add a new pair and increase `size`.
5. Keep the load factor `size / cap` below about 0.75. When it goes above, allocate a bucket array of double size and re-insert every pair (the bucket number changes because `cap` changed).
6. `remove(key)`: find the pair in its bucket and delete it, decreasing `size`.
7. Counting pattern: `count[x] = count.get(x, 0) + 1` for each item `x`.
8. Layering: counting pairs calls `countFreq` to count every value (code piece `u-freq`) and `getOrDefault` to read a count that may be missing (code piece `u-hget`).
--think
- Signal: "have I seen this before?", "how many of each?", "find two items that add up to a target", "group items that are equal in some way", "first non-repeating". If the answer is a lookup by value, think hash first.
- Decide first what the key is. Often you must build a good key: a sorted string for anagrams, a tuple of coordinates, a normalised fraction. Two things that should match must produce the same key.
- A prefix sum plus a hash table of earlier prefix sums answers "number of subarrays with sum k" in one pass. This pattern is very common.
- Use a plain array instead when keys are small integers (0..10^6): faster and no collisions. Use a sorted map when you need order (nearest key, range of keys). Use a set when you do not need values.
- Pitfalls: the average cost is O(1) but a bad or attacked hash can make it O(n) (in contests, `unordered_map` and Java `HashMap` with integer keys can be hacked, so mix in a random value or use a custom hash). Changing a key's fields after inserting it. Using `double` keys. Iterating while inserting.
--cost
Average O(1) per get, put and remove. Worst case O(n) when many keys collide. Space O(n).
--java
// a small chained hash table from int keys to int values (shows the mechanism)
static class IntMap {
    int[][] keys, vals; int[] len; int size;
    IntMap() { init(16); }
    void init(int cap) { keys = new int[cap][2]; vals = new int[cap][2]; len = new int[cap]; size = 0; }
    int bucket(int k) { return (k * 0x9E3779B9 >>> 7) & (keys.length - 1); }   // cap is a power of two

    Integer get(int k) {
        int b = bucket(k);
        for (int i = 0; i < len[b]; i++) if (keys[b][i] == k) return vals[b][i];
        return null;
    }

    void put(int k, int v) {
        int b = bucket(k);
        for (int i = 0; i < len[b]; i++) if (keys[b][i] == k) { vals[b][i] = v; return; }
        if (len[b] == keys[b].length) {                       // grow this bucket
            keys[b] = java.util.Arrays.copyOf(keys[b], len[b] * 2);
            vals[b] = java.util.Arrays.copyOf(vals[b], len[b] * 2);
        }
        keys[b][len[b]] = k; vals[b][len[b]++] = v;
        if (++size > keys.length * 3 / 4) rehash();
    }

    void rehash() {                                           // double the table, re-insert all
        int[][] ok = keys, ov = vals; int[] ol = len;
        init(ok.length * 2);
        for (int b = 0; b < ok.length; b++)
            for (int i = 0; i < ol[b]; i++) put(ok[b][i], ov[b][i]);
    }
}

// everyday use: number of pairs (i < j) with a[i] + a[j] == target.
// countFreq builds the counts (piece u-freq), getOrDefault reads one safely (piece u-hget)
static long pairsWithSum(int[] a, int target) {
    java.util.Map<Integer, Integer> f = countFreq(a);
    long pairs = 0;
    for (int x : f.keySet()) {
        int y = target - x;
        if (x < y) pairs += (long) f.get(x) * getOrDefault(f, y, 0);        // each unordered pair once
        else if (x == y) pairs += (long) f.get(x) * (f.get(x) - 1) / 2;      // pairs inside one value
    }
    return pairs;
}
--py
class IntMap:
    """A small chained hash table (shows the mechanism; use dict in real code)."""
    def __init__(self, cap=16):
        self.buckets = [[] for _ in range(cap)]
        self.size = 0

    def _bucket(self, k):
        return self.buckets[hash(k) % len(self.buckets)]

    def get(self, k, default=None):
        for key, val in self._bucket(k):
            if key == k: return val
        return default

    def put(self, k, v):
        b = self._bucket(k)
        for i, (key, _) in enumerate(b):
            if key == k:
                b[i] = (k, v); return
        b.append((k, v))
        self.size += 1
        if self.size > len(self.buckets) * 3 // 4:      # load factor above 0.75: double
            old = self.buckets
            self.buckets = [[] for _ in range(len(old) * 2)]
            self.size = 0
            for chain in old:
                for key, val in chain: self.put(key, val)

def pairs_with_sum(a, target):
    """Number of pairs (i < j) with a[i] + a[j] == target.
    count_freq builds the counts (piece u-freq), get_or_default reads one safely (piece u-hget)."""
    f = count_freq(a)
    pairs = 0
    for x in f:
        y = target - x
        if x < y:
            pairs += f[x] * get_or_default(f, y)        # each unordered pair once
        elif x == y:
            pairs += f[x] * (f[x] - 1) // 2             # pairs inside one value
    return pairs
--cpp
// a small chained hash table from int keys to int values (shows the mechanism)
struct IntMap {
    vector<vector<pair<int,int>>> buckets;
    size_t sz = 0;
    IntMap() : buckets(16) {}
    size_t bucket(int k) const { return (unsigned)(k * 2654435761u) % buckets.size(); }

    bool get(int k, int& out) const {
        for (auto& [key, val] : buckets[bucket(k)]) if (key == k) { out = val; return true; }
        return false;
    }

    void put(int k, int v) {
        auto& b = buckets[bucket(k)];
        for (auto& [key, val] : b) if (key == k) { val = v; return; }
        b.push_back({k, v});
        if (++sz > buckets.size() * 3 / 4) {          // load factor above 0.75: double
            vector<vector<pair<int,int>>> old(buckets.size() * 2);
            swap(old, buckets); sz = 0;
            for (auto& chain : old) for (auto& [key, val] : chain) put(key, val);
        }
    }
};

// everyday use: number of pairs (i < j) with a[i] + a[j] == target.
// countFreq builds the counts (piece u-freq), getOrDefault reads one safely (piece u-hget)
long long pairsWithSum(const vector<int>& a, int target) {
    map<int, int> f = countFreq(a);
    long long pairs = 0;
    for (auto& [x, c] : f) {
        int y = target - x;
        if (x < y) pairs += (long long)c * getOrDefault(f, y, 0);   // each unordered pair once
        else if (x == y) pairs += (long long)c * (c - 1) / 2;        // pairs inside one value
    }
    return pairs;
}
--uses
u-freq
u-hget

### hset
--idea
A set is a collection that remembers which values are in it and ignores repeats. It answers one question quickly: "is this value in the collection?". Think of a guest list at a door: you only care whether a name is on it, not how many times it was written.
A hash set is a hash table that stores only keys. Add, remove and "contains" each take about one step on average. A set also gives you free de-duplication: put everything in and the repeats vanish.
--build
1. Create an empty hash set `seen`.
2. `add(x)`: put `x` in. If it was already there, nothing changes. Many languages return whether it was new, which is handy.
3. `contains(x)`: is `x` in the set? This is the main operation.
4. `remove(x)`: take `x` out.
5. Typical loop: for each item `x`, check `contains(x)` before `add(x)`. The first time the check succeeds, you have found a duplicate or a cycle.
6. Set operations: union (everything in either), intersection (in both), difference (in the first but not the second). Build them by looping over the smaller set and asking the larger one.
--think
- Signal: "contains duplicate", "first repeated value", "longest run of consecutive numbers", "does the cycle ever repeat?", "common elements of two lists". Any question about membership, not counts or order.
- Decide first if you need only membership (set) or also a count (hash map), or if order matters (sorted set). Use a boolean array when values are small integers.
- Longest consecutive run: put all numbers in a set, and only start counting from numbers whose predecessor `x - 1` is not in the set. That makes it O(n), not O(n^2).
- Cycle detection in a number sequence (like repeatedly squaring digits): keep a set of visited states. If you reach one again, you looped.
- Pitfalls: sets of lists or arrays (in Java and C++ use a string or a pair as the key; in Python convert to a tuple), changing an item after adding it, and expecting an order from a hash set (there is none).
--cost
Average O(1) per add, remove, contains. Worst case O(n). Space O(n).
--java
// length of the longest run of consecutive integers (like 4, 5, 6, 7) in an unsorted array
static int longestConsecutive(int[] a) {
    java.util.HashSet<Integer> set = new java.util.HashSet<>();
    for (int x : a) set.add(x);
    int best = 0;
    for (int x : set) {
        if (set.contains(x - 1)) continue;           // not the start of a run
        int len = 1;
        while (set.contains(x + len)) len++;
        best = Math.max(best, len);
    }
    return best;
}

// first value that appears twice, or -1
static int firstRepeat(int[] a) {
    java.util.HashSet<Integer> seen = new java.util.HashSet<>();
    for (int x : a) if (!seen.add(x)) return x;       // add returns false if already present
    return -1;
}
--py
def longest_consecutive(a):
    """Length of the longest run of consecutive integers in an unsorted list."""
    s = set(a)
    best = 0
    for x in s:
        if x - 1 in s:
            continue                      # not the start of a run
        length = 1
        while x + length in s:
            length += 1
        best = max(best, length)
    return best

def first_repeat(a):
    seen = set()
    for x in a:
        if x in seen:
            return x
        seen.add(x)
    return -1
--cpp
// length of the longest run of consecutive integers in an unsorted vector
int longestConsecutive(const vector<int>& a) {
    unordered_set<int> s(a.begin(), a.end());
    int best = 0;
    for (int x : s) {
        if (s.count(x - 1)) continue;                 // not the start of a run
        int len = 1;
        while (s.count(x + len)) len++;
        best = max(best, len);
    }
    return best;
}

// first value that appears twice, or -1
int firstRepeat(const vector<int>& a) {
    unordered_set<int> seen;
    for (int x : a)
        if (!seen.insert(x).second) return x;         // insert reports whether it was new
    return -1;
}

### stack
--idea
A stack is a pile where you can only touch the top. You put a new item on top (`push`) and take the top item off (`pop`). The last item in is the first one out, like a stack of plates or the Back button of a browser. It remembers things in the reverse order you met them.
That reverse memory is what makes stacks useful. Anything with nesting (brackets inside brackets, a function calling a function, an undo history) needs to "finish the most recent thing first", and that is exactly what a stack does. Recursion is secretly a stack too: the computer's call stack.
--build
1. Keep an array `data` and an integer `top` that counts the items (the next free slot).
2. `push(x)`: write `data[top] = x`, then `top++`. If the array is full, double it.
3. `pop()`: check `top > 0`, then `top--` and return `data[top]`.
4. `peek()`: return `data[top - 1]` without removing it.
5. `isEmpty()`: `top == 0`.
6. Bracket matching: for each character, push an opening bracket. On a closing bracket, pop and check it is the matching opener. The text is balanced when you never pop an empty stack and the stack is empty at the end.
7. Postfix (reverse Polish) calculation: push numbers; on an operator, pop two, apply, push the result.
8. Layering: the bracket check and the postfix evaluator do not touch the array directly, they call the three stack moves `push`, `peek` and `pop` (code piece `u-stackop`).
--think
- Signal: nested structure, "most recent unmatched", "undo", "evaluate an expression", "simulate recursion without recursion", "reverse the order". Depth-first search is a stack.
- Decide first what you push (an index, a value, or a pair) and what event pops. Pushing indexes instead of values is usually better, because you can recover both the value and the position.
- Neighbouring techniques: use a queue when the first thing in should come first (breadth-first order). Use a monotonic stack when you need the "next greater element". Use recursion when the depth is small and the code is clearer.
- Pitfalls: popping an empty stack (always check), forgetting leftover items at the end, and Java's old `Stack` class (use `ArrayDeque` instead, it is faster and cleaner).
--cost
Push, pop and peek are each O(1) (amortised when the array grows). Space O(n).
--java
// array-backed stack of ints (the mechanism)
static class IntStack {
    int[] data = new int[4]; int top = 0;
    void push(int x) {
        if (top == data.length) data = java.util.Arrays.copyOf(data, top * 2);   // double when full
        data[top++] = x;
    }
    int pop()  { if (top == 0) throw new RuntimeException("empty"); return data[--top]; }
    int peek() { return data[top - 1]; }
    boolean isEmpty() { return top == 0; }
}

// are the brackets in s balanced? Uses the stack moves push, peek and pop (piece u-stackop)
static boolean balanced(String s) {
    java.util.Deque<Integer> st = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
    for (char c : s.toCharArray()) {
        if (c == '(' || c == '[' || c == '{') push(st, c);
        else if (c == ')' || c == ']' || c == '}') {
            if (st.isEmpty()) return false;
            int o = pop(st);
            if ((c == ')' && o != '(') || (c == ']' && o != '[') || (c == '}' && o != '{')) return false;
        }
    }
    return st.isEmpty();                       // leftover openers mean unbalanced
}

// value of a postfix expression such as "3 4 + 2 *" (tokens separated by spaces)
static int evalPostfix(String expr) {
    java.util.Deque<Integer> st = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
    for (String tok : expr.split(" ")) {
        if (tok.length() == 1 && "+-*".indexOf(tok.charAt(0)) >= 0) {
            int b = pop(st), a = pop(st);       // the right operand was pushed last
            push(st, tok.equals("+") ? a + b : tok.equals("-") ? a - b : a * b);
        } else push(st, Integer.parseInt(tok));
    }
    return peek(st);
}
--py
class IntStack:
    """The mechanism: a list used from one end."""
    def __init__(self):
        self.data = []

    def push(self, x):
        self.data.append(x)

    def pop(self):
        if not self.data: raise IndexError("empty")
        return self.data.pop()

    def peek(self):
        return self.data[-1]

    def is_empty(self):
        return not self.data

# the functions below call the stack moves push, peek and pop (piece u-stackop)
def balanced(s):
    """Are the brackets in s balanced?"""
    pair = {')': '(', ']': '[', '}': '{'}
    st = []
    for c in s:
        if c in '([{':
            push(st, c)
        elif c in pair:
            if not st or pop(st) != pair[c]:
                return False
    return not st                  # leftover openers mean unbalanced

def eval_postfix(expr):
    """Value of a postfix expression such as '3 4 + 2 *'."""
    st = []
    for tok in expr.split():
        if tok in ('+', '-', '*'):
            b, a = pop(st), pop(st)         # the right operand was pushed last
            push(st, a + b if tok == '+' else a - b if tok == '-' else a * b)
        else:
            push(st, int(tok))
    return peek(st)
--cpp
// array-backed stack of ints (the mechanism)
struct IntStack {
    vector<int> data;
    void push(int x) { data.push_back(x); }
    int pop() { int x = data.back(); data.pop_back(); return x; }   // caller checks non-empty
    int peek() const { return data.back(); }
    bool empty() const { return data.empty(); }
};

// are the brackets in s balanced? Uses the stack moves push, peek and pop (piece u-stackop)
bool balanced(const string& s) {
    stack<int> st;
    for (char c : s) {
        if (c == '(' || c == '[' || c == '{') push(st, c);
        else if (c == ')' || c == ']' || c == '}') {
            if (st.empty()) return false;
            int o = pop(st);
            if ((c == ')' && o != '(') || (c == ']' && o != '[') || (c == '}' && o != '{')) return false;
        }
    }
    return st.empty();                         // leftover openers mean unbalanced
}

// value of a postfix expression such as "3 4 + 2 *" (tokens separated by spaces)
int evalPostfix(const string& expr) {
    stack<int> st;
    string tok; stringstream ss(expr);
    while (ss >> tok) {
        if (tok == "+" || tok == "-" || tok == "*") {
            int b = pop(st), a = pop(st);       // the right operand was pushed last
            push(st, tok == "+" ? a + b : tok == "-" ? a - b : a * b);
        } else push(st, stoi(tok));
    }
    return peek(st);
}
--uses
u-stackop

### queue
--idea
A queue is a line at a shop: new people join at the back (`enqueue`) and the person at the front is served first (`dequeue`). First in, first out. It keeps things in the order they arrived, so it is the natural tool for "process in the order discovered".
The key use in algorithms is breadth-first search: you visit a start point, then everything one step away, then everything two steps away, because the queue serves older discoveries first. That is why it finds shortest paths when every step costs the same.
--build
1. Keep an array `data` of fixed size, and two counters `head` (index of the front) and `tail` (index where the next item goes). Start both at 0.
2. `enqueue(x)`: `data[tail] = x`, then `tail++`.
3. `dequeue()`: check `head < tail`, read `data[head]`, then `head++`. Do not shift anything, just move `head`.
4. `isEmpty()`: `head == tail`. `size()`: `tail - head`.
5. If you can bound the total number of pushes (for example each node enters once, so at most `n` pushes), this simple array is enough. If not, wrap the indexes around (see circular buffers).
6. Breadth-first search: put the start in, mark it seen. While the queue is not empty, take the front, and for each unseen neighbour mark it and enqueue it.
7. Layering: breadth-first search calls the two queue moves `enqueue` and `dequeue` (code piece `u-queueop`).
--think
- Signal: "minimum number of steps", "shortest path with equal costs", "level by level", "process in arrival order", "simulate a line". Also a sliding window of recent items.
- Decide first what each queue entry holds (a node, or a node plus its distance), and when to mark visited. Mark when you enqueue, not when you dequeue, or the same node enters many times.
- Neighbouring techniques: if steps have different costs use Dijkstra (a priority queue, not a plain queue). If steps cost 0 or 1, use a deque. If you need the smallest item, use a heap.
- Level by level: record the queue's size at the start of a round and process exactly that many items; that cleanly separates distance 1, 2, 3.
- Pitfalls: marking visited too late, dequeuing from an empty queue, and in Java using `LinkedList` or the old `Queue` methods that throw versus return null (`poll` returns null, `remove` throws).
--cost
Enqueue and dequeue are O(1). Space O(n).
--java
// array queue when the total number of pushes is at most cap (the mechanism)
static class IntQueue {
    int[] data; int head = 0, tail = 0;
    IntQueue(int cap) { data = new int[cap]; }
    void enqueue(int x) { data[tail++] = x; }
    int dequeue() { return data[head++]; }
    boolean isEmpty() { return head == tail; }
    int size() { return tail - head; }
}

// breadth-first distances from src (-1 = unreachable); uses enqueue and dequeue (piece u-queueop)
static int[] bfs(java.util.List<Integer>[] adj, int src) {
    int[] dist = new int[adj.length];
    java.util.Arrays.fill(dist, -1);
    java.util.Deque<Integer> q = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
    dist[src] = 0; enqueue(q, src);
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {
        int u = dequeue(q);
        for (int v : adj[u])
            if (dist[v] < 0) { dist[v] = dist[u] + 1; enqueue(q, v); }   // mark on enqueue
    }
    return dist;
}
--py
from collections import deque

class IntQueue:
    """The mechanism: a deque used from both ends, one end for in, the other for out."""
    def __init__(self):
        self.q = deque()

    def enqueue(self, x):
        self.q.append(x)

    def dequeue(self):
        return self.q.popleft()      # O(1); list.pop(0) would be O(n)

    def is_empty(self):
        return not self.q

# uses enqueue and dequeue (piece u-queueop)
def bfs(adj, src):
    """Distances from src (-1 = unreachable). adj is a list of neighbour lists."""
    dist = [-1] * len(adj)
    dist[src] = 0
    q = deque()
    enqueue(q, src)
    while q:
        u = dequeue(q)
        for v in adj[u]:
            if dist[v] < 0:
                dist[v] = dist[u] + 1    # mark on enqueue
                enqueue(q, v)
    return dist
--cpp
// array queue when the total number of pushes is at most cap (the mechanism)
struct IntQueue {
    vector<int> data; int head = 0, tail = 0;
    IntQueue(int cap) : data(cap) {}
    void enqueue(int x) { data[tail++] = x; }
    int dequeue() { return data[head++]; }
    bool empty() const { return head == tail; }
    int size() const { return tail - head; }
};

// breadth-first distances from src (-1 = unreachable); uses enqueue and dequeue (piece u-queueop)
vector<int> bfs(const vector<vector<int>>& adj, int src) {
    vector<int> dist(adj.size(), -1);
    queue<int> q;
    dist[src] = 0; enqueue(q, src);
    while (!q.empty()) {
        int u = dequeue(q);
        for (int v : adj[u])
            if (dist[v] < 0) { dist[v] = dist[u] + 1; enqueue(q, v); }   // mark on enqueue
    }
    return dist;
}
--uses
u-queueop

### deque
--idea
A deque (say "deck", short for double-ended queue) is a line where you can add or remove at both ends. It does the job of a stack and a queue at once: push and pop at the front, push and pop at the back, all in constant time. Think of a train where carriages can be attached or removed at either end.
Its best trick in algorithms is the sliding window maximum. Keep a deque of positions whose values are in decreasing order. The front always holds the largest value in the window, and old or useless items fall off the ends. It also powers 0-1 breadth-first search, where some steps cost 0 and some cost 1.
--build
1. Store items in a circular array of capacity `cap`, with `head` (index of the front item) and `size`.
2. `pushBack(x)`: write at `(head + size) % cap`, then `size++`.
3. `pushFront(x)`: move `head` back one step with `head = (head - 1 + cap) % cap`, write there, `size++`.
4. `popFront()`: read `data[head]`, `head = (head + 1) % cap`, `size--`. `popBack()`: read `data[(head + size - 1) % cap]`, `size--`.
5. If `size == cap` on a push, allocate double the space and copy the items in order starting from `head`.
6. Sliding window maximum of width `k`: for each index `i`, pop from the back while the back value is `<= a[i]` (they can never be the maximum again), push `i` at the back, pop from the front if its index is `<= i - k`, and the front is the answer for the window ending at `i`.
7. 0-1 BFS: when an edge costs 0, push the neighbour at the front; when it costs 1, push at the back.
--think
- Signal: "maximum or minimum of every window of size k", "shortest path with edge costs 0 or 1", "add or remove from both ends", "palindrome check by comparing ends".
- Decide first what the deque stores (positions, not values) and what order you keep it in. For window problems the order is monotone (always decreasing for a max), which is called a monotonic deque.
- Neighbouring techniques: a heap also gives the window maximum but costs O(log n) per step, the deque is O(1) amortised. A sparse table answers it for fixed windows if you can prepare first.
- Why it is fast: every index is pushed once and popped at most once, so the total work is O(n) even though there is a loop inside the loop.
- Pitfalls: storing values and then being unable to tell when an item left the window, using `<` instead of `<=` (it still works but keeps equal items), and in Java using `LinkedList` where `ArrayDeque` is faster.
--cost
Every end operation O(1) (amortised when it grows). Sliding window maximum O(n) total. Space O(n).
--java
// max of every window of width k
static int[] slidingMax(int[] a, int k) {
    int n = a.length;
    int[] res = new int[n - k + 1];
    java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> dq = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();   // holds indexes, values decreasing
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        while (!dq.isEmpty() && a[dq.peekLast()] <= a[i]) dq.pollLast();   // smaller items are useless now
        dq.addLast(i);
        if (dq.peekFirst() <= i - k) dq.pollFirst();                       // left the window
        if (i >= k - 1) res[i - k + 1] = a[dq.peekFirst()];
    }
    return res;
}

// shortest distances when each edge costs 0 or 1; adj[u] has {v, w} pairs
static int[] bfs01(java.util.List<int[]>[] adj, int src) {
    int[] dist = new int[adj.length];
    java.util.Arrays.fill(dist, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
    java.util.ArrayDeque<Integer> dq = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
    dist[src] = 0; dq.addFirst(src);
    while (!dq.isEmpty()) {
        int u = dq.pollFirst();
        for (int[] e : adj[u])
            if (dist[u] + e[1] < dist[e[0]]) {
                dist[e[0]] = dist[u] + e[1];
                if (e[1] == 0) dq.addFirst(e[0]); else dq.addLast(e[0]);   // free edge goes to the front
            }
    }
    return dist;
}
--py
from collections import deque

def sliding_max(a, k):
    """Max of every window of width k."""
    dq, res = deque(), []              # dq holds indexes, values decreasing
    for i, x in enumerate(a):
        while dq and a[dq[-1]] <= x:
            dq.pop()                   # smaller items are useless now
        dq.append(i)
        if dq[0] <= i - k:
            dq.popleft()               # left the window
        if i >= k - 1:
            res.append(a[dq[0]])
    return res

def bfs01(adj, src):
    """Shortest distances when every edge costs 0 or 1. adj[u] = [(v, w), ...]."""
    INF = float('inf')
    dist = [INF] * len(adj)
    dist[src] = 0
    dq = deque([src])
    while dq:
        u = dq.popleft()
        for v, w in adj[u]:
            if dist[u] + w < dist[v]:
                dist[v] = dist[u] + w
                if w == 0: dq.appendleft(v)    # free edge goes to the front
                else: dq.append(v)
    return dist
--cpp
// max of every window of width k
vector<int> slidingMax(const vector<int>& a, int k) {
    int n = a.size();
    vector<int> res;
    deque<int> dq;                                   // holds indexes, values decreasing
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        while (!dq.empty() && a[dq.back()] <= a[i]) dq.pop_back();   // smaller items are useless now
        dq.push_back(i);
        if (dq.front() <= i - k) dq.pop_front();                      // left the window
        if (i >= k - 1) res.push_back(a[dq.front()]);
    }
    return res;
}

// shortest distances when each edge costs 0 or 1; adj[u] has {v, w} pairs
vector<int> bfs01(const vector<vector<pair<int,int>>>& adj, int src) {
    vector<int> dist(adj.size(), INT_MAX);
    deque<int> dq;
    dist[src] = 0; dq.push_front(src);
    while (!dq.empty()) {
        int u = dq.front(); dq.pop_front();
        for (auto [v, w] : adj[u])
            if (dist[u] + w < dist[v]) {
                dist[v] = dist[u] + w;
                if (w == 0) dq.push_front(v); else dq.push_back(v);   // free edge goes to the front
            }
    }
    return dist;
}

### linked
--idea
A linked list is a chain of small boxes called nodes. Each node holds a value and an arrow (a pointer, or reference) to the next node. You keep hold of the first node, the head, and follow arrows to reach the rest. It is a treasure hunt where each clue tells you where the next one is.
Unlike an array, nodes can sit anywhere in memory, so inserting or removing needs only a change of arrows, no shifting. The price is that you cannot jump to item number `i`; you must walk there. Most linked-list problems are really pointer puzzles: reverse the arrows, find the middle, find a loop.
--build
1. Define a node with two fields: `val` and `next` (null for the last node).
2. Keep `head`, the first node. An empty list has `head = null`.
3. Insert at the front: make a node, set `node.next = head`, then `head = node`. This is O(1).
4. Insert after a node `p`: `node.next = p.next`, then `p.next = node`. Order matters; reversing these two lines loses the rest of the list.
5. Delete the node after `p`: `p.next = p.next.next`.
6. Use a dummy node placed before the head, so that inserting or deleting at the front is no longer a special case. Return `dummy.next` at the end.
7. Reverse in place: keep `prev = null` and `cur = head`. Repeat: save `nxt = cur.next`, set `cur.next = prev`, move `prev = cur`, `cur = nxt`. At the end `prev` is the new head.
8. Middle and loops: move `slow` one step and `fast` two steps at once. When `fast` reaches the end, `slow` is in the middle. If a loop exists they meet.
9. Layering: list merge sort calls `mergeLists` (code piece `u-listmerge`) to join the sorted halves, and the palindrome check calls `reverseList` (code piece `u-listrev`) on the back half.
--think
- Signal: the statement gives you nodes with `next` pointers, or asks you to splice, reverse, merge, remove the n-th from the end, or detect a cycle, often "in O(1) extra space".
- Decide first which pointers you must save before overwriting an arrow. Draw three boxes and the arrows on paper, and write the assignments in the order that never loses a node.
- Use a dummy head almost always. It removes the "what if the first node changes" case.
- Neighbouring techniques: if you need random access, use an array. If you need quick removal of a node you already hold, use a doubly linked list. Fast and slow pointers (Floyd's method) find a cycle, its start, and the middle with no extra memory.
- Pitfalls: dereferencing null (`p.next.next` when `p.next` is null), forgetting to set the last node's `next` to null after splitting, losing the head, and an infinite loop when a cycle exists and you walk until null.
--cost
Insert or delete at a node you hold is O(1). Finding the i-th node is O(i). Space is one pointer per item.
--java
static class ListNode {
    int val; ListNode next;
    ListNode(int v) { val = v; }
}

static ListNode middle(ListNode head) {          // second middle if the length is even
    ListNode slow = head, fast = head;
    while (fast != null && fast.next != null) { slow = slow.next; fast = fast.next.next; }
    return slow;
}

static boolean hasCycle(ListNode head) {
    ListNode slow = head, fast = head;
    while (fast != null && fast.next != null) {
        slow = slow.next; fast = fast.next.next;
        if (slow == fast) return true;           // the fast runner lapped the slow one
    }
    return false;
}

// merge sort on a list: cut in half, sort both, join with mergeLists (piece u-listmerge)
static ListNode sortList(ListNode head) {
    if (head == null || head.next == null) return head;
    ListNode slow = head, fast = head.next;      // slow stops at the end of the first half
    while (fast != null && fast.next != null) { slow = slow.next; fast = fast.next.next; }
    ListNode second = slow.next;
    slow.next = null;                            // cut the list in two
    return mergeLists(sortList(head), sortList(second));
}

// palindrome test with O(1) extra space: reverse the second half with reverseList (piece u-listrev)
static boolean isPalindrome(ListNode head) {
    if (head == null || head.next == null) return true;
    ListNode slow = head, fast = head;
    while (fast.next != null && fast.next.next != null) { slow = slow.next; fast = fast.next.next; }
    ListNode second = reverseList(slow.next);    // flip the back half
    boolean ok = true;
    for (ListNode p = head, q = second; q != null; p = p.next, q = q.next)
        if (p.val != q.val) ok = false;
    slow.next = reverseList(second);             // put the list back as it was
    return ok;
}
--py
class ListNode:
    def __init__(self, val, nxt=None):
        self.val, self.next = val, nxt

def middle(head):             # second middle if the length is even
    slow = fast = head
    while fast and fast.next:
        slow, fast = slow.next, fast.next.next
    return slow

def has_cycle(head):
    slow = fast = head
    while fast and fast.next:
        slow, fast = slow.next, fast.next.next
        if slow is fast:
            return True       # the fast runner lapped the slow one
    return False

def sort_list(head):
    """Merge sort on a list: cut in half, sort both, join with merge_lists (piece u-listmerge)."""
    if head is None or head.next is None:
        return head
    slow, fast = head, head.next          # slow stops at the end of the first half
    while fast and fast.next:
        slow, fast = slow.next, fast.next.next
    second, slow.next = slow.next, None   # cut the list in two
    return merge_lists(sort_list(head), sort_list(second))

def is_palindrome(head):
    """O(1) extra space: reverse the second half with reverse_list (piece u-listrev)."""
    if head is None or head.next is None:
        return True
    slow = fast = head
    while fast.next and fast.next.next:
        slow, fast = slow.next, fast.next.next
    second = reverse_list(slow.next)      # flip the back half
    ok, p, q = True, head, second
    while q:
        if p.val != q.val: ok = False
        p, q = p.next, q.next
    slow.next = reverse_list(second)      # put the list back as it was
    return ok
--cpp
struct ListNode {
    int val; ListNode* next;
    ListNode(int v, ListNode* n = nullptr) : val(v), next(n) {}
};

ListNode* middle(ListNode* head) {         // second middle if the length is even
    ListNode *slow = head, *fast = head;
    while (fast && fast->next) { slow = slow->next; fast = fast->next->next; }
    return slow;
}

bool hasCycle(ListNode* head) {
    ListNode *slow = head, *fast = head;
    while (fast && fast->next) {
        slow = slow->next; fast = fast->next->next;
        if (slow == fast) return true;     // the fast runner lapped the slow one
    }
    return false;
}

// merge sort on a list: cut in half, sort both, join with mergeLists (piece u-listmerge)
ListNode* sortList(ListNode* head) {
    if (!head || !head->next) return head;
    ListNode *slow = head, *fast = head->next;     // slow stops at the end of the first half
    while (fast && fast->next) { slow = slow->next; fast = fast->next->next; }
    ListNode* second = slow->next;
    slow->next = nullptr;                          // cut the list in two
    return mergeLists(sortList(head), sortList(second));
}

// palindrome test with O(1) extra space: reverse the second half with reverseList (piece u-listrev)
bool isPalindrome(ListNode* head) {
    if (!head || !head->next) return true;
    ListNode *slow = head, *fast = head;
    while (fast->next && fast->next->next) { slow = slow->next; fast = fast->next->next; }
    ListNode* second = reverseList(slow->next);    // flip the back half
    bool ok = true;
    for (ListNode *p = head, *q = second; q; p = p->next, q = q->next)
        if (p->val != q->val) ok = false;
    slow->next = reverseList(second);              // put the list back as it was
    return ok;
}
--uses
u-listrev
u-listmerge

### dll
--idea
A doubly linked list is a linked list where every node points both forward (`next`) and backward (`prev`). With both arrows, a node can remove itself without knowing where the list started: it tells its left neighbour "skip me" and its right neighbour "skip me", and it is gone in a constant number of steps.
That one power is why it appears inside caches and schedulers. If a hash table gives you the node in O(1), the doubly linked list lets you unlink it and move it to the front in O(1). Together they make an LRU cache.
--build
1. Define a node with `val`, `prev`, `next`.
2. Make two permanent sentinel nodes, `head` and `tail`, that hold no data. Link `head.next = tail` and `tail.prev = head`. The real items always live between them, so you never test for null.
3. `insertAfter(p, x)`: create `n`. Set `n.prev = p`, `n.next = p.next`, then `p.next.prev = n`, then `p.next = n`.
4. `remove(n)`: set `n.prev.next = n.next` and `n.next.prev = n.prev`.
5. `pushFront(x)` is `insertAfter(head, x)`. `pushBack(x)` is `insertAfter(tail.prev, x)`.
6. `moveToFront(n)`: `remove(n)` then insert it after `head`.
7. Walking backward is symmetrical: start at `tail.prev` and follow `prev`.
--think
- Signal: "remove a node you already have a handle to in O(1)", "move to front", "undo and redo history", "browser tabs", "a list you edit in the middle often".
- Decide first what keeps the handles. Usually a hash map from key to node. The map finds the node, the list reorders it.
- Neighbouring techniques: an array is better for plain index access. A linked list with `next` only is enough if you only ever append and walk forward. A deque (circular array) is better for pushes at both ends and has better memory speed.
- Sentinels make every case the same: first node, last node, and empty list all use the same four assignments.
- Pitfalls: updating the four pointers in the wrong order, forgetting the back arrow when removing, and leaving a removed node still pointing into the list (harmless to logic, but a leak in some languages).
--cost
Insert, remove and move are O(1) given the node. Finding a node by position is O(n). Space is two pointers per item.
--java
static class Node {
    int key, val; Node prev, next;
    Node(int k, int v) { key = k; val = v; }
}

static class DList {
    Node head = new Node(0, 0), tail = new Node(0, 0);   // sentinels hold no data
    DList() { head.next = tail; tail.prev = head; }

    void insertAfter(Node p, Node n) {
        n.prev = p; n.next = p.next;
        p.next.prev = n; p.next = n;
    }
    void remove(Node n) {
        n.prev.next = n.next; n.next.prev = n.prev;
    }
    void pushFront(Node n) { insertAfter(head, n); }
    void moveToFront(Node n) { remove(n); pushFront(n); }
    Node popBack() {                                     // null when empty
        Node n = tail.prev;
        if (n == head) return null;
        remove(n); return n;
    }
}
--py
class Node:
    def __init__(self, key=0, val=0):
        self.key, self.val = key, val
        self.prev = self.next = None

class DList:
    def __init__(self):
        self.head, self.tail = Node(), Node()     # sentinels hold no data
        self.head.next, self.tail.prev = self.tail, self.head

    def insert_after(self, p, n):
        n.prev, n.next = p, p.next
        p.next.prev = n
        p.next = n

    def remove(self, n):
        n.prev.next = n.next
        n.next.prev = n.prev

    def push_front(self, n):
        self.insert_after(self.head, n)

    def move_to_front(self, n):
        self.remove(n)
        self.push_front(n)

    def pop_back(self):                           # None when empty
        n = self.tail.prev
        if n is self.head: return None
        self.remove(n)
        return n
--cpp
struct Node {
    int key = 0, val = 0;
    Node *prev = nullptr, *next = nullptr;
    Node(int k = 0, int v = 0) : key(k), val(v) {}
};

struct DList {
    Node head, tail;                                  // sentinels hold no data
    DList() { head.next = &tail; tail.prev = &head; }

    void insertAfter(Node* p, Node* n) {
        n->prev = p; n->next = p->next;
        p->next->prev = n; p->next = n;
    }
    void remove(Node* n) {
        n->prev->next = n->next; n->next->prev = n->prev;
    }
    void pushFront(Node* n) { insertAfter(&head, n); }
    void moveToFront(Node* n) { remove(n); pushFront(n); }
    Node* popBack() {                                 // nullptr when empty
        Node* n = tail.prev;
        if (n == &head) return nullptr;
        remove(n); return n;
    }
};

### circbuf
--idea
A circular buffer (or ring buffer) is a queue stored in a fixed-size array whose two ends wrap around. When the write position reaches the end of the array it jumps back to position 0, reusing space that has been freed at the front. Picture a clock face: the hands keep going round and never run off the edge.
It is perfect when you only care about the most recent `k` things: the last 100 log lines, the last 5 temperatures, an audio buffer. It never allocates new memory after it is created, and when full it can either refuse new items or overwrite the oldest one.
--build
1. Allocate `data` of capacity `cap`. Keep `head` (index of the oldest item) and `size` (how many items are stored).
2. The tail position is derived, not stored: `tail = (head + size) % cap` is where the next item goes.
3. `push(x)`: if `size == cap`, the buffer is full. Either return false (reject) or overwrite the oldest by doing `head = (head + 1) % cap` and `size--` first. Then write `data[tail] = x` and `size++`.
4. `pop()`: if `size == 0` fail. Otherwise read `data[head]`, set `head = (head + 1) % cap`, `size--`.
5. `get(i)`: the i-th oldest item is `data[(head + i) % cap]`, for `0 <= i < size`.
6. Keeping `size` separately avoids the classic confusion where "head == tail" could mean either empty or full.
--think
- Signal: "keep only the last k items", "fixed memory", "producer and consumer", "moving average of the last k values", "design a circular queue".
- Decide first what happens when it is full: reject or overwrite. Overwrite suits logs and moving averages; reject suits work queues.
- Neighbouring techniques: a growable deque never loses data but allocates. A sliding window with a running sum plus a ring buffer gives a moving average in O(1) per value.
- Use the modulo for every index move. A power-of-two capacity lets you replace `% cap` with `& (cap - 1)`.
- Pitfalls: the empty versus full ambiguity (fixed by storing `size`), negative modulo when stepping backward (add `cap` first), and reading `get(i)` for `i >= size`.
--cost
Push, pop and get are O(1). Space is fixed at O(cap).
--java
static class Ring {
    int[] data; int head = 0, size = 0;
    Ring(int cap) { data = new int[cap]; }

    // returns false if full and overwrite is false
    boolean push(int x, boolean overwrite) {
        if (size == data.length) {
            if (!overwrite) return false;
            head = (head + 1) % data.length; size--;       // drop the oldest
        }
        data[(head + size) % data.length] = x;
        size++;
        return true;
    }
    int pop() {                                             // caller checks size > 0
        int x = data[head];
        head = (head + 1) % data.length; size--;
        return x;
    }
    int get(int i) { return data[(head + i) % data.length]; }   // i-th oldest
}
--py
class Ring:
    def __init__(self, cap):
        self.data = [0] * cap
        self.head = 0
        self.size = 0

    def push(self, x, overwrite=False):
        """Returns False if the buffer is full and overwrite is off."""
        cap = len(self.data)
        if self.size == cap:
            if not overwrite: return False
            self.head = (self.head + 1) % cap          # drop the oldest
            self.size -= 1
        self.data[(self.head + self.size) % cap] = x
        self.size += 1
        return True

    def pop(self):                                      # caller checks size > 0
        x = self.data[self.head]
        self.head = (self.head + 1) % len(self.data)
        self.size -= 1
        return x

    def get(self, i):                                   # i-th oldest
        return self.data[(self.head + i) % len(self.data)]
--cpp
struct Ring {
    vector<int> data; int head = 0, size = 0;
    Ring(int cap) : data(cap) {}

    // returns false if full and overwrite is false
    bool push(int x, bool overwrite = false) {
        int cap = data.size();
        if (size == cap) {
            if (!overwrite) return false;
            head = (head + 1) % cap; size--;               // drop the oldest
        }
        data[(head + size) % cap] = x;
        size++;
        return true;
    }
    int pop() {                                            // caller checks size > 0
        int x = data[head];
        head = (head + 1) % data.size(); size--;
        return x;
    }
    int get(int i) const { return data[(head + i) % data.size()]; }   // i-th oldest
};

### bitset
--idea
A bitset stores many yes or no flags by packing them into the bits of ordinary integers. One 64-bit integer holds 64 flags, so a million flags take only about 16,000 integers (128 KB), and an operation like "combine two sets of flags" handles 64 flags in a single CPU instruction.
Think of a long row of light switches grouped in sets of 64. To reach switch `i`, find the group `i / 64` and the position `i % 64` inside it. Because whole groups are processed at once, bitsets make some algorithms 64 times faster, such as reachability tables, sieve of Eratosthenes, and subset-sum dynamic programming.
--build
1. Choose the number of flags `n`. Make an array `words` of `(n + 63) / 64` unsigned 64-bit integers, all zero.
2. `set(i)`: `words[i >> 6] |= 1L << (i & 63)`. Here `i >> 6` means divide by 64 and `i & 63` means remainder by 64.
3. `clear(i)`: `words[i >> 6] &= ~(1L << (i & 63))`.
4. `get(i)`: `(words[i >> 6] >> (i & 63)) & 1`.
5. `count()`: add up the popcount (number of 1 bits) of every word.
6. Whole-set operations go word by word: `and`, `or`, `xor` of two bitsets is a loop over the words.
7. Shift left by `s` bits (used in subset-sum): move whole words by `s / 64`, then shift the bits by `s % 64`, carrying the bits that spill into the next word.
--think
- Signal: "n up to 10^5 or 10^6 and an O(n^2) or O(n * m) check", "set of reachable sums", "which of these 5000 items contain this feature", "sieve up to 10^8". If the inner loop is only "or this row into that row", a bitset divides the time by 64.
- Decide first what index means a bit. For subset sum, bit `s` means "sum `s` is reachable", and adding an item `w` is `bits |= bits << w`.
- Neighbouring techniques: a boolean array is simpler and fine for small `n`. `vector<bool>` in C++ is already packed but slower to use for bulk operations. A hash set is for sparse values, a bitset is for dense small-range values.
- Transitive closure of a graph with 5000 nodes in `n^3 / 64` operations is a standard bitset use.
- Pitfalls: writing `1 << i` with an `int` when `i >= 32` (use `1L` or `1ULL`), forgetting that `>>` on signed values in Java keeps the sign (use `>>>`), and off-by-one in the word count.
--cost
Set, clear, get are O(1). Whole-set operations are O(n / 64). Space n / 8 bytes.
--java
static class Bits {
    long[] w; int n;
    Bits(int n) { this.n = n; w = new long[(n + 63) >> 6]; }
    void set(int i)   { w[i >> 6] |= 1L << (i & 63); }
    void clear(int i) { w[i >> 6] &= ~(1L << (i & 63)); }
    boolean get(int i) { return ((w[i >> 6] >>> (i & 63)) & 1L) != 0; }
    int count() { int c = 0; for (long x : w) c += Long.bitCount(x); return c; }
    void or(Bits o) { for (int i = 0; i < w.length; i++) w[i] |= o.w[i]; }

    // this |= this << s   (the subset-sum step); bits shifted past n are dropped
    void orShiftLeft(int s) {
        int ws = s >> 6, bs = s & 63;
        for (int i = w.length - 1; i >= ws; i--) {          // go from the top so sources are still old
            long v = w[i - ws] << bs;
            if (bs > 0 && i - ws - 1 >= 0) v |= w[i - ws - 1] >>> (64 - bs);   // carry from the lower word
            w[i] |= v;
        }
        int extra = w.length * 64 - n;                      // clear unused top bits
        if (extra > 0) w[w.length - 1] &= -1L >>> extra;
    }
}
--py
class Bits:
    """Python ints are arbitrary-size, so one int can be the whole bitset."""
    def __init__(self, n):
        self.n = n
        self.v = 0

    def set(self, i):   self.v |= 1 << i
    def clear(self, i): self.v &= ~(1 << i)
    def get(self, i):   return (self.v >> i) & 1 == 1
    def count(self):    return bin(self.v).count("1")
    def or_(self, o):   self.v |= o.v

    def or_shift_left(self, s):
        """self |= self << s, dropping bits at or beyond n."""
        self.v |= self.v << s
        self.v &= (1 << self.n) - 1

def subset_sums(items, limit):
    """Which sums up to limit can be made from the items (each used at most once)."""
    b = Bits(limit + 1)
    b.set(0)
    for w in items:
        b.or_shift_left(w)
    return [i for i in range(limit + 1) if b.get(i)]
--cpp
// subset sums with std::bitset (size fixed at compile time)
const int N = 1001;
vector<int> subsetSums(const vector<int>& items) {
    bitset<N> b;
    b[0] = 1;
    for (int w : items) b |= b << w;        // each item either skipped or added
    vector<int> res;
    for (int i = 0; i < N; i++) if (b[i]) res.push_back(i);
    return res;
}

// the same mechanism by hand, to see what bitset does inside
struct Bits {
    vector<unsigned long long> w; int n;
    Bits(int n) : w((n + 63) / 64), n(n) {}
    void set(int i)   { w[i >> 6] |= 1ULL << (i & 63); }
    void clear(int i) { w[i >> 6] &= ~(1ULL << (i & 63)); }
    bool get(int i) const { return (w[i >> 6] >> (i & 63)) & 1; }
    int count() const { int c = 0; for (auto x : w) c += __builtin_popcountll(x); return c; }
};

### heap
--idea
A heap (priority queue) is a collection that always hands you the smallest item (a min-heap) or the largest (a max-heap) quickly, while still letting you add new items quickly. Think of an emergency room: new patients arrive at any time, and the most urgent one is always seen next.
It is stored as a tree packed into a plain array. Every parent is smaller than or equal to its two children, so the smallest item is always at position 0. Adding or removing only has to repair one path from a leaf to the top, which is about log n steps because the tree is full and short.
--build
1. Store items in an array `h` with `size`. Index `i` has parent `(i - 1) / 2` and children `2i + 1` and `2i + 2`. No pointers are needed.
2. Invariant: for every `i > 0`, `h[(i - 1) / 2] <= h[i]` (min-heap).
3. `push(x)`: put `x` at index `size`, `size++`, then `siftUp`: while the item is smaller than its parent, swap them and move up.
4. `peek()`: the answer is `h[0]`.
5. `pop()`: save `h[0]`. Move the last item to index 0, `size--`, then `siftDown`: while some child is smaller, swap with the smaller child and move down.
6. Build a heap from `n` items in O(n): start at the last parent `n / 2 - 1` and `siftDown` every index back to 0. This is faster than `n` pushes.
7. For a max-heap, flip the comparison (or store negated numbers).
8. Layering: `push` calls `up` and `pop` calls `down` (code piece `u-sift`), and `pop` exchanges two slots with `swap` (code piece `u-swap`).
--think
- Signal: "repeatedly take the smallest or largest", "k-th largest", "merge k sorted lists", "schedule by earliest deadline", "next event in a simulation", Dijkstra, Huffman coding.
- Decide first which item you need to be at the top, and what you store (a number, or a pair of priority and id). In pairs, the first field is what is compared.
- Neighbouring techniques: a sorted array is better if nothing is added after building. A balanced tree can also remove arbitrary items and find the next larger one, but a heap is simpler and faster for just "min". For top `k` of a stream, keep a heap of size `k`.
- A heap cannot search or delete an arbitrary item quickly. If you need that, use "lazy deletion": leave stale entries in, and skip them when they surface.
- Pitfalls: Python's `heapq` is only a min-heap (push negatives for max), C++ `priority_queue` is a max-heap by default, a comparator that is not consistent, and forgetting that the top is only the min, the rest is not sorted.
--cost
Push and pop O(log n). Peek O(1). Build from an array O(n). Space O(n).
--java
// min-heap of ints, array based. up and down come from the piece u-sift, swap from u-swap
static class MinHeap {
    int[] h = new int[16]; int size = 0;

    void push(int x) {
        if (size == h.length) h = java.util.Arrays.copyOf(h, size * 2);
        h[size++] = x;
        up(size - 1);                    // float the new item up
    }

    int peek() { return h[0]; }

    int pop() {
        int top = h[0];
        swap(h, 0, --size);              // last item goes to the top
        down(0, size);                   // sink it to its place
        return top;
    }
    boolean isEmpty() { return size == 0; }
}
--py
class MinHeap:
    """Min-heap on a list. up and down come from the piece u-sift, swap from u-swap."""
    def __init__(self):
        self.h = []

    def push(self, x):
        self.h.append(x)
        up(self.h, len(self.h) - 1)      # float the new item up

    def peek(self):
        return self.h[0]

    def pop(self):
        h = self.h
        swap(h, 0, len(h) - 1)           # last item goes to the top
        top = h.pop()
        down(h, 0, len(h))               # sink it to its place
        return top

    def __len__(self):
        return len(self.h)
# in real code: import heapq; heapq.heappush(h, x); heapq.heappop(h)
--cpp
// min-heap of ints, array based. up and down come from the piece u-sift, swapAt from u-swap
struct MinHeap {
    vector<int> h;

    void push(int x) {
        h.push_back(x);
        up(h.size() - 1);                // float the new item up
    }

    int peek() const { return h[0]; }

    int pop() {
        int top = h[0];
        swapAt(h, 0, h.size() - 1);      // last item goes to the top
        h.pop_back();
        down(0, h.size());               // sink it to its place
        return top;
    }
    bool empty() const { return h.empty(); }
};
// in real code: priority_queue<int, vector<int>, greater<int>> pq;
--uses
u-sift
u-swap

### tree
--idea
A binary tree is a family tree where every node has at most two children, called left and right. The first node is the root, nodes with no children are leaves, and the number of steps from the root to a node is its depth. A tree is a recursive shape: the left child is the root of a smaller tree, and so is the right child.
That recursion is the whole trick. To solve something for a tree, solve it for the left subtree and the right subtree, and combine the two answers at the root. Height, size, sum, and "is it symmetric" are all this one pattern with a different combining line.
--build
1. A node has `val`, `left`, `right`. A missing child is null. The tree is just the root node (null means the empty tree).
2. The base case of every recursive function: if the node is null, return the answer for an empty tree (0 for a count, -1 or 0 for a height).
3. Height: `1 + max(height(left), height(right))`. Size: `1 + size(left) + size(right)`.
4. Preorder (visit the node, then left, then right), inorder (left, node, right) and postorder (left, right, node) differ only in when you act on the node. Inorder on a search tree gives sorted order.
5. Level order (breadth-first): put the root in a queue. Take a node, record it, add its non-null children. Process one whole level per round by counting the queue size first.
6. To avoid deep recursion on a skinny tree, do the walk with your own stack.
7. Build a tree from preorder plus inorder lists: the first preorder item is the root; its position in inorder splits the left and right parts; recurse on the matching slices.
8. Layering: the balance check calls `height` (code piece `u-treeh`) at every node.
--think
- Signal: nodes with two children, "depth", "diameter", "lowest common ancestor", "path sum", "serialise a tree", "is it balanced". Any hierarchy.
- Decide first what each call should return, and whether the answer needs extra information from below (for a diameter, return the height while updating a global best with the path through the node).
- Neighbouring techniques: general trees with many children are handled the same way with a loop over children. A tree given as an edge list needs an adjacency list first (see graph representation). Dynamic programming on trees is this pattern plus state.
- Choose recursion for clarity, an explicit stack when the depth can reach 10^5, and a queue when the problem is about levels or the nearest leaf.
- Pitfalls: not handling the null node, confusing height measured in nodes versus edges, comparing only the children to the parent (for a search tree check the whole allowed range), and stack overflow on a degenerate (linked-list shaped) tree.
--cost
Visiting every node is O(n). Recursion uses O(height) stack space, between O(log n) and O(n).
--java
static class Node {
    int val; Node left, right;
    Node(int v) { val = v; }
}

// height(v) comes from the piece u-treeh; everything below is built on it
int size(Node t) { return t == null ? 0 : 1 + size(t.left) + size(t.right); }

// is every node's left and right height within 1 of each other?
boolean isBalanced(Node t) {
    if (t == null) return true;
    return Math.abs(height(t.left) - height(t.right)) <= 1 && isBalanced(t.left) && isBalanced(t.right);
}

void inorder(Node t, java.util.List<Integer> out) {
    if (t == null) return;
    inorder(t.left, out); out.add(t.val); inorder(t.right, out);
}

// values level by level, one list per level
java.util.List<java.util.List<Integer>> levels(Node root) {
    java.util.List<java.util.List<Integer>> res = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
    if (root == null) return res;
    java.util.ArrayDeque<Node> q = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();
    q.add(root);
    while (!q.isEmpty()) {
        int cnt = q.size();                       // exactly one level
        java.util.List<Integer> lv = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
        while (cnt-- > 0) {
            Node n = q.poll(); lv.add(n.val);
            if (n.left != null) q.add(n.left);
            if (n.right != null) q.add(n.right);
        }
        res.add(lv);
    }
    return res;
}
--py
class Node:
    def __init__(self, val, left=None, right=None):
        self.val, self.left, self.right = val, left, right

# height(v) comes from the piece u-treeh; everything below is built on it
def size(t):
    return 0 if t is None else 1 + size(t.left) + size(t.right)

def is_balanced(t):
    """Is every node's left and right height within 1 of each other?"""
    if t is None: return True
    return abs(height(t.left) - height(t.right)) <= 1 and is_balanced(t.left) and is_balanced(t.right)

def inorder(t, out):
    if t is None: return
    inorder(t.left, out); out.append(t.val); inorder(t.right, out)

def levels(root):
    """Values level by level, one list per level."""
    res = []
    if root is None: return res
    q = [root]
    while q:
        res.append([n.val for n in q])       # exactly one level
        q = [c for n in q for c in (n.left, n.right) if c is not None]
    return res
--cpp
struct Node {
    int val; Node *left = nullptr, *right = nullptr;
    Node(int v) : val(v) {}
};

// height(v) comes from the piece u-treeh; everything below is built on it
int treeSize(Node* t) { return t ? 1 + treeSize(t->left) + treeSize(t->right) : 0; }

// is every node's left and right height within 1 of each other?
bool isBalanced(Node* t) {
    if (!t) return true;
    return abs(height(t->left) - height(t->right)) <= 1 && isBalanced(t->left) && isBalanced(t->right);
}

void inorder(Node* t, vector<int>& out) {
    if (!t) return;
    inorder(t->left, out); out.push_back(t->val); inorder(t->right, out);
}

// values level by level, one vector per level
vector<vector<int>> levels(Node* root) {
    vector<vector<int>> res;
    if (!root) return res;
    queue<Node*> q; q.push(root);
    while (!q.empty()) {
        int cnt = q.size();                      // exactly one level
        res.push_back({});
        while (cnt--) {
            Node* n = q.front(); q.pop();
            res.back().push_back(n->val);
            if (n->left) q.push(n->left);
            if (n->right) q.push(n->right);
        }
    }
    return res;
}
--uses
u-treeh

### bst
--idea
A binary search tree (BST) is a binary tree with one ordering rule at every node: everything in the left subtree is smaller than the node, and everything in the right subtree is larger. Searching is then like the guessing game "higher or lower": compare with the node, throw away one side, repeat.
If the tree is roughly balanced, each comparison halves the remaining items, so search, insert and delete take about log n steps. If you insert already-sorted data, the tree becomes a straight line and everything slows to O(n); that is why balanced variants (AVL, red-black, treap) exist.
--build
1. Node has `key`, `left`, `right`. Invariant: left subtree keys `< key < ` right subtree keys.
2. `search(key)`: start at the root. If equal, found. If smaller, go left. If larger, go right. If you fall off (null), it is absent.
3. `insert(key)`: walk like a search. At the null spot, put a new node there. Recursive form: `insert(t, k)` returns the subtree root, `t.left = insert(t.left, k)` or `t.right = ...`.
4. `delete(key)`: find the node. If it has no children, remove it. If one child, replace it by that child. If two children, copy in the smallest key of the right subtree (the successor), then delete that successor from the right subtree.
5. `min` is the leftmost node, `max` the rightmost. Successor of a key is the smallest key greater than it: remember the last node where you turned left.
6. Inorder traversal visits keys in sorted order.
7. Layering: building a tree from values calls `insert` (code piece `u-treeins`), one value at a time.
--think
- Signal: "insert and search with order", "find the next larger or smaller value", "validate a BST", "k-th smallest", and any problem on a tree that comes with a sortedness rule.
- Decide first how duplicates are handled (reject, count them, or always go right). Be consistent.
- Neighbouring techniques: in contests use the built-in ordered set or map instead of writing a BST. Write your own when you need extra data in nodes (subtree sizes, sums), which leads to order statistic trees. For guaranteed speed, use a balanced one.
- Validating a BST: pass down the allowed `(low, high)` range. Checking only parent against child misses cases where a deep node breaks a higher rule.
- Pitfalls: skewed trees from sorted input, forgetting to reattach the result of the recursive delete, deleting a node with two children without picking the successor, and using `int` bounds that collide with real keys (use `long` or null bounds).
--cost
Search, insert, delete take O(h) where h is the height. Balanced: O(log n). Worst case (a line): O(n). Space O(n).
--java
static class Node {
    int key; Node left, right;
    Node(int k) { key = k; }
}

// insert(v, x) comes from the piece u-treeins: walk left or right, attach at the empty spot.
// The functions below are built on the same tree shape.

boolean contains(Node t, int k) {
    while (t != null) {
        if (k == t.key) return true;
        t = (k < t.key) ? t.left : t.right;
    }
    return false;
}

Node delete(Node t, int k) {
    if (t == null) return null;
    if (k < t.key) t.left = delete(t.left, k);
    else if (k > t.key) t.right = delete(t.right, k);
    else {
        if (t.left == null) return t.right;     // zero or one child
        if (t.right == null) return t.left;
        Node s = t.right;
        while (s.left != null) s = s.left;      // successor: leftmost in the right subtree
        t.key = s.key;
        t.right = delete(t.right, s.key);
    }
    return t;
}

boolean isBst(Node t, long lo, long hi) {   // call with Long.MIN_VALUE, Long.MAX_VALUE
    if (t == null) return true;
    if (t.key <= lo || t.key >= hi) return false;
    return isBst(t.left, lo, t.key) && isBst(t.right, t.key, hi);
}

// build a tree from values, one insert at a time
Node build(int[] values) {
    Node root = null;
    for (int x : values) root = insert(root, x);
    return root;
}
--py
class Node:
    def __init__(self, key):
        self.key = key
        self.left = self.right = None

# insert(v, x) comes from the piece u-treeins: walk left or right, attach at the empty spot.
# The functions below are built on the same tree shape.

def contains(t, k):
    while t:
        if k == t.key: return True
        t = t.left if k < t.key else t.right
    return False

def delete(t, k):
    if t is None: return None
    if k < t.key: t.left = delete(t.left, k)
    elif k > t.key: t.right = delete(t.right, k)
    else:
        if t.left is None: return t.right      # zero or one child
        if t.right is None: return t.left
        s = t.right
        while s.left: s = s.left               # successor: leftmost in the right subtree
        t.key = s.key
        t.right = delete(t.right, s.key)
    return t

def is_bst(t, lo=float('-inf'), hi=float('inf')):
    if t is None: return True
    if not (lo < t.key < hi): return False
    return is_bst(t.left, lo, t.key) and is_bst(t.right, t.key, hi)

def build(values):
    """Build a tree from values, one insert at a time."""
    root = None
    for x in values:
        root = insert(root, x)
    return root
--cpp
struct Node {
    int key; Node *left = nullptr, *right = nullptr;
    Node(int k) : key(k) {}
};

// insert(v, x) comes from the piece u-treeins: walk left or right, attach at the empty spot.
// The functions below are built on the same tree shape.

bool contains(Node* t, int k) {
    while (t) {
        if (k == t->key) return true;
        t = (k < t->key) ? t->left : t->right;
    }
    return false;
}

Node* erase(Node* t, int k) {
    if (!t) return nullptr;
    if (k < t->key) t->left = erase(t->left, k);
    else if (k > t->key) t->right = erase(t->right, k);
    else {
        if (!t->left) { Node* r = t->right; delete t; return r; }   // zero or one child
        if (!t->right) { Node* l = t->left; delete t; return l; }
        Node* s = t->right;
        while (s->left) s = s->left;            // successor: leftmost in the right subtree
        t->key = s->key;
        t->right = erase(t->right, s->key);
    }
    return t;
}

bool isBst(Node* t, long long lo = LLONG_MIN, long long hi = LLONG_MAX) {
    if (!t) return true;
    if (t->key <= lo || t->key >= hi) return false;
    return isBst(t->left, lo, t->key) && isBst(t->right, t->key, hi);
}

// build a tree from values, one insert at a time
Node* build(const vector<int>& values) {
    Node* root = nullptr;
    for (int x : values) root = insert(root, x);
    return root;
}
--uses
u-treeins

### trie
--idea
A trie (say "try", from retrieval) stores words in a tree where each step down is one letter. Words that start the same share the same path, so "car", "cart" and "cat" all begin with the shared route c, a. Think of a paper dictionary's thumb tabs, then second letters, then third letters, but with the tabs forming a tree.
Searching a word costs its length, not the number of words stored. And a trie answers questions a hash table cannot: "which words start with `ca`?", "what is the longest stored prefix of this text?". Each node needs only to know its children (one per letter) and whether a word ends there.
--build
1. A node has `next[26]` (child for each letter, empty at first), and `end` (does a word finish here?). Create one empty root.
2. `insert(word)`: start at the root. For each letter `c`, if `next[c]` is empty create a new node, then move into it. After the last letter set `end = true`.
3. `contains(word)`: walk letter by letter. If a child is missing, answer no. At the end answer `end`.
4. `startsWith(prefix)`: same walk, but at the end answer yes if you got there at all.
5. Keep a counter `pass` in each node (how many words go through it). Then "how many words have this prefix" is the `pass` of the node at the end of the prefix.
6. For big alphabets use a hash map of children instead of an array of 26, or store the trie in flat arrays: `child[node][letter]` as an `int` table, node 0 is the root.
7. Bitwise trie: the same thing with the bits of numbers (two children, 0 and 1). It finds the maximum xor of a number with a set of numbers by choosing the opposite bit each time if possible.
8. Layering: `insert` and `search` are the code piece `u-trieins`; the prefix questions here extend that trie with `startsWith`, `countPrefix` and `longestWordPrefix`.
--think
