- Signal: "words with a common prefix", "autocomplete", "does any word in the dictionary start this text", "longest common prefix", "maximum xor pair", "word search in a grid with a dictionary".
- Decide first the alphabet and the node layout. A 26-array is fastest but uses memory; a map saves memory but is slower.
- Neighbouring techniques: a hash set answers exact membership in O(length) too and is smaller, so use a trie only when prefixes matter. A sorted list plus binary search also answers prefix questions. Aho-Corasick extends a trie to search many words in a text at once.
- Why the cost is good: every letter of every inserted word creates at most one node, so total memory is bounded by the total input length.
- Pitfalls: forgetting `end` (so "car" matches because "cart" exists), index `c - 'a'` out of range for capital letters or digits, memory blow-up with 26 pointers per node on huge dictionaries, and recursion depth on very long words.
--cost
Insert, search and prefix test are O(L) for a word of length L, independent of how many words are stored. Space up to O(total letters x alphabet).
--java
// extends the piece u-trieins (insert and search over arrays nxt and end) with prefix questions
static class PrefixTrie extends Trie {
    private int walk(String s) {                 // node reached by s, or -1
        int v = 0;
        for (char ch : s.toCharArray()) {
            v = nxt[v][ch - 'a'];
            if (v == 0) return -1;
        }
        return v;
    }
    boolean startsWith(String p) { return walk(p) >= 0; }

    private int countWords(int v) {              // words stored at or below node v
        int c = end[v] ? 1 : 0;
        for (int k = 0; k < 26; k++) if (nxt[v][k] != 0) c += countWords(nxt[v][k]);
        return c;
    }
    int countPrefix(String p) { int v = walk(p); return v < 0 ? 0 : countWords(v); }

    // length of the longest stored word that is a prefix of text, or -1
    int longestWordPrefix(String text) {
        int v = 0, best = -1;
        for (int i = 0; i < text.length(); i++) {
            v = nxt[v][text.charAt(i) - 'a'];
            if (v == 0) break;
            if (end[v]) best = i + 1;
        }
        return best;
    }
}
--py
class PrefixTrie(Trie):
    """Extends the piece u-trieins (insert and search over self.nodes and self.end) with prefix questions."""
    def _walk(self, s):
        v = 0
        for ch in s:
            if ch not in self.nodes[v]: return -1
            v = self.nodes[v][ch]
        return v

    def starts_with(self, p):
        return self._walk(p) >= 0

    def _count_words(self, v):                    # words stored at or below node v
        return (1 if self.end[v] else 0) + sum(self._count_words(c) for c in self.nodes[v].values())

    def count_prefix(self, p):
        v = self._walk(p)
        return 0 if v < 0 else self._count_words(v)

    def longest_word_prefix(self, text):
        """Length of the longest stored word that is a prefix of text, or -1."""
        v, best = 0, -1
        for i, ch in enumerate(text):
            if ch not in self.nodes[v]: break
            v = self.nodes[v][ch]
            if self.end[v]: best = i + 1
        return best
--cpp
// extends the piece u-trieins (insert and search over arrays nxt and end) with prefix questions
struct PrefixTrie : Trie {
    int walk(const string& s) const {             // node reached by s, or -1
        int v = 0;
        for (char ch : s) {
            v = nxt[v][ch - 'a'];
            if (!v) return -1;
        }
        return v;
    }
    bool startsWith(const string& p) const { return walk(p) >= 0; }

    int countWords(int v) const {                 // words stored at or below node v
        int c = end[v] ? 1 : 0;
        for (int k = 0; k < 26; k++) if (nxt[v][k]) c += countWords(nxt[v][k]);
        return c;
    }
    int countPrefix(const string& p) const { int v = walk(p); return v < 0 ? 0 : countWords(v); }

    // length of the longest stored word that is a prefix of text, or -1
    int longestWordPrefix(const string& text) const {
        int v = 0, best = -1;
        for (int i = 0; i < (int)text.size(); i++) {
            v = nxt[v][text[i] - 'a'];
            if (!v) break;
            if (end[v]) best = i + 1;
        }
        return best;
    }
};
--uses
u-trieins

### mono
--idea
A monotonic stack is an ordinary stack with one extra rule: the values inside are always kept in order (for example, decreasing from bottom to top). Before pushing a new item, you pop every item that would break the order. Those popped items have just met the thing that answers their question.
The classic question is "for each item, where is the next greater item to its right?". Walk left to right. When the new item is bigger than the one on top, the new item is the answer for the top item, so pop it and record the answer. Each item is pushed once and popped once, so the whole sweep is linear even though it has a loop inside a loop. A monotonic queue is the same idea with a deque, used for window maximums.
--build
1. Make an empty stack `st` that holds indexes (not values), and an answer array `nxt` filled with "none" (for example `n`, meaning no greater item).
2. For `i` from 0 to `n - 1`: while the stack is not empty and `a[st.top] < a[i]`, pop `j = st.top` and set `nxt[j] = i`. Item `i` is the first greater item to the right of `j`.
3. Push `i`.
4. Items still on the stack at the end have no greater item to their right.
5. Previous greater (to the left) is read off the same sweep: after the pops, the new top of the stack is the nearest item to the left that is `>= a[i]`.
6. Largest rectangle in a histogram: for each bar find the nearest shorter bar on each side (a monotonic stack with increasing heights). The bar's rectangle spans between them, and its area is `height x width`.
7. For ties, decide once whether you pop on `<` or on `<=`, and use the same rule everywhere. This decides which of equal items owns the answer.
8. Layering: every push, peek and pop on the index stack is a call to the stack moves of code piece `u-stackop`.
--think
- Signal: "next greater", "next smaller", "how many days until a warmer day", "largest rectangle", "span of stock prices", "sum of subarray minimums", "trapping rain water". Any question about the nearest item on one side that beats the current one.
- Decide first which direction you scan, whether the stack is increasing or decreasing, and what you do at the moment of popping. The popped item is the one that gets its answer.
- Neighbouring techniques: a sparse table or segment tree gives range minimum but not "nearest smaller" directly. Two pointers can handle some of these in simpler cases. For window maximum use the monotonic deque (see deque).
- Count contributions: in "sum of subarray minimums", each element is the minimum of some number of subarrays, equal to `(i - prevSmaller) x (nextSmaller - i)`. Use strict on one side and non-strict on the other so equal values are not double counted.
- Pitfalls: storing values and losing positions, forgetting leftover items, mixing `<` with `<=` between the two sweeps, and using the wrong stack direction (increasing versus decreasing).
--cost
O(n) time, because every index is pushed and popped at most once. Space O(n).
--java
// nxt[i] = index of the next item strictly greater than a[i], or n if none
// (stack moves push, peek, pop come from the piece u-stackop)
static int[] nextGreater(int[] a) {
    int n = a.length;
    int[] nxt = new int[n];
    java.util.Arrays.fill(nxt, n);
    java.util.Deque<Integer> st = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();   // indexes, values decreasing
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        while (!st.isEmpty() && a[peek(st)] < a[i]) nxt[pop(st)] = i;   // i answers these
        push(st, i);
    }
    return nxt;
}

// area of the largest rectangle in a histogram
static long largestRectangle(int[] h) {
    int n = h.length;
    long best = 0;
    java.util.Deque<Integer> st = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();   // indexes, heights increasing
    for (int i = 0; i <= n; i++) {
        int cur = (i == n) ? 0 : h[i];                            // a height-0 sentinel flushes the stack
        while (!st.isEmpty() && h[peek(st)] >= cur) {
            int height = h[pop(st)];
            int left = st.isEmpty() ? -1 : peek(st);              // nearest shorter bar on the left
            best = Math.max(best, (long) height * (i - left - 1));
        }
        push(st, i);
    }
    return best;
}
--py
def next_greater(a):
    """nxt[i] = index of the next item strictly greater than a[i], or n if none.
    The stack moves push, peek, pop come from the piece u-stackop."""
    n = len(a)
    nxt = [n] * n
    st = []                                  # indexes, values decreasing
    for i, x in enumerate(a):
        while st and a[peek(st)] < x:
            nxt[pop(st)] = i                 # i answers these
        push(st, i)
    return nxt

def largest_rectangle(h):
    """Area of the largest rectangle in a histogram."""
    best = 0
    st = []                                  # indexes, heights increasing
    for i in range(len(h) + 1):
        cur = h[i] if i < len(h) else 0      # a height-0 sentinel flushes the stack
        while st and h[peek(st)] >= cur:
            height = h[pop(st)]
            left = peek(st) if st else -1    # nearest shorter bar on the left
            best = max(best, height * (i - left - 1))
        push(st, i)
    return best
--cpp
// nxt[i] = index of the next item strictly greater than a[i], or n if none
// (stack moves push, peek, pop come from the piece u-stackop)
vector<int> nextGreater(const vector<int>& a) {
    int n = a.size();
    vector<int> nxt(n, n);
    stack<int> st;                                     // indexes, values decreasing
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        while (!st.empty() && a[peek(st)] < a[i]) nxt[pop(st)] = i;   // i answers these
        push(st, i);
    }
    return nxt;
}

// area of the largest rectangle in a histogram
long long largestRectangle(const vector<int>& h) {
    int n = h.size();
    long long best = 0;
    stack<int> st;                                     // indexes, heights increasing
    for (int i = 0; i <= n; i++) {
        int cur = (i == n) ? 0 : h[i];                 // a height-0 sentinel flushes the stack
        while (!st.empty() && h[peek(st)] >= cur) {
            long long height = h[pop(st)];
            int left = st.empty() ? -1 : peek(st);     // nearest shorter bar on the left
            best = max(best, height * (i - left - 1));
        }
        push(st, i);
    }
    return best;
}
--uses
u-stackop

### tmap
--idea
An ordered set (or ordered map) is a collection that keeps its keys sorted at all times, while still letting you insert and delete quickly. A plain hash set can tell you "is 17 in here?" but cannot tell you "what is the smallest key above 17?". An ordered set can, because it knows the order.
It is usually a balanced search tree under the hood (a red-black tree in C++ `std::set` and `std::map`, and Java `TreeSet` and `TreeMap`). The questions it answers are about neighbours: the floor (largest key `<= x`), the ceiling (smallest key `>= x`), the first and last key, and a walk over a range of keys in order.
--build
1. Pick the container: a set stores keys only; a map stores key and value pairs. Both keep keys sorted.
2. Insert and remove by key. Each takes O(log n).
3. For "nearest at or above `x`" use the ceiling query: `ceiling(x)` in Java, `lower_bound(x)` in C++. For "nearest at or below `x`" use floor: `floor(x)` in Java, one step before `upper_bound(x)` in C++.
4. For the smallest and largest keys use `first` and `last` (Java), or `begin()` and `prev(end())` (C++).
5. To walk a range, find the ceiling of the low end, then step forward while the key is within the high end.
6. In Python there is no built-in ordered set. Keep a sorted list and use `bisect` to find the position; inserting into the list costs O(n) but is very fast in practice for up to about 10^5 items. For larger data use a library such as `sortedcontainers`.
7. Typical use: scheduling with intervals. Keep start times in the set. For a new interval, check its floor and ceiling neighbours to see whether it overlaps either one.
--think
- Signal: "closest value", "next larger element that was inserted", "does this interval overlap an existing one", "maintain a sorted window and read its median or min and max", "count of items in a range" (the last one needs an order statistic tree).
- Decide first whether you need order at all. If not, use a hash set or map, which is faster. If you need only min or max, a heap is simpler.
- Neighbouring techniques: sorted array plus binary search works if the set does not change. A segment tree or Fenwick tree works on coordinate-compressed values when you also need counts and sums. A heap cannot do floor and ceiling.
- Think of each query as "find a position by key, then look one step left or right". Almost every use is that.
- Pitfalls: C++ `lower_bound` returns `end()` when there is nothing, and you must not dereference it. Calling `std::lower_bound` on a `set` (a slow linear walk) instead of the member `s.lower_bound(x)`. Java `TreeMap.floorKey` returns null when none exists, so check before unboxing. Duplicates are ignored by a set: use a multiset for repeats.
--cost
Insert, remove, floor, ceiling are O(log n). Ordered walk O(n). Space O(n).
--java
// the smallest stored value that is >= x, or -1 if none; and the largest <= x
static int ceilingOf(java.util.TreeSet<Integer> s, int x) {
    Integer r = s.ceiling(x);
    return r == null ? -1 : r;
}
static int floorOf(java.util.TreeSet<Integer> s, int x) {
    Integer r = s.floor(x);
    return r == null ? -1 : r;
}

// can the interval [start, end) be booked without overlapping an earlier booking?
// bookings maps start -> end, intervals never overlap each other
static boolean book(java.util.TreeMap<Integer, Integer> bookings, int start, int end) {
    java.util.Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> before = bookings.floorEntry(start);    // starts at or before us
    if (before != null && before.getValue() > start) return false;               // it runs into us
    java.util.Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> after = bookings.ceilingEntry(start);  // starts at or after us
    if (after != null && after.getKey() < end) return false;                     // we run into it
    bookings.put(start, end);
    return true;
}
--py
import bisect

class SortedSet:
    """Sorted list plus bisect. Insert is O(n) by list shifting but very fast in practice."""
    def __init__(self):
        self.a = []

    def add(self, x):
        i = bisect.bisect_left(self.a, x)
        if i == len(self.a) or self.a[i] != x:
            self.a.insert(i, x)

    def remove(self, x):
        i = bisect.bisect_left(self.a, x)
        if i < len(self.a) and self.a[i] == x:
            self.a.pop(i)

    def ceiling(self, x):                     # smallest >= x, or None
        i = bisect.bisect_left(self.a, x)
        return self.a[i] if i < len(self.a) else None

    def floor(self, x):                       # largest <= x, or None
        i = bisect.bisect_right(self.a, x)
        return self.a[i - 1] if i > 0 else None

def book(starts, ends, start, end):
    """Book [start, end) if free. starts is a SortedSet of starts; ends maps start -> end."""
    b = starts.floor(start)
    if b is not None and ends[b] > start: return False       # an earlier booking runs into us
    a = starts.ceiling(start)
    if a is not None and a < end: return False               # we run into a later booking
    starts.add(start); ends[start] = end
    return True
--cpp
// smallest stored value >= x, or -1 if none
int ceilingOf(const set<int>& s, int x) {
    auto it = s.lower_bound(x);
    return it == s.end() ? -1 : *it;
}

// largest stored value <= x, or -1 if none
int floorOf(const set<int>& s, int x) {
    auto it = s.upper_bound(x);                 // first key > x
    return it == s.begin() ? -1 : *prev(it);    // the one before it
}

// can [start, end) be booked without overlapping an earlier booking?
// bookings maps start -> end, intervals never overlap each other
bool book(map<int,int>& bookings, int start, int end) {
    auto after = bookings.lower_bound(start);                 // starts at or after us
    if (after != bookings.end() && after->first < end) return false;   // we run into it
    if (after != bookings.begin()) {
        auto before = prev(after);                            // starts before us
        if (before->second > start) return false;             // it runs into us
    }
    bookings[start] = end;
    return true;
}

### multiset
--idea
A multiset is an ordered set that allows repeats. You can put the number 5 in three times, and it will be stored three times. It keeps everything sorted, so you can read the smallest or largest at any moment, and unlike a heap you can also remove any specific value.
Think of a sorted bag of marbles. You can ask for the lightest marble, the heaviest, or the next heavier one than a given weight, and you can take out one marble of a chosen weight. The classic use is a sliding window: add the new item, remove the one that left, and read the min or max.
--build
1. Store counts, not copies: a sorted map from `value` to `count`. This is the same as a tree-based ordered map.
2. `add(x)`: `count[x] = getOrDefault(count, x, 0) + 1` (create the key if missing).
3. `removeOne(x)`: read `c = getOrDefault(count, x, 0)`; if `c` is 0 the value is absent. Otherwise `count[x]--`; if it reaches 0, delete the key. If `x` is absent, do nothing (or report an error).
4. `min()` is the first key, `max()` is the last key.
5. `contains(x)`: `count[x] > 0`. `countOf(x)`: read the count with `getOrDefault(count, x, 0)` (code piece `u-hget`), which gives 0 for a missing key. `add` and `removeOne` read the old count the same way.
6. `ceiling(x)` and `floor(x)` work exactly as in an ordered set, on the keys.
7. Sliding window median or max of width `k`: add `a[i]`; once `i >= k`, `removeOne(a[i - k])`; read `max()` or the middle element.
--think
- Signal: you need min or max of a changing collection and also need to delete elements that are not at the top, with repeats allowed. "Window of the last k items", "remove one occurrence", "closest value to x among those currently present".
- Decide first whether you actually need removal of arbitrary items. If you only remove the top, a heap is simpler. If you can delete lazily (skip stale entries later), a heap with a "to delete" counter also works and is faster.
- Neighbouring techniques: a sorted map with counts is the same thing and gives more control. For the median of a window, two multisets (lower half, upper half) rebalanced after each step, or an order statistic tree.
- Pitfalls in C++: `s.erase(value)` removes all copies of that value; to remove only one, use `s.erase(s.find(value))`. In Java `TreeMap.merge` with a lambda that returns null removes the key, which is a handy trick. Remember `size()` is the number of copies, not distinct values.
--cost
Add, remove one, min, max, floor, ceiling: O(log n). Space O(n) for distinct values (with counts).
--uses
u-hget
--java
// multiset of ints as a TreeMap of value -> count
static class MultiSet {
    java.util.TreeMap<Integer, Integer> cnt = new java.util.TreeMap<>();
    int size = 0;

    void add(int x) { cnt.put(x, getOrDefault(cnt, x, 0) + 1); size++; }

    boolean removeOne(int x) {
        int c = getOrDefault(cnt, x, 0);                 // piece u-hget
        if (c == 0) return false;
        if (c == 1) cnt.remove(x); else cnt.put(x, c - 1);
        size--;
        return true;
    }
    int min() { return cnt.firstKey(); }
    int max() { return cnt.lastKey(); }
    int countOf(int x) { return getOrDefault(cnt, x, 0); }
}

// max of every window of width k
static int[] windowMax(int[] a, int k) {
    MultiSet ms = new MultiSet();
    int[] res = new int[a.length - k + 1];
    for (int i = 0; i < a.length; i++) {
        ms.add(a[i]);
        if (i >= k) ms.removeOne(a[i - k]);      // the item that just left the window
        if (i >= k - 1) res[i - k + 1] = ms.max();
    }
    return res;
}
--py
import bisect

class MultiSet:
    """Counts in a dict plus a sorted list of the distinct values, for min and max."""
    def __init__(self):
        self.cnt, self.keys = {}, []

    def add(self, x):
        c = get_or_default(self.cnt, x, 0)            # piece u-hget
        if c == 0:
            bisect.insort(self.keys, x)               # a new distinct value
        self.cnt[x] = c + 1

    def remove_one(self, x):
        c = get_or_default(self.cnt, x, 0)
        if c == 0:
            return False
        if c == 1:
            del self.cnt[x]
            self.keys.pop(bisect.bisect_left(self.keys, x))
        else:
            self.cnt[x] = c - 1
        return True

    def min(self): return self.keys[0]
    def max(self): return self.keys[-1]
    def count_of(self, x): return get_or_default(self.cnt, x, 0)

def window_max(a, k):
    ms, res = MultiSet(), []
    for i, x in enumerate(a):
        ms.add(x)
        if i >= k:
            ms.remove_one(a[i - k])           # the item that just left the window
        if i >= k - 1:
            res.append(ms.max())
    return res
--cpp
// multiset of ints as a map of value -> count (sorted by value)
struct MultiSet {
    map<int, int> cnt;

    void add(int x) { cnt[x] = getOrDefault(cnt, x, 0) + 1; }

    bool removeOne(int x) {                    // remove ONE copy
        int c = getOrDefault(cnt, x, 0);       // piece u-hget
        if (c == 0) return false;
        if (c == 1) cnt.erase(x); else cnt[x] = c - 1;
        return true;
    }
    int min() const { return cnt.begin()->first; }
    int max() const { return cnt.rbegin()->first; }
    int countOf(int x) const { return getOrDefault(cnt, x, 0); }
};

// max of every window of width k
vector<int> windowMax(const vector<int>& a, int k) {
    MultiSet ms;
    vector<int> res;
    for (int i = 0; i < (int)a.size(); i++) {
        ms.add(a[i]);
        if (i >= k) ms.removeOne(a[i - k]);   // the item that just left the window
        if (i >= k - 1) res.push_back(ms.max());
    }
    return res;
}

### lru
--idea
An LRU cache (least recently used) is a small store that keeps the `capacity` items you touched most recently and throws away the one you have not touched for the longest time when it is full. It bets that what you used lately you will use again soon, which is true for browsers, databases and memory pages.
The challenge is doing both "find an item by key" and "tell me which item is the oldest" in constant time. Two structures cooperate. A hash table finds the item for a key at once. A doubly linked list keeps items in order of use, newest at the front. The hash table stores pointers into the list, so after finding an item you can unlink it and move it to the front without searching.
--build
1. Keep a hash map `map` from key to list node, and a doubly linked list with sentinel nodes `head` and `tail`. Items near `head` are recent, items near `tail` are old.
2. `get(key)`: if the key is not in `map`, return "not found". Otherwise take the node, unlink it, insert it right after `head`, and return its value.
3. `put(key, value)`: if the key exists, update the value and move the node to the front.
4. If the key is new: create a node, put it in `map`, insert it after `head`.
5. If `map.size()` now exceeds `capacity`: take the node before `tail` (the oldest), unlink it, and delete its key from `map`. That is why each node stores its own key.
6. Every step is a hash lookup plus a fixed number of pointer changes, so everything is O(1).
7. Layering: the ready-made `LRU` (code piece `u-lru`) is used as a cache in `cachedSquare`; the version built here shows what is inside it.
--think
- Signal: "design a cache with capacity c", "evict the least recently used", "O(1) get and put". It is a famous design problem.
- Decide first what counts as a use. Usually both `get` and `put` refresh an item.
- Neighbouring techniques: LFU (least frequently used) needs counts as well and is more complex. FIFO eviction needs only a queue. In Java, `LinkedHashMap` with access order and `removeEldestEntry` is an LRU in a few lines. Python has `OrderedDict.move_to_end` and `popitem(last=False)`.
- Why a hash map alone fails: it has no order. Why a list alone fails: finding the key is O(n). You need both, joined by the stored pointer.
- Pitfalls: forgetting to store the key in the node (so you cannot delete it from the map on eviction), evicting before inserting when the key already exists, not refreshing on `get`, and the order of the four pointer updates when linking.
--cost
`get` and `put` are O(1) each. Space O(capacity).
--java
static class LRUCache {
    class Node { int key, val; Node prev, next; Node(int k, int v) { key = k; val = v; } }
    int cap; java.util.HashMap<Integer, Node> map = new java.util.HashMap<>();
    Node head = new Node(0, 0), tail = new Node(0, 0);    // sentinels

    LRUCache(int cap) { this.cap = cap; head.next = tail; tail.prev = head; }

    void unlink(Node n) { n.prev.next = n.next; n.next.prev = n.prev; }
    void pushFront(Node n) { n.next = head.next; n.prev = head; head.next.prev = n; head.next = n; }

    int get(int key) {                          // -1 if absent
        Node n = map.get(key);
        if (n == null) return -1;
        unlink(n); pushFront(n);                // now the most recent
        return n.val;
    }

    void put(int key, int val) {
        Node n = map.get(key);
        if (n != null) { n.val = val; unlink(n); pushFront(n); return; }
        n = new Node(key, val);
        map.put(key, n); pushFront(n);
        if (map.size() > cap) {                 // evict the oldest
            Node old = tail.prev;
            unlink(old); map.remove(old.key);
        }
    }
}

// the ready-made cache LRU (piece u-lru) in use: keep slow results so repeats are cheap.
// calls[0] counts how often the slow work really ran.
static int cachedSquare(LRU<Integer, Integer> cache, int x, int[] calls) {
    Integer hit = cache.get(x);
    if (hit != null) return hit;                // found: no slow work
    calls[0]++;                                 // pretend this is expensive
    int v = x * x;
    cache.put(x, v);
    return v;
}
--py
from collections import OrderedDict

class LRUCache:
    """OrderedDict is a hash map plus a doubly linked list in one object."""
    def __init__(self, cap):
        self.cap = cap
        self.d = OrderedDict()

    def get(self, key):                    # -1 if absent
        if key not in self.d: return -1
        self.d.move_to_end(key)            # now the most recent (at the end)
        return self.d[key]

    def put(self, key, val):
        if key in self.d:
            self.d.move_to_end(key)
        self.d[key] = val
        if len(self.d) > self.cap:
            self.d.popitem(last=False)     # evict the oldest (at the front)

def cached_square(cache, x, calls):
    """Uses the ready-made cache LRU (piece u-lru): keep slow results so repeats are cheap.
    calls[0] counts how often the slow work really ran."""
    hit = cache.get(x)
    if hit is not None:
        return hit                         # found: no slow work
    calls[0] += 1                          # pretend this is expensive
    v = x * x
    cache.put(x, v)
    return v
--cpp
struct LRUCache {
    int cap;
    list<pair<int,int>> items;                              // front = most recent; pairs of (key, value)
    unordered_map<int, list<pair<int,int>>::iterator> pos;  // key -> its place in the list

    LRUCache(int cap) : cap(cap) {}

    int get(int key) {                                      // -1 if absent
        auto it = pos.find(key);
        if (it == pos.end()) return -1;
        items.splice(items.begin(), items, it->second);     // move node to the front in O(1)
        return it->second->second;
    }

    void put(int key, int val) {
        auto it = pos.find(key);
        if (it != pos.end()) {
            it->second->second = val;
            items.splice(items.begin(), items, it->second);
            return;
        }
        items.emplace_front(key, val);
        pos[key] = items.begin();
        if ((int)items.size() > cap) {                      // evict the oldest
            pos.erase(items.back().first);
            items.pop_back();
        }
    }
};

// the ready-made cache LRU (piece u-lru) in use: keep slow results so repeats are cheap.
// calls counts how often the slow work really ran. Values here are never -1.
int cachedSquare(LRU& cache, int x, int& calls) {
    int hit = cache.get(x);
    if (hit != -1) return hit;                  // found: no slow work
    calls++;                                    // pretend this is expensive
    int v = x * x;
    cache.put(x, v);
    return v;
}
--uses
u-lru

### topk
--idea
Top-k means keeping only the `k` best items out of a long stream, without storing or sorting all of them. To keep the `k` largest, use a min-heap of size `k`. Its top is the weakest of the current best `k`. A new item is compared only against that weakest one: if it is bigger, it kicks the weakest out; if not, it is ignored.
It feels backwards (a min-heap to find the largest), but it is just a bouncer at a club with `k` places: the bouncer only needs to know who the least impressive guest inside is.
--build
1. Make an empty min-heap `h`. (For the `k` smallest, use a max-heap.)
2. For each item `x` in the stream: if `h.size < k`, push `x`.
3. Otherwise compare `x` with `h.peek()`, the smallest of the kept items. If `x > h.peek()`, pop the top and push `x`. Else skip `x`.
4. After the stream, `h` holds the `k` largest items (in no particular order). Pop them all and reverse to get them in descending order.
5. The `k`-th largest overall is `h.peek()` at the end, because it is the smallest of the best `k`.
6. For "top `k` most frequent": count with a hash map first, then run the same heap over `(count, item)` pairs.
7. For a stream where the answer is asked at any time, the heap always reflects the current top `k`.
8. Layering: the best-k heap is filled and drained with `heapPush` and `heapPop` (code pieces `u-hpush`, `u-hpop`).
--think
- Signal: "k-th largest", "k most frequent words", "k closest points", "top k results from a huge file". The key sign is that `k` is much smaller than `n` or that data arrives as a stream.
- Decide first whether you want largest or smallest, and flip the heap type to the opposite (largest uses a min-heap). Decide how to break ties.
- Neighbouring techniques: if `k` is close to `n`, sorting all is just as good, O(n log n). Quickselect finds the k-th in O(n) on average but needs all data in memory and does not stream. Counting sort works when values are small. For merging `k` sorted lists use a heap of one head per list.
- Choose by cost: heap of size `k` is O(n log k), which beats O(n log n) when `k` is small.
- Pitfalls: using a max-heap of everything (memory O(n)), comparing against the wrong end, off-by-one in `k`, and forgetting that Python `heapq` is a min-heap (which fits the largest-k case nicely).
--cost
O(n log k) time, O(k) space.
--java
// the k largest values of a, in descending order. MinHeap (push, pop) is the piece u-hpush, u-hpop
static int[] topK(int[] a, int k) {
    MinHeap h = new MinHeap();
    for (int x : a) {
        if (h.size < k) h.push(x);
        else if (x > h.h[0]) { h.pop(); h.push(x); }      // beats the weakest of the best k
    }
    int[] res = new int[h.size];
    for (int i = res.length - 1; i >= 0; i--) res[i] = h.pop();   // pops smallest first, fill from the back
    return res;
}

// the k most frequent words (heap of pairs, so the library heap is used here)
static java.util.List<String> topWords(String[] words, int k) {
    java.util.HashMap<String, Integer> cnt = new java.util.HashMap<>();
    for (String w : words) cnt.merge(w, 1, Integer::sum);
    java.util.PriorityQueue<java.util.Map.Entry<String, Integer>> h =
        new java.util.PriorityQueue<>((x, y) -> x.getValue().intValue() != y.getValue().intValue()
            ? Integer.compare(x.getValue(), y.getValue()) : y.getKey().compareTo(x.getKey()));
    for (var e : cnt.entrySet()) { h.add(e); if (h.size() > k) h.poll(); }
    java.util.LinkedList<String> res = new java.util.LinkedList<>();
    while (!h.isEmpty()) res.addFirst(h.poll().getKey());
    return res;
}
--py
import heapq
from collections import Counter

def top_k(a, k):
    """The k largest values, in descending order. heap_push and heap_pop are the pieces u-hpush, u-hpop."""
    h = []                                   # min-heap of the best k so far
    for x in a:
        if len(h) < k:
            heap_push(h, x)
        elif x > h[0]:
            heap_pop(h)                      # drop the weakest
            heap_push(h, x)
    return sorted(h, reverse=True)

def top_words(words, k):
    """The k most frequent words; ties go to the alphabetically smaller word."""
    cnt = Counter(words)
    h = []
    for w, c in cnt.items():
        heapq.heappush(h, (c, _Rev(w)))      # weakest = lowest count, then alphabetically larger
        if len(h) > k: heapq.heappop(h)
    return [r.s for c, r in sorted(h, key=lambda t: (-t[0], t[1].s))]

class _Rev:
    """Wrapper that reverses string order so the heap's weakest is the alphabetically larger word."""
    def __init__(self, s): self.s = s
    def __lt__(self, o): return self.s > o.s
--cpp
// the k largest values of a, in descending order. heapPush and heapPop are the pieces u-hpush, u-hpop
vector<int> topK(const vector<int>& a, int k) {
    vector<int> h;                                          // min-heap: h.front() is the smallest
    for (int x : a) {
        if ((int)h.size() < k) heapPush(h, x);
        else if (x > h.front()) { heapPop(h); heapPush(h, x); }   // beats the weakest of the best k
    }
    vector<int> res(h.size());
    for (int i = (int)res.size() - 1; i >= 0; i--) res[i] = heapPop(h);
    return res;
}

// the k most frequent words; ties go to the alphabetically smaller word
vector<string> topWords(const vector<string>& words, int k) {
    unordered_map<string, int> cnt;
    for (auto& w : words) cnt[w]++;
    auto weaker = [](const pair<int,string>& x, const pair<int,string>& y) {   // true if x is better than y
        return x.first != y.first ? x.first > y.first : x.second < y.second;
    };
    priority_queue<pair<int,string>, vector<pair<int,string>>, decltype(weaker)> h(weaker);   // top = weakest
    for (auto& [w, c] : cnt) { h.push({c, w}); if ((int)h.size() > k) h.pop(); }
    vector<string> res(h.size());
    for (int i = (int)res.size() - 1; i >= 0; i--) { res[i] = h.top().second; h.pop(); }
    return res;
}
--uses
u-hpush
u-hpop

### medstream
--idea
The running median is the middle value of all numbers seen so far, answered after every new number. Sorting everything each time would be far too slow. The trick is to split the numbers into a lower half and an upper half, and only keep track of the edge between them.
Use two heaps: a max-heap `lo` for the smaller half (its top is the largest of the small ones) and a min-heap `hi` for the larger half (its top is the smallest of the large ones). The median is always one of the two tops, or the average of both.
--build
1. `lo` is a max-heap, `hi` is a min-heap. Invariants: every item in `lo` is `<=` every item in `hi`, and `lo.size` is equal to `hi.size` or exceeds it by exactly 1.
2. `add(x)`: if `lo` is empty or `x <= lo.top`, push `x` into `lo`. Otherwise push into `hi`.
3. Rebalance: if `lo.size > hi.size + 1`, move `lo.top` to `hi`. If `hi.size > lo.size`, move `hi.top` to `lo`.
4. `median()`: if `lo.size > hi.size` the answer is `lo.top`. If the sizes are equal, the answer is `(lo.top + hi.top) / 2`.
5. Why it holds: step 2 keeps the order between the halves, and step 3 only moves the single boundary item, which cannot break the order.
6. For a sliding window, add the new item and remove the old one lazily (a counter of items waiting to be removed from each heap), and fix sizes with the number of live items.
7. Layering: both halves are heaps driven by `heapPush` and `heapPop` (code pieces `u-hpush`, `u-hpop`); the lower half is stored negated.
--think
- Signal: "median of a stream", "median after each insertion", "keep the middle", and also "k-th smallest where k stays near the middle". Variations ask for the sum of the lower half.
- Decide first how the tie between sizes works: give the extra item to the lower half (as above) and be consistent. Decide if the median for even counts is the average or the lower middle.
- Neighbouring techniques: a balanced tree with subtree sizes (order statistic tree) gives any k-th and supports deletes. A Fenwick tree over compressed values gives any k-th too. Use two heaps when you only ever need the middle and deletes are rare or lazy.
- The same split trick generalises: keep the `k` smallest in one heap and the rest in another to track the k-th smallest of a stream.
- Pitfalls: integer average overflow (use `lo.top + (hi.top - lo.top) / 2` or `double`), forgetting to rebalance after every add, and comparing with the wrong top (empty heaps).
--cost
`add` is O(log n). `median` is O(1). Space O(n).
--java
// two heaps built from the piece MinHeap (u-hpush, u-hpop). The lower half is stored negated, so a min-heap acts as a max-heap.
static class MedianStream {
    MinHeap lo = new MinHeap(), hi = new MinHeap();

    void add(int x) {
        if (lo.size == 0 || x <= -lo.h[0]) lo.push(-x); else hi.push(x);
        if (lo.size > hi.size + 1) hi.push(-lo.pop());         // move the boundary item across
        else if (hi.size > lo.size) lo.push(-hi.pop());
    }

    double median() {                                          // needs at least one item
        if (lo.size > hi.size) return -lo.h[0];
        return ((double) -lo.h[0] + hi.h[0]) / 2.0;
    }
}
--py
class MedianStream:
    """Two heaps built from the pieces heap_push and heap_pop (u-hpush, u-hpop)."""
    def __init__(self):
        self.lo = []          # smaller half, stored negated so the min-heap acts as a max-heap
        self.hi = []          # larger half

    def add(self, x):
        if not self.lo or x <= -self.lo[0]:
            heap_push(self.lo, -x)
        else:
            heap_push(self.hi, x)
        if len(self.lo) > len(self.hi) + 1:                  # move the boundary item across
            heap_push(self.hi, -heap_pop(self.lo))
        elif len(self.hi) > len(self.lo):
            heap_push(self.lo, -heap_pop(self.hi))

    def median(self):                                        # needs at least one item
        if len(self.lo) > len(self.hi):
            return -self.lo[0]
        return (-self.lo[0] + self.hi[0]) / 2
--cpp
// two heaps built from the pieces heapPush and heapPop (u-hpush, u-hpop).
// The lower half is stored negated, so a min-heap acts as a max-heap.
struct MedianStream {
    vector<int> lo, hi;

    void add(int x) {
        if (lo.empty() || x <= -lo.front()) heapPush(lo, -x); else heapPush(hi, x);
        if (lo.size() > hi.size() + 1) heapPush(hi, -heapPop(lo));   // move the boundary item across
        else if (hi.size() > lo.size()) heapPush(lo, -heapPop(hi));
    }

    double median() const {                                  // needs at least one item
        if (lo.size() > hi.size()) return -lo.front();
        return ((double)-lo.front() + hi.front()) / 2.0;
    }
};
--uses
u-hpush
u-hpop

### fenwick
--idea
A Fenwick tree (also called a binary indexed tree) answers "what is the sum of the first `i` items?" while the items keep changing. A plain prefix-sum array answers the question in one step but needs O(n) to update. A plain array updates in one step but needs O(n) to sum. A Fenwick tree does both in about log n steps, using only one array.
The trick is that slot `i` does not store one item. It stores the sum of a block of items that ends at `i`, and the block length is the lowest set bit of `i` (written `i & -i`). Slot 12 (binary 1100) covers 4 items (9 to 12), slot 8 covers 8 items (1 to 8), slot 7 covers just item 7. Any prefix is the sum of a few such blocks stacked end to start, and there are at most log n of them.
--build
1. Use 1-based positions. Make `tree` of size `n + 1`, all zero. Slot `i` will hold the sum of items `i - lowbit(i) + 1 .. i`, where `lowbit(i) = i & -i`.
2. `add(i, delta)`: add `delta` into `tree[i]`, then jump to the next block that contains `i`: `i += lowbit(i)`. Repeat while `i <= n`.
3. `prefix(i)`: start with `sum = 0`. Add `tree[i]`, then jump to the block just before this one: `i -= lowbit(i)`. Repeat while `i > 0`.
4. `range(l, r)` (sum of items `l..r`) is `prefix(r) - prefix(l - 1)`.
5. To build from an array in O(n): copy the values into `tree`, then for each `i`, add `tree[i]` into its parent `i + lowbit(i)` if that index is within `n`.
6. To set item `i` to a new value, call `add(i, newValue - oldValue)`. Keep the plain array as well so you know the old value.
7. Find the smallest `i` with `prefix(i) >= k` (needs all items non-negative): start `pos = 0`, and for `step` from the highest power of two `<= n` down to 1, if `pos + step <= n` and `tree[pos + step] < k` then `pos += step` and `k -= tree[pos]`. The answer is `pos + 1`. This is a binary search without calling `prefix`.
8. Layering: `add` and `sum` are the code pieces `u-bitadd` and `u-bitsum`. The range sum, the search for a running total, and the inversion count are all written by calling them.
--think
- Signal: "point update, range sum" repeated up to 10^5 or 10^6 times. Counting inversions, counting how many earlier items are smaller, "how many numbers are below x so far". Anything that is a running count over a value range.
- Decide first what the index is. Often you must compress values to ranks `1..m` first, because a Fenwick tree needs small positive indexes.
- Neighbouring techniques: a segment tree does everything a Fenwick tree does and also range min, max and range updates, but is longer to write. A Fenwick tree is shorter and faster, so use it when the operation has an inverse (sum, xor, count), because `range = prefix(r) - prefix(l - 1)` needs subtraction. Range min cannot be done this way.
- Range update plus point query: store differences, so `add(l, d)` and `add(r + 1, -d)`, and the item at `i` is `prefix(i)`.
- Pitfalls: index 0 (an infinite loop, since `0 & -0 = 0`), using 0-based positions by mistake, `int` overflow in sums (use `long`), and forgetting that `tree` has size `n + 1`.
--cost
`add` and `prefix` are O(log n). Build O(n). Space O(n).
--java
static class Fenwick {
    int n; long[] bit;                              // bit[1..n] holds the block sums
    Fenwick(int n) { this.n = n; bit = new long[n + 1]; }

    // add(i, v) and sum(i) are the pieces u-bitadd and u-bitsum (0-based: sum(i) = a[0] + ... + a[i])

    long range(int l, int r) { return sum(r) - sum(l - 1); }      // sum(-1) is 0

    int lowerBound(long k) {                        // first index i with sum(i) >= k (items >= 0), n if none
        int pos = 0;
        for (int step = Integer.highestOneBit(n); step > 0; step >>= 1)
            if (pos + step <= n && bit[pos + step] < k) { pos += step; k -= bit[pos]; }
        return pos;
    }

    // number of pairs i < j with a[i] > a[j], for values in 0..maxVal-1
    static long inversions(int[] a, int maxVal) {
        Fenwick f = new Fenwick(maxVal);
        long inv = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < a.length; i++) {
            inv += i - f.sum(a[i]);                 // earlier items minus those <= a[i]
            f.add(a[i], 1);
        }
        return inv;
    }
}
--py
n, bit = 0, []                                      # bit[1..n] holds the block sums

def init(size):
    global n, bit
    n, bit = size, [0] * (size + 1)

# add(i, v) and total(i) are the pieces u-bitadd and u-bitsum (0-based: total(i) = a[0] + ... + a[i])

def range_sum(l, r):
    return total(r) - total(l - 1)                  # total(-1) is 0

def lower_bound(k):
    """First index i with total(i) >= k (items >= 0), n if none."""
    pos, step = 0, 1 << n.bit_length()
    while step:
        if pos + step <= n and bit[pos + step] < k:
            pos += step
            k -= bit[pos]
        step >>= 1
    return pos

def inversions(a, max_val):
    """Number of pairs i < j with a[i] > a[j], for values in 0..max_val-1."""
    init(max_val)
    inv = 0
    for i, x in enumerate(a):
        inv += i - total(x)                         # earlier items minus those <= x
        add(x, 1)
    return inv
--cpp
struct Fenwick {
    int n; vector<long long> bit;                   // bit[1..n] holds the block sums
    Fenwick(int n) : n(n), bit(n + 1, 0) {}

    // add(i, v) and sum(i) are the pieces u-bitadd and u-bitsum (0-based: sum(i) = a[0] + ... + a[i])

    long long range(int l, int r) { return sum(r) - sum(l - 1); }   // sum(-1) is 0

    int lowerBound(long long k) {                   // first index i with sum(i) >= k (items >= 0), n if none
        int pos = 0;
        for (int step = 1 << (31 - __builtin_clz(n)); step > 0; step >>= 1)
            if (pos + step <= n && bit[pos + step] < k) { pos += step; k -= bit[pos]; }
        return pos;
    }
};

// number of pairs i < j with a[i] > a[j], for values in 0..maxVal-1
long long inversions(const vector<int>& a, int maxVal) {
    Fenwick f(maxVal);
    long long inv = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < (int)a.size(); i++) {
        inv += i - f.sum(a[i]);                     // earlier items minus those <= a[i]
        f.add(a[i], 1);
    }
    return inv;
}
--uses
u-bitadd
u-bitsum

### fenwick2d
--idea
A 2D Fenwick tree does for a grid what the ordinary Fenwick tree does for a row: it answers "what is the sum of the rectangle from the top-left corner to cell `(r, c)`?" while single cells change, in about log(rows) x log(cols) steps. Any rectangle inside the grid is then four such corner sums combined.
It is a Fenwick tree whose every slot is itself a Fenwick tree. The outer tree splits the rows into blocks by the lowest-set-bit rule, and each block holds an inner Fenwick tree over the columns. You walk the outer index with the usual jumps, and for every outer slot you touch you walk the inner index with the usual jumps too.
--build
1. Use 1-based rows and columns. Make `t` of size `(R + 1) x (C + 1)`, all zero.
2. `add(r, c, delta)`: for `i = r; i <= R; i += i & -i`, and inside that for `j = c; j <= C; j += j & -j`, do `t[i][j] += delta`.
3. `prefix(r, c)`: for `i = r; i > 0; i -= i & -i`, and inside for `j = c; j > 0; j -= j & -j`, add `t[i][j]`. This is the sum of the rectangle from `(1, 1)` to `(r, c)`.
4. Rectangle sum with corners `(r1, c1)` and `(r2, c2)`: `prefix(r2, c2) - prefix(r1 - 1, c2) - prefix(r2, c1 - 1) + prefix(r1 - 1, c1 - 1)`. This is inclusion and exclusion: subtract the two strips above and to the left, and add back the overlap that was subtracted twice.
5. To set a cell to a new value, `add` the difference between new and old (keep a plain grid of current values).
6. Build the whole thing by calling `add` for each cell, O(R x C x log R x log C), or use the O(R x C) parent-propagation trick in both dimensions.
--think
- Signal: a grid with point updates and rectangle sum queries, where a plain 2D prefix-sum table (which has no updates) is not enough. "Count of points inside a rectangle" after coordinate compression.
- Decide first the grid size. Memory is `R x C`, so this fits when both are up to about 2000 to 5000. If points are sparse in a huge range, compress coordinates, or use offline sorting with a 1D Fenwick tree instead.
- Neighbouring techniques: 2D prefix sums for static grids. A 2D segment tree for min or max. Offline "sweep line plus 1D Fenwick" for counting points in rectangles when all queries are known in advance, which uses much less memory.
- Same restriction as the 1D tree: the operation needs an inverse (sum, xor, counts) because of the subtraction in step 4.
- Pitfalls: 0-based indexes (a zero index loops forever), forgetting the add-back term in the rectangle formula, `int` overflow in sums, and mixing up `R` and `C` bounds in the two loops.
--cost
Update and prefix query O(log R x log C). Space O(R x C).
--java
static class Fenwick2D {
    int R, C; long[][] t;
    Fenwick2D(int R, int C) { this.R = R; this.C = C; t = new long[R + 1][C + 1]; }

    void add(int r, int c, long delta) {
        for (int i = r; i <= R; i += i & -i)
            for (int j = c; j <= C; j += j & -j) t[i][j] += delta;
    }
    long prefix(int r, int c) {                         // sum of rectangle (1,1)..(r,c)
        long s = 0;
        for (int i = r; i > 0; i -= i & -i)
            for (int j = c; j > 0; j -= j & -j) s += t[i][j];
        return s;
    }
    long rect(int r1, int c1, int r2, int c2) {         // inclusion and exclusion
        return prefix(r2, c2) - prefix(r1 - 1, c2) - prefix(r2, c1 - 1) + prefix(r1 - 1, c1 - 1);
    }
}
--py
class Fenwick2D:
    def __init__(self, R, C):
        self.R, self.C = R, C
        self.t = [[0] * (C + 1) for _ in range(R + 1)]

    def add(self, r, c, delta):
        i = r
        while i <= self.R:
            j = c
            while j <= self.C:
                self.t[i][j] += delta
                j += j & -j
            i += i & -i

    def prefix(self, r, c):                       # sum of rectangle (1,1)..(r,c)
        s, i = 0, r
        while i > 0:
            j = c
            while j > 0:
                s += self.t[i][j]
                j -= j & -j
            i -= i & -i
        return s

    def rect(self, r1, c1, r2, c2):               # inclusion and exclusion
        return (self.prefix(r2, c2) - self.prefix(r1 - 1, c2)
                - self.prefix(r2, c1 - 1) + self.prefix(r1 - 1, c1 - 1))
--cpp
struct Fenwick2D {
    int R, C; vector<vector<long long>> t;
    Fenwick2D(int R, int C) : R(R), C(C), t(R + 1, vector<long long>(C + 1, 0)) {}

    void add(int r, int c, long long delta) {
        for (int i = r; i <= R; i += i & -i)
            for (int j = c; j <= C; j += j & -j) t[i][j] += delta;
    }
    long long prefix(int r, int c) const {              // sum of rectangle (1,1)..(r,c)
        long long s = 0;
        for (int i = r; i > 0; i -= i & -i)
            for (int j = c; j > 0; j -= j & -j) s += t[i][j];
        return s;
    }
    long long rect(int r1, int c1, int r2, int c2) const {   // inclusion and exclusion
        return prefix(r2, c2) - prefix(r1 - 1, c2) - prefix(r2, c1 - 1) + prefix(r1 - 1, c1 - 1);
    }
};

### sparse
--idea
A sparse table answers "what is the smallest item between positions `l` and `r`?" in a single step, after a one-time preparation. It never changes: it is for data that stays fixed. The idea is to pre-compute the answer for every range whose length is a power of two (1, 2, 4, 8, ...), starting at every position.
Any range can then be covered by two such ranges that may overlap: one starting at `l`, one ending at `r`, both with the largest power-of-two length that fits. For minimum, overlap does not matter (the smallest item is still the smallest if you look at it twice), so the answer is the smaller of the two stored values.
--build
1. Let `K = floor(log2(n)) + 1`. Make a table `t[k][i]`, meaning "the minimum of the `2^k` items starting at `i`".
2. Level 0 is the array itself: `t[0][i] = a[i]`.
3. Each higher level combines two halves of the level below: `t[k][i] = min(t[k-1][i], t[k-1][i + 2^(k-1)])`. Fill only for `i + 2^k <= n`.
4. To answer `min(l..r)` (inclusive): let `len = r - l + 1` and `k = floor(log2(len))`. The answer is `min(t[k][l], t[k][r - 2^k + 1])`. The two ranges together cover `l..r`.
5. Get `floor(log2(len))` fast with a bit trick (`31 - numberOfLeadingZeros(len)`) or a precomputed table.
6. This overlap trick works for operations where applying an item twice changes nothing (min, max, gcd, and, or). For sum it does not work directly: see the disjoint sparse table.
7. Layering: the number of levels and the level to use for a query both come from `floorLog2` (code piece `u-log2`).
--think
- Signal: many range min, max or gcd queries on an array that does not change. "q up to 10^6 queries", "no updates".
- Decide first the operation: it must be idempotent (doing it twice equals doing it once) for the overlapping trick, or associative if you split the range into non-overlapping pieces (then queries take O(log n)).
- Neighbouring techniques: a segment tree handles updates but each query costs O(log n). A Fenwick tree cannot do min. A monotonic deque handles fixed-width windows. Sparse table plus Euler tour gives lowest common ancestor in O(1) per query.
- Use it when queries massively outnumber builds: build is O(n log n) memory and time, queries are O(1).
- Pitfalls: building a row beyond the valid `i`, mixing inclusive and exclusive `r`, computing `log2` with floating point (rounding errors near powers of two), and huge memory (`n = 10^6` gives 20 million entries).
--cost
Build O(n log n) time and space. Query O(1).
--java
static class SparseMin {
    int[][] t;
    SparseMin(int[] a) {
        int n = a.length, K = floorLog2(n) + 1;                       // number of levels (piece u-log2)
        t = new int[K][];
        t[0] = a.clone();
        for (int k = 1; k < K; k++) {
            int len = n - (1 << k) + 1;                               // starting points that fit
            t[k] = new int[Math.max(len, 0)];
            for (int i = 0; i < len; i++)
                t[k][i] = Math.min(t[k - 1][i], t[k - 1][i + (1 << (k - 1))]);
        }
    }
    int min(int l, int r) {                                           // inclusive
        int k = floorLog2(r - l + 1);                                 // biggest power of two that fits
        return Math.min(t[k][l], t[k][r - (1 << k) + 1]);             // two overlapping blocks
    }
}
--py
class SparseMin:
    def __init__(self, a):
        n = len(a)
        self.t = [list(a)]
        for k in range(1, floor_log2(n) + 1):                  # number of levels (piece u-log2)
            prev, half = self.t[-1], 1 << (k - 1)
            self.t.append([min(prev[i], prev[i + half]) for i in range(n - (1 << k) + 1)])

    def min(self, l, r):                           # inclusive
        k = floor_log2(r - l + 1)                  # biggest power of two that fits
        return min(self.t[k][l], self.t[k][r - (1 << k) + 1])   # two overlapping blocks
--cpp
struct SparseMin {
    vector<vector<int>> t;
    SparseMin(const vector<int>& a) {
        int n = a.size();
        t.push_back(a);
        for (int k = 1; k <= floorLog2(n); k++) {                  // number of levels (piece u-log2)
            int len = n - (1 << k) + 1;                            // starting points that fit
            t.push_back(vector<int>(len));
            for (int i = 0; i < len; i++)
                t[k][i] = min(t[k - 1][i], t[k - 1][i + (1 << (k - 1))]);
        }
    }
    int query(int l, int r) const {                                // inclusive
        int k = floorLog2(r - l + 1);                              // biggest power of two that fits
        return min(t[k][l], t[k][r - (1 << k) + 1]);               // two overlapping blocks
    }
};
--uses
u-log2

### segment
--idea
A segment tree answers "what is the sum (or min, or max) of positions `l` to `r`?" and also lets you change any single position, both in about log n steps. Picture a tournament bracket: the leaves are the items, each match above stores the result of combining its two children, and the champion at the top is the answer for the whole array.
Any range `l..r` is built from a handful of the bracket's nodes, at most about 2 log n, whose pieces do not overlap and together cover exactly the range. When one item changes, only the matches on the path from its leaf to the top need to be replayed.
--build
1. Let `n` be the number of items. Make an array `t` of size `2n`. The leaves live at `t[n .. 2n-1]` (item `i` is at `t[n + i]`). Node `p` has children `2p` and `2p+1`, and parent `p / 2`. This "bottom-up" layout needs no recursion.
2. Pick the combine rule and its neutral value: for sum it is `+` and 0; for min it is `min` and infinity; for max, `max` and minus infinity.
3. Build: copy the items into the leaves. For `p` from `n - 1` down to 1, set `t[p] = combine(t[2p], t[2p+1])`.
4. `update(i, v)`: set `p = i + n`, `t[p] = v`. Then while `p > 1`: `p /= 2`, `t[p] = combine(t[2p], t[2p+1])`.
5. `query(l, r)` over the half-open range `[l, r)`: set `l += n`, `r += n`. While `l < r`: if `l` is odd, take `t[l]` into the answer and `l++`. If `r` is odd, `r--` and take `t[r]`. Then `l /= 2`, `r /= 2`. Each taken node is a whole subtree that lies inside the range.
6. Invariant: for every internal node `p`, `t[p]` equals the combine of the items under it. Update and build restore that for the changed path, so queries can trust it.
7. For operations where the order matters (like matrix multiplication or string concatenation), keep a left result and a right result separately and combine them in order at the end. Sum, min and max do not care about order.
8. Layering: the constructor calls `buildSeg` (code piece `u-segbuild`), and the tree is read and changed with `query` and `update` (code pieces `u-segq`, `u-segupd`). `MinSegTree` shows the change needed for another operation.
--think
- Signal: "point update and range query" and the operation is not subtractable, or you need min, max, gcd, count of something, "k-th". Also the base for harder ideas: lazy propagation (range updates), persistence, merge sort tree.
- Decide first what each node stores and how two children combine into the parent. Everything else follows. If the combine needs more than one number, store a small struct (for example sum, min and max together, or "longest run" with prefix and suffix runs).
- Neighbouring techniques: a Fenwick tree is shorter when the operation has an inverse. A sparse table is faster when there are no updates. Sqrt decomposition is simpler but slower. If you also need range updates (add to a whole range), you need lazy propagation.
- Descending the tree finds things too: "first position where the prefix sum reaches `k`" walks from the root, going left if the left sum is enough, else subtracting it and going right.
- Pitfalls: mixing half-open and closed ranges, using a wrong neutral value (for min use a large value, not 0), non-power-of-two `n` (the bottom-up layout above works for any `n` for commutative operations, but the top node `t[1]` is then not the whole-array answer), and `int` overflow in sums.
--cost
Build O(n). Update and query O(log n). Space O(n).
--java
static class SegTree {
    int n; int[] t;
    SegTree(int[] a) { n = a.length; t = buildSeg(a); }            // piece u-segbuild fills the tree

    // query(l, r) over [l, r) and update(i, v) are the pieces u-segq and u-segupd
}

// the same tree for the smallest value instead of the sum: only the combine rule and the neutral value change
static class MinSegTree {
    int n; int[] t;
    MinSegTree(int[] a) {
        n = a.length; t = new int[2 * n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) t[n + i] = a[i];
        for (int p = n - 1; p >= 1; p--) t[p] = Math.min(t[2 * p], t[2 * p + 1]);
    }
    void update(int i, int v) {
        for (t[i += n] = v; i > 1; i >>= 1) t[i >> 1] = Math.min(t[i], t[i ^ 1]);
    }
    int query(int l, int r) {                                      // min over [l, r), neutral value is "infinity"
        int res = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
        for (l += n, r += n; l < r; l >>= 1, r >>= 1) {
            if ((l & 1) == 1) res = Math.min(res, t[l++]);
            if ((r & 1) == 1) res = Math.min(res, t[--r]);
        }
        return res;
    }
}
--py
# sum tree: build_seg(a) is the piece u-segbuild; query(l, r) over [l, r) and update(i, v) are
# the pieces u-segq and u-segupd. They share the module-level n and t.
def make_seg(a):
    global n, t
    n, t = len(a), build_seg(a)

class MinSegTree:
    """The same tree for the smallest value instead of the sum: only the combine rule and the neutral value change."""
    def __init__(self, a):
        self.n = len(a)
        self.t = [0] * self.n + list(a)
        for p in range(self.n - 1, 0, -1):
            self.t[p] = min(self.t[2 * p], self.t[2 * p + 1])

    def update(self, i, v):
        i += self.n
        self.t[i] = v
        while i > 1:
            self.t[i >> 1] = min(self.t[i], self.t[i ^ 1])
            i >>= 1

    def query(self, l, r):                         # min over [l, r), neutral value is "infinity"
        res = float('inf')
        l += self.n; r += self.n
        while l < r:
            if l & 1: res = min(res, self.t[l]); l += 1
            if r & 1: r -= 1; res = min(res, self.t[r])
            l >>= 1; r >>= 1
        return res
--cpp
struct SegTree {
    int n; vector<int> t;
    SegTree(const vector<int>& a) : n(a.size()), t(buildSeg(a)) {}   // piece u-segbuild fills the tree

    // query(l, r) over [l, r) and update(i, v) are the pieces u-segq and u-segupd
};

// the same tree for the smallest value instead of the sum: only the combine rule and the neutral value change
struct MinSegTree {
    int n; vector<int> t;
    MinSegTree(const vector<int>& a) : n(a.size()), t(2 * a.size()) {
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) t[n + i] = a[i];
        for (int p = n - 1; p >= 1; p--) t[p] = min(t[2 * p], t[2 * p + 1]);
    }
    void update(int i, int v) {
        for (t[i += n] = v; i > 1; i >>= 1) t[i >> 1] = min(t[i], t[i ^ 1]);
    }
    int query(int l, int r) const {                  // min over [l, r), neutral value is "infinity"
        int res = INT_MAX;
        for (l += n, r += n; l < r; l >>= 1, r >>= 1) {
            if (l & 1) res = min(res, t[l++]);
            if (r & 1) res = min(res, t[--r]);
        }
        return res;
    }
};
--uses
u-segbuild
u-segq
u-segupd

### wdsu
--idea
Weighted union-find is union-find that also remembers how far apart two items are. Ordinary union-find says "A and B are in the same group". The weighted version says "B is exactly 5 more than A" (a height difference, a price ratio turned into a difference of logarithms, a parity of 0 or 1, a distance along a line).
Every item stores `diff[x]`: its value minus its parent's value. Following parents up to the root and adding the diffs gives the item's value relative to the root. Two items in the same group differ by the difference of their relative values, so any pair can be answered without storing all pairs.
--build
1. Arrays `parent[i] = i`, `diff[i] = 0`, `size[i] = 1`. Meaning: `diff[x] = value(x) - value(parent[x])`. A root has `diff = 0`.
2. `find(x)` returns the root and makes `diff[x]` relative to the root. Walk up and collect the path. Then go through the path from the node nearest the root outward: for each node `v` with old parent `p`, if `p` is not the root do `diff[v] += diff[p]` (the parent `p` has already been fixed to be relative to the root), then set `parent[v] = root`.
3. `union(a, b, d)` records the fact `value(b) - value(a) = d`. Find roots `ra = find(a)`, `rb = find(b)`. Now `diff[a]` is `value(a) - value(ra)`, and likewise for `b`.
4. If `ra == rb`: no merge is needed. The fact is consistent exactly when `diff[b] - diff[a] == d`. Return that boolean (a contradiction means the input facts conflict).
5. If different, hang the smaller group under the bigger one. To hang `rb` under `ra`: `parent[rb] = ra` and `diff[rb] = value(rb) - value(ra) = d + diff[a] - diff[b]`. If instead `ra` must go under `rb`, swap the roles by swapping `a` and `b` and negating `d`.
6. `query(a, b)`: if `find(a) == find(b)`, the answer is `diff[b] - diff[a]`. Otherwise the distance is unknown.
7. For parity (odd or even difference) use the same code with `diff` stored modulo 2 (xor).
--think
- Signal: "x - y = d" facts given one at a time with queries like "what is z - w?" or "are these facts consistent?". Also weights on edges with a "same or different team" style statement, ratios of currencies, and "are there contradictions".
- Decide first what the weight means and which operation combines weights along a path: addition for differences, multiplication for ratios, xor for parity. It must be associative and have inverses (so you can subtract).
- Neighbouring techniques: plain union-find if you only need "same group". A graph search from the query points is O(n) each, which is too slow for many queries. A shortest-path method is for inequalities, not equalities.
- The direction of the formula is the most common bug. Write down: `diff[x] = value(x) - value(parent[x])` and recompute step 5 from scratch for yourself.
- Pitfalls: not updating `diff` during path compression, comparing `diff` values before calling `find` on both items, an infinite recursion if you write `find` recursively on a deep tree (use the loop form), and ignoring the case `ra == rb` (that is where contradictions are found).
--cost
Almost O(1) per operation (inverse Ackermann). Space O(n).
--java
static class WDSU {
    int[] parent, size; long[] diff;       // diff[x] = value(x) - value(parent[x])
    WDSU(int n) {
        parent = new int[n]; size = new int[n]; diff = new long[n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { parent[i] = i; size[i] = 1; }
    }

    int find(int x) {
        int root = x;
        while (parent[root] != root) root = parent[root];
        // fix the path from the node nearest the root outward: record the path first
        java.util.ArrayList<Integer> path = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
        for (int v = x; parent[v] != v; v = parent[v]) path.add(v);
        for (int k = path.size() - 1; k >= 0; k--) {
            int v = path.get(k), p = parent[v];
            if (p != root) diff[v] += diff[p];     // p is already relative to the root
            parent[v] = root;
        }
        return root;
    }

    // record value(b) - value(a) = d. Returns false if it contradicts earlier facts.
    boolean union(int a, int b, long d) {
        int ra = find(a), rb = find(b);
        if (ra == rb) return diff[b] - diff[a] == d;
        if (size[ra] < size[rb]) { int t = a; a = b; b = t; t = ra; ra = rb; rb = t; d = -d; }
        parent[rb] = ra;
        diff[rb] = d + diff[a] - diff[b];
        size[ra] += size[rb];
        return true;
    }

    // value(b) - value(a), or null if not connected
    Long query(int a, int b) {
        if (find(a) != find(b)) return null;
        return diff[b] - diff[a];
    }
}
--py
class WDSU:
    def __init__(self, n):
        self.parent = list(range(n))
        self.size = [1] * n
        self.diff = [0] * n               # diff[x] = value(x) - value(parent[x])

    def find(self, x):
        path = []
        while self.parent[x] != x:
            path.append(x)
            x = self.parent[x]
        root = x
        for v in reversed(path):          # from the node nearest the root outward
            p = self.parent[v]
            if p != root:
                self.diff[v] += self.diff[p]   # p is already relative to the root
            self.parent[v] = root
        return root

    def union(self, a, b, d):
        """Record value(b) - value(a) = d. Returns False if it contradicts earlier facts."""
        ra, rb = self.find(a), self.find(b)
        if ra == rb:
            return self.diff[b] - self.diff[a] == d
        if self.size[ra] < self.size[rb]:
            a, b, ra, rb, d = b, a, rb, ra, -d
        self.parent[rb] = ra
        self.diff[rb] = d + self.diff[a] - self.diff[b]
        self.size[ra] += self.size[rb]
        return True

    def query(self, a, b):
        """value(b) - value(a), or None if not connected."""
        if self.find(a) != self.find(b):
            return None
        return self.diff[b] - self.diff[a]
--cpp
struct WDSU {
    vector<int> parent, sz; vector<long long> diff;   // diff[x] = value(x) - value(parent[x])
    WDSU(int n) : parent(n), sz(n, 1), diff(n, 0) { iota(parent.begin(), parent.end(), 0); }

    int find(int x) {
        vector<int> path;
        while (parent[x] != x) { path.push_back(x); x = parent[x]; }
        int root = x;
        for (int k = (int)path.size() - 1; k >= 0; k--) {   // from the node nearest the root outward
            int v = path[k], p = parent[v];
            if (p != root) diff[v] += diff[p];               // p is already relative to the root
            parent[v] = root;
        }
        return root;
    }

    // record value(b) - value(a) = d. Returns false if it contradicts earlier facts.
    bool unite(int a, int b, long long d) {
        int ra = find(a), rb = find(b);
        if (ra == rb) return diff[b] - diff[a] == d;
        if (sz[ra] < sz[rb]) { swap(a, b); swap(ra, rb); d = -d; }
        parent[rb] = ra;
        diff[rb] = d + diff[a] - diff[b];
        sz[ra] += sz[rb];
        return true;
    }

    // value(b) - value(a); returns false if not connected
    bool query(int a, int b, long long& out) {
        if (find(a) != find(b)) return false;
        out = diff[b] - diff[a];
        return true;
    }
};

### avl
--idea
An AVL tree is a binary search tree that repairs itself so it never gets lopsided. It keeps one rule: at every node, the heights of the left and right subtrees differ by at most 1. That rule forces the tree to stay short, with height at most about 1.44 log n, so every search, insert and delete is O(log n) even on sorted input.
When an insert or delete breaks the rule at some node, the tree fixes it with a rotation: a small local rearrangement of two or three nodes that lifts the taller side up one level while keeping the left-to-right order of keys intact. Only the nodes on the path from the changed spot back to the root can be out of balance, so the repair is cheap.
--build
1. Each node stores `key`, `left`, `right` and `height` (a leaf has height 1, an empty tree has height 0).
2. Define `balance(t) = height(t.left) - height(t.right)`. The invariant is `-1 <= balance <= 1` at every node.
3. Insert like a normal search tree, recursively. On the way back up, at each node recompute `height = 1 + max(height(left), height(right))` and call `rebalance`.
4. Case "left-left" (`balance > 1` and the left child leans left or is even): do one right rotation at the node.
5. Case "left-right" (`balance > 1` and the left child leans right): rotate the left child left first, then rotate the node right.
6. The two right-leaning cases are mirror images: "right-right" is one left rotation, "right-left" is a right rotation of the right child and then a left rotation of the node.
7. Right rotation at `y` with left child `x`: `y.left = x.right`, `x.right = y`, update heights of `y` then `x`, return `x` as the new subtree root. Left rotation is the mirror.
8. Delete works the same way as in an ordinary search tree (replace by the successor when there are two children), and you call `rebalance` on every node on the way back up. Deleting can need several rotations on one path, inserting at most one.
--think
- Signal: you need an ordered set that is guaranteed to stay fast, or the problem asks you to implement a self-balancing tree. In contests you rarely write one by hand: use `TreeMap`, `std::set` or a sorted list.
- Decide first how heights are stored (in each node) and always update height before using it in the parent. Most bugs are a stale height.
- Neighbouring techniques: a red-black tree balances less strictly, so it does fewer rotations on updates but a slightly taller tree. AVL searches faster, red-black updates faster. A treap gets the same expected speed with much less code by using random priorities. A B-tree is for disks.
- Think of the four cases as pictures of three nodes in a zig-zag or a straight line. The straight line needs one rotation, the zig-zag needs two.
- Pitfalls: updating the height of the new subtree root before the old root, returning the old root instead of the new one from a rotation, and forgetting that the tree is reached by returning the subtree root from each recursive call.
--cost
Search, insert, delete O(log n) worst case. Space O(n). At most one rotation (single or double) per insert.
--java
static class Node {
    int key, h = 1; Node left, right;
    Node(int k) { key = k; }
}
static int ht(Node t) { return t == null ? 0 : t.h; }
static void fix(Node t) { t.h = 1 + Math.max(ht(t.left), ht(t.right)); }
static int bal(Node t) { return ht(t.left) - ht(t.right); }

static Node rotateRight(Node y) {
    Node x = y.left;
    y.left = x.right; x.right = y;
    fix(y); fix(x);                      // child first, then the new root
    return x;
}
static Node rotateLeft(Node x) {
    Node y = x.right;
    x.right = y.left; y.left = x;
    fix(x); fix(y);
    return y;
}
static Node rebalance(Node t) {
    fix(t);
    if (bal(t) > 1) {
        if (bal(t.left) < 0) t.left = rotateLeft(t.left);     // left-right case
        return rotateRight(t);
    }
    if (bal(t) < -1) {
        if (bal(t.right) > 0) t.right = rotateRight(t.right); // right-left case
        return rotateLeft(t);
    }
    return t;
}
static Node insert(Node t, int k) {
    if (t == null) return new Node(k);
    if (k < t.key) t.left = insert(t.left, k);
    else if (k > t.key) t.right = insert(t.right, k);
    else return t;                       // duplicates ignored
    return rebalance(t);
}
--py
class Node:
    def __init__(self, key):
        self.key, self.h = key, 1
        self.left = self.right = None

def ht(t): return t.h if t else 0
def fix(t): t.h = 1 + max(ht(t.left), ht(t.right))
def bal(t): return ht(t.left) - ht(t.right)

def rotate_right(y):
    x = y.left
    y.left, x.right = x.right, y
    fix(y); fix(x)                       # child first, then the new root
    return x

def rotate_left(x):
    y = x.right
    x.right, y.left = y.left, x
    fix(x); fix(y)
    return y

def rebalance(t):
    fix(t)
    if bal(t) > 1:
        if bal(t.left) < 0: t.left = rotate_left(t.left)        # left-right case
        return rotate_right(t)
    if bal(t) < -1:
        if bal(t.right) > 0: t.right = rotate_right(t.right)    # right-left case
        return rotate_left(t)
    return t

def insert(t, k):
    if t is None: return Node(k)
    if k < t.key: t.left = insert(t.left, k)
    elif k > t.key: t.right = insert(t.right, k)
    else: return t                       # duplicates ignored
    return rebalance(t)
--cpp
struct Node {
    int key, h = 1; Node *left = nullptr, *right = nullptr;
    Node(int k) : key(k) {}
};
int ht(Node* t) { return t ? t->h : 0; }
void fix(Node* t) { t->h = 1 + max(ht(t->left), ht(t->right)); }
int bal(Node* t) { return ht(t->left) - ht(t->right); }

Node* rotateRight(Node* y) {
    Node* x = y->left;
    y->left = x->right; x->right = y;
    fix(y); fix(x);                      // child first, then the new root
    return x;
}
Node* rotateLeft(Node* x) {
    Node* y = x->right;
    x->right = y->left; y->left = x;
    fix(x); fix(y);
    return y;
}
Node* rebalance(Node* t) {
    fix(t);
    if (bal(t) > 1) {
        if (bal(t->left) < 0) t->left = rotateLeft(t->left);        // left-right case
        return rotateRight(t);
    }
    if (bal(t) < -1) {
        if (bal(t->right) > 0) t->right = rotateRight(t->right);    // right-left case
        return rotateLeft(t);
    }
    return t;
}
Node* insert(Node* t, int k) {
    if (!t) return new Node(k);
    if (k < t->key) t->left = insert(t->left, k);
    else if (k > t->key) t->right = insert(t->right, k);
    else return t;                       // duplicates ignored
    return rebalance(t);
}

### rbt
--idea
A red-black tree is a binary search tree whose nodes are coloured red or black, with colour rules that stop the tree from becoming lopsided. The rules guarantee that the longest path from the root to a leaf is never more than twice the shortest, so the height is at most about 2 log n and every operation is O(log n). It is the structure behind `std::map`, `std::set`, Java `TreeMap`.
Colours are a cheap way to store "how unbalanced is this?". A red link means "this node is glued to its parent, treat them as one fat node". Black links count as real levels. The rules say every path has the same number of black links, so after gluing, all leaves are at the same depth, like a 2-3 tree. Inserting means adding a red node, then fixing violations with rotations and colour flips as you walk back up.
--build
1. This lesson builds the left-leaning red-black tree (LLRB), a simpler variant with the same height guarantee. Each node stores `key`, `left`, `right` and `red` (true or false). A missing child counts as black.
2. Rules: (a) the root is black; (b) no node has two red links in a row (a red node never has a red child); (c) every path from a node down to a missing child passes the same number of black nodes; (d) in the left-leaning form, a red link is only ever a left child.
3. Insert like a normal search tree, recursively, and the new node is red. A new node is always attached by a red link.
4. On the way back up from the recursion, repair this node in exactly this order using the three local fixes below:
5. Fix 1: if the right child is red and the left child is not, rotate left at this node. (This removes a right-leaning red link.)
6. Fix 2: if the left child is red and the left-left grandchild is also red, rotate right at this node. (Two reds in a row on the left.)
7. Fix 3: if both children are red, flip colours: make both children black and this node red. (The red link moves up to the parent, like a 4-node splitting.)
8. A rotation moves a child above its parent and passes the colour with it: `rotateLeft(h)`: `x = h.right; h.right = x.left; x.left = h; x.red = h.red; h.red = true; return x`. `rotateRight` is the mirror.
9. After the recursion finishes, set the root black.
10. Delete is considerably longer (it pushes red links down the path before removing). In practice you use the library version.
--think
- Signal: you need guaranteed O(log n) ordered operations. You almost never write one in a contest, but "why is `std::map` O(log n) worst case" or "implement a balanced tree" asks for exactly this.
- Decide first which rules to keep in your head: "no red-red" and "same black count on every path". Every fix exists to restore one of them.
- Neighbouring techniques: AVL is stricter (faster lookups, more rotations). A treap or skip list gets the same expected bounds with simpler code. A B-tree is for disk pages. The full red-black tree (not left-leaning) allows red links on either side and needs fewer rotations for deletion.
- Why the colour trick gives balance: with rule (c), compress every red node into its black parent and you get a perfectly level tree, and rule (b) makes at most every second link red, so the real height is at most double.
- Pitfalls: applying the three fixes in the wrong order, forgetting to blacken the root after each insert, treating a null child as red, and mixing up which colour is stored on the rotated nodes.
--cost
Search and insert O(log n) worst case, height at most about 2 log2 n. Space O(n) plus one bit per node.
--java
static class Node {
    int key; Node left, right; boolean red;
    Node(int k) { key = k; red = true; }          // new nodes are red
}
static boolean isRed(Node t) { return t != null && t.red; }

static Node rotateLeft(Node h) {
    Node x = h.right;
    h.right = x.left; x.left = h;
    x.red = h.red; h.red = true;                  // x takes h's colour, h becomes red
    return x;
}
static Node rotateRight(Node h) {
    Node x = h.left;
    h.left = x.right; x.right = h;
    x.red = h.red; h.red = true;
    return x;
}
static void flip(Node h) { h.red = true; h.left.red = false; h.right.red = false; }
