static Node insert(Node h, int k) {
    if (h == null) return new Node(k);
    if (k < h.key) h.left = insert(h.left, k);
    else if (k > h.key) h.right = insert(h.right, k);
    if (isRed(h.right) && !isRed(h.left)) h = rotateLeft(h);        // fix 1
    if (isRed(h.left) && isRed(h.left.left)) h = rotateRight(h);    // fix 2
    if (isRed(h.left) && isRed(h.right)) flip(h);                   // fix 3
    return h;
}
static Node insertRoot(Node root, int k) {
    root = insert(root, k);
    root.red = false;                                               // the root is always black
    return root;
}
--py
class Node:
    def __init__(self, key):
        self.key = key
        self.left = self.right = None
        self.red = True                          # new nodes are red

def is_red(t): return t is not None and t.red

def rotate_left(h):
    x = h.right
    h.right, x.left = x.left, h
    x.red, h.red = h.red, True                   # x takes h's colour, h becomes red
    return x

def rotate_right(h):
    x = h.left
    h.left, x.right = x.right, h
    x.red, h.red = h.red, True
    return x

def flip(h):
    h.red = True
    h.left.red = h.right.red = False

def _insert(h, k):
    if h is None: return Node(k)
    if k < h.key: h.left = _insert(h.left, k)
    elif k > h.key: h.right = _insert(h.right, k)
    if is_red(h.right) and not is_red(h.left): h = rotate_left(h)         # fix 1
    if is_red(h.left) and is_red(h.left.left): h = rotate_right(h)        # fix 2
    if is_red(h.left) and is_red(h.right): flip(h)                        # fix 3
    return h

def insert(root, k):
    root = _insert(root, k)
    root.red = False                                                      # the root is always black
    return root
--cpp
struct Node {
    int key; Node *left = nullptr, *right = nullptr; bool red = true;   // new nodes are red
    Node(int k) : key(k) {}
};
bool isRed(Node* t) { return t && t->red; }

Node* rotateLeft(Node* h) {
    Node* x = h->right;
    h->right = x->left; x->left = h;
    x->red = h->red; h->red = true;                // x takes h's colour, h becomes red
    return x;
}
Node* rotateRight(Node* h) {
    Node* x = h->left;
    h->left = x->right; x->right = h;
    x->red = h->red; h->red = true;
    return x;
}
void flip(Node* h) { h->red = true; h->left->red = false; h->right->red = false; }

Node* insertRec(Node* h, int k) {
    if (!h) return new Node(k);
    if (k < h->key) h->left = insertRec(h->left, k);
    else if (k > h->key) h->right = insertRec(h->right, k);
    if (isRed(h->right) && !isRed(h->left)) h = rotateLeft(h);        // fix 1
    if (isRed(h->left) && isRed(h->left->left)) h = rotateRight(h);   // fix 2
    if (isRed(h->left) && isRed(h->right)) flip(h);                   // fix 3
    return h;
}
Node* insertKey(Node* root, int k) {
    root = insertRec(root, k);
    root->red = false;                                                // the root is always black
    return root;
}

### btree
--idea
A B-tree is a search tree where each node holds many keys, not just one, and has many children. Reading from a disk (or any slow storage) is expensive per trip but cheap per byte, so you want each trip to bring in as much useful data as possible. A node with hundreds of keys narrows the search by a factor of hundreds in one read, so a tree of a billion keys is only about 4 levels deep. Databases and file systems use B-trees (or the B+ tree variant) for this reason.
A node with `m` keys has `m + 1` children: the keys split the possible values into `m + 1` ranges, and child `i` holds exactly the values between key `i - 1` and key `i`. All leaves sit at the same depth. The tree stays balanced by splitting nodes that get too full, and the split pushes one key up, so the tree grows at the root, not at the leaves.
--build
1. Pick the minimum degree `t` (at least 2). Every node except the root holds between `t - 1` and `2t - 1` keys, so a non-leaf node has between `t` and `2t` children. A node is "full" when it has `2t - 1` keys.
2. A node stores a sorted `keys` list, a `children` list (empty for a leaf), and a `leaf` flag.
3. `search(x, k)`: find the first index `i` with `keys[i] >= k` (binary or linear). If `keys[i] == k`, found. If the node is a leaf, not found. Else repeat in `children[i]`.
4. `splitChild(x, i)`: child `y = x.children[i]` is full. Create a new node `z`. Move the upper `t - 1` keys of `y` (indexes `t .. 2t-2`) into `z`, and if `y` is not a leaf, its upper `t` children too. The middle key `y.keys[t - 1]` goes up into `x.keys` at position `i`, and `z` becomes `x.children[i + 1]`. Now `y` and `z` each hold `t - 1` keys.
5. `insertNonFull(x, k)`: if `x` is a leaf, insert `k` into `keys` at its sorted position. Otherwise find the child index `i` where `k` belongs. If that child is full, `splitChild(x, i)` first, and if `k` is greater than the key that came up, use `i + 1`. Then recurse into the child.
6. Insert at the root: if the root is full, make a new empty root whose only child is the old root, split that child, and only then call `insertNonFull`. This is the only way the tree gets taller.
7. Invariant: no node ever exceeds `2t - 1` keys, because we split any full node on the way down, before we enter it, so a split never needs to propagate back up.
8. Deletion borrows from or merges with a sibling when a node would drop below `t - 1` keys. It is long, so only the idea is given here: always make sure the node you descend into has at least `t` keys.
--think
- Signal: data lives on disk, a database index, a file system, "minimise the number of block reads". You rarely implement one in a coding task, but the question "why do databases use B-trees instead of binary trees" appears often.
- Decide first the node size from the block size. If a disk page is 4 KB and a key plus pointer is 16 bytes, `2t` is about 250.
- Neighbouring techniques: B+ trees keep all values at the leaves and link the leaves in a list, which makes range scans fast, and are the usual database choice. In memory, a balanced binary tree is fine. A skip list or sorted array can replace it for small data.
- Why this stays balanced without rotations: nodes split into two half-full siblings, and only splitting the root adds a level, so every leaf is always at the same depth.
- Pitfalls: choosing the wrong middle key when splitting (index `t - 1`), forgetting to move the children when the node is not a leaf, off-by-one on `i` after the split, and treating `t` as the maximum instead of the minimum degree.
--cost
Search and insert O(log n) key comparisons and O(log_t n) node reads. Space O(n).
--java
static class BNode {
    java.util.ArrayList<Integer> keys = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
    java.util.ArrayList<BNode> children = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
    boolean leaf = true;
}

static class BTree {
    final int t; BNode root = new BNode();
    BTree(int t) { this.t = t; }

    boolean contains(BNode x, int k) {
        int i = 0;
        while (i < x.keys.size() && k > x.keys.get(i)) i++;
        if (i < x.keys.size() && x.keys.get(i) == k) return true;
        return !x.leaf && contains(x.children.get(i), k);
    }

    void splitChild(BNode x, int i) {
        BNode y = x.children.get(i), z = new BNode();
        z.leaf = y.leaf;
        int mid = y.keys.get(t - 1);
        z.keys.addAll(y.keys.subList(t, y.keys.size()));              // upper t-1 keys
        y.keys.subList(t - 1, y.keys.size()).clear();                 // y keeps the lower t-1
        if (!y.leaf) {
            z.children.addAll(y.children.subList(t, y.children.size()));
            y.children.subList(t, y.children.size()).clear();
        }
        x.keys.add(i, mid);                                           // middle key moves up
        x.children.add(i + 1, z);
    }

    void insertNonFull(BNode x, int k) {
        int i = 0;
        while (i < x.keys.size() && k > x.keys.get(i)) i++;
        if (x.leaf) { x.keys.add(i, k); return; }
        if (x.children.get(i).keys.size() == 2 * t - 1) {
            splitChild(x, i);
            if (k > x.keys.get(i)) i++;
        }
        insertNonFull(x.children.get(i), k);
    }

    void insert(int k) {
        if (root.keys.size() == 2 * t - 1) {                          // full root: the tree grows here
            BNode s = new BNode(); s.leaf = false; s.children.add(root);
            root = s; splitChild(s, 0);
        }
        insertNonFull(root, k);
    }
}
--py
class BNode:
    def __init__(self, leaf=True):
        self.keys, self.children, self.leaf = [], [], leaf

class BTree:
    def __init__(self, t=2):
        self.t = t
        self.root = BNode()

    def contains(self, x, k):
        i = 0
        while i < len(x.keys) and k > x.keys[i]:
            i += 1
        if i < len(x.keys) and x.keys[i] == k:
            return True
        return (not x.leaf) and self.contains(x.children[i], k)

    def split_child(self, x, i):
        t, y = self.t, x.children[i]
        z = BNode(y.leaf)
        mid = y.keys[t - 1]
        z.keys = y.keys[t:]                       # upper t-1 keys
        y.keys = y.keys[:t - 1]                   # y keeps the lower t-1
        if not y.leaf:
            z.children = y.children[t:]
            y.children = y.children[:t]
        x.keys.insert(i, mid)                     # middle key moves up
        x.children.insert(i + 1, z)

    def insert_non_full(self, x, k):
        i = 0
        while i < len(x.keys) and k > x.keys[i]:
            i += 1
        if x.leaf:
            x.keys.insert(i, k)
            return
        if len(x.children[i].keys) == 2 * self.t - 1:
            self.split_child(x, i)
            if k > x.keys[i]:
                i += 1
        self.insert_non_full(x.children[i], k)

    def insert(self, k):
        if len(self.root.keys) == 2 * self.t - 1:     # full root: the tree grows here
            s = BNode(False)
            s.children.append(self.root)
            self.root = s
            self.split_child(s, 0)
        self.insert_non_full(self.root, k)
--cpp
struct BNode {
    vector<int> keys; vector<BNode*> children; bool leaf = true;
};

struct BTree {
    int t; BNode* root = new BNode();
    BTree(int t = 2) : t(t) {}

    bool contains(BNode* x, int k) const {
        int i = 0;
        while (i < (int)x->keys.size() && k > x->keys[i]) i++;
        if (i < (int)x->keys.size() && x->keys[i] == k) return true;
        return !x->leaf && contains(x->children[i], k);
    }

    void splitChild(BNode* x, int i) {
        BNode* y = x->children[i]; BNode* z = new BNode();
        z->leaf = y->leaf;
        int mid = y->keys[t - 1];
        z->keys.assign(y->keys.begin() + t, y->keys.end());           // upper t-1 keys
        y->keys.resize(t - 1);                                        // y keeps the lower t-1
        if (!y->leaf) {
            z->children.assign(y->children.begin() + t, y->children.end());
            y->children.resize(t);
        }
        x->keys.insert(x->keys.begin() + i, mid);                     // middle key moves up
        x->children.insert(x->children.begin() + i + 1, z);
    }

    void insertNonFull(BNode* x, int k) {
        int i = 0;
        while (i < (int)x->keys.size() && k > x->keys[i]) i++;
        if (x->leaf) { x->keys.insert(x->keys.begin() + i, k); return; }
        if ((int)x->children[i]->keys.size() == 2 * t - 1) {
            splitChild(x, i);
            if (k > x->keys[i]) i++;
        }
        insertNonFull(x->children[i], k);
    }

    void insert(int k) {
        if ((int)root->keys.size() == 2 * t - 1) {                    // full root: the tree grows here
            BNode* s = new BNode(); s->leaf = false; s->children.push_back(root);
            root = s; splitChild(s, 0);
        }
        insertNonFull(root, k);
    }
};

### skiplist
--idea
A skip list is a sorted linked list with express lanes. The bottom lane links every item in order. A lane above it links only about half of the items, the lane above that about a quarter, and so on. To find a value, start at the top lane and run along it until the next stop would overshoot, then drop one lane down and continue. It is like taking the express train, then the local train, then walking.
Which items appear in the higher lanes is decided by coin flips: when an item is inserted, keep flipping a coin and promote it one more lane for each head. On average half the items reach lane 2, a quarter reach lane 3, and so on, which gives the same "halve the work each step" effect as a balanced tree without any rotations. Redis uses skip lists for its sorted sets.
--build
1. A node holds `key` and an array `next[0 .. level - 1]` of forward pointers (`next[i]` is the next node in lane `i`). Make one `head` node with `MAX` lanes (like 16) and no key.
2. Keep `level` (the number of lanes currently in use), starting at 1.
3. `search(k)`: set `cur = head`. For `i` from `level - 1` down to 0: while `cur.next[i]` exists and its key `< k`, move `cur = cur.next[i]`. After all lanes, `cur.next[0]` is the first node with key `>= k`. Check if it equals `k`.
4. `insert(k)`: do the same walk, but remember in `update[i]` the last node visited in lane `i` (the node whose lane-`i` pointer must change).
5. Pick the new node's height `h`: start at 1 and keep adding 1 while a fair coin says heads (`randInt(0, 1) == 1`, the random-integer piece) and `h < MAX`. If `h > level`, set `update[level .. h-1] = head` and `level = h`.
6. Splice the new node `n` in: for each lane `i < h`: `n.next[i] = update[i].next[i]`, then `update[i].next[i] = n`.
7. `erase(k)`: do the walk with `update[]`. If `update[0].next[0]` has key `k`, then for each lane `i` where `update[i].next[i]` is that node, set `update[i].next[i]` to the node's `next[i]`. Then shrink `level` while the top lane is empty.
--think
- Signal: you need an ordered set but want less code than a balanced tree, or concurrent access (skip lists are easier to make lock-free). "Design a skiplist" is a well-known interview problem.
- Decide first the promotion probability `p` (1/2 is typical, 1/4 saves memory) and the maximum number of lanes (about `log(1/p)(n)`; 16 to 32 is plenty).
- Neighbouring techniques: balanced trees give guaranteed bounds while a skip list is only expected O(log n). A treap is another randomised option. A sorted array with binary search beats everything if there are no updates.
- Remember the `update[]` array: it is the same idea as "the path from the root" in a tree, just one pointer per lane.
- Pitfalls: forgetting to raise `level` for a very tall new node, walking with `<=` instead of `<` (the search must stop before the key), leaving stale lanes after erase, and using a poor random source.
--cost
Search, insert, erase are O(log n) expected. Space O(n) expected (about 2n pointers with p = 1/2).
--java
static class SkipList {
    static final int MAX = 16;
    static class Node { int key; Node[] next; Node(int k, int h) { key = k; next = new Node[h]; } }
    Node head = new Node(0, MAX);
    int level = 1;

    boolean contains(int k) {
        Node cur = head;
        for (int i = level - 1; i >= 0; i--)
            while (cur.next[i] != null && cur.next[i].key < k) cur = cur.next[i];
        cur = cur.next[0];
        return cur != null && cur.key == k;
    }

    void insert(int k) {
        Node[] update = new Node[MAX];
        Node cur = head;
        for (int i = level - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
            while (cur.next[i] != null && cur.next[i].key < k) cur = cur.next[i];
            update[i] = cur;                              // last node before k in lane i
        }
        if (cur.next[0] != null && cur.next[0].key == k) return;   // already present
        int h = 1;
        while (h < MAX && randInt(0, 1) == 1) h++;         // coin flips decide the height
        if (h > level) { for (int i = level; i < h; i++) update[i] = head; level = h; }
        Node n = new Node(k, h);
        for (int i = 0; i < h; i++) { n.next[i] = update[i].next[i]; update[i].next[i] = n; }
    }

    boolean erase(int k) {
        Node[] update = new Node[MAX];
        Node cur = head;
        for (int i = level - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
            while (cur.next[i] != null && cur.next[i].key < k) cur = cur.next[i];
            update[i] = cur;
        }
        Node t = cur.next[0];
        if (t == null || t.key != k) return false;
        for (int i = 0; i < level; i++)
            if (update[i].next[i] == t) update[i].next[i] = t.next[i];
        while (level > 1 && head.next[level - 1] == null) level--;
        return true;
    }
}
--py
class Node:
    def __init__(self, key, h):
        self.key = key
        self.next = [None] * h

class SkipList:
    MAX = 16

    def __init__(self):
        self.head = Node(None, self.MAX)
        self.level = 1

    def contains(self, k):
        cur = self.head
        for i in range(self.level - 1, -1, -1):
            while cur.next[i] and cur.next[i].key < k:
                cur = cur.next[i]
        cur = cur.next[0]
        return cur is not None and cur.key == k

    def _path(self, k):
        update, cur = [self.head] * self.MAX, self.head
        for i in range(self.level - 1, -1, -1):
            while cur.next[i] and cur.next[i].key < k:
                cur = cur.next[i]
            update[i] = cur                      # last node before k in lane i
        return update, cur.next[0]

    def insert(self, k):
        update, nxt = self._path(k)
        if nxt and nxt.key == k: return          # already present
        h = 1
        while h < self.MAX and rand_int(0, 1) == 1:   # coin flips decide the height
            h += 1
        self.level = max(self.level, h)
        n = Node(k, h)
        for i in range(h):
            n.next[i] = update[i].next[i]
            update[i].next[i] = n

    def erase(self, k):
        update, t = self._path(k)
        if not t or t.key != k: return False
        for i in range(self.level):
            if update[i].next[i] is t:
                update[i].next[i] = t.next[i]
        while self.level > 1 and self.head.next[self.level - 1] is None:
            self.level -= 1
        return True
--cpp
struct SkipList {
    static const int MAX = 16;
    struct Node { int key; vector<Node*> next; Node(int k, int h) : key(k), next(h, nullptr) {} };
    Node* head = new Node(0, MAX);
    int level = 1;

    bool contains(int k) const {
        Node* cur = head;
        for (int i = level - 1; i >= 0; i--)
            while (cur->next[i] && cur->next[i]->key < k) cur = cur->next[i];
        cur = cur->next[0];
        return cur && cur->key == k;
    }

    void insert(int k) {
        Node* update[MAX]; Node* cur = head;
        for (int i = level - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
            while (cur->next[i] && cur->next[i]->key < k) cur = cur->next[i];
            update[i] = cur;                              // last node before k in lane i
        }
        if (cur->next[0] && cur->next[0]->key == k) return;   // already present
        int h = 1;
        while (h < MAX && randInt(0, 1) == 1) h++;               // coin flips decide the height
        if (h > level) { for (int i = level; i < h; i++) update[i] = head; level = h; }
        Node* n = new Node(k, h);
        for (int i = 0; i < h; i++) { n->next[i] = update[i]->next[i]; update[i]->next[i] = n; }
    }

    bool erase(int k) {
        Node* update[MAX]; Node* cur = head;
        for (int i = level - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
            while (cur->next[i] && cur->next[i]->key < k) cur = cur->next[i];
            update[i] = cur;
        }
        Node* t = cur->next[0];
        if (!t || t->key != k) return false;
        for (int i = 0; i < level; i++)
            if (update[i]->next[i] == t) update[i]->next[i] = t->next[i];
        while (level > 1 && !head->next[level - 1]) level--;
        return true;
    }
};
--uses
u-rand

### itree
--idea
An interval tree stores a set of ranges, such as meetings (start, end), and answers "which stored ranges overlap this query range (or contain this point)?" quickly. Scanning every range works but is O(n) per query. The interval tree skips whole branches that cannot possibly overlap.
It is an ordinary binary search tree ordered by each interval's start. The one extra fact stored in every node is `maxEnd`: the largest end value anywhere in that node's subtree. If a subtree's `maxEnd` is less than the query's start, then every interval in it ends before the query begins, so the whole subtree is skipped.
--build
1. A node holds `lo`, `hi` (the interval), `maxHi` (the largest `hi` in its subtree), `left`, `right`. The tree is ordered by `lo`.
2. `insert(lo, hi)`: go down like a search tree comparing `lo` values. On the way back up, set `maxHi = max(hi of this node, maxHi of left, maxHi of right)`.
3. Two intervals `[a, b]` and `[c, d]` overlap exactly when `a <= d` and `c <= b`.
4. `query(l, r)` collects all intervals overlapping `[l, r]`. At a node `t`: (a) if the left child exists and `left.maxHi >= l`, there may be overlaps there, so recurse left. (b) If `t` itself overlaps (`t.lo <= r` and `t.hi >= l`), report it. (c) If `t.lo <= r`, recurse right; otherwise every interval on the right starts after `r` (they have `lo >= t.lo > r`), so skip.
5. For "does any interval overlap?" (just a yes or no), stop at the first match; this walks one path only, O(log n).
6. This plain version is not balanced. To guarantee O(log n) height, build it on a treap, AVL or red-black tree and update `maxHi` in the same places where sizes or heights are updated (after every rotation).
7. Alternative for static data: sort intervals, then build a balanced tree by taking the middle by start, which gives a balanced tree at once.
--think
- Signal: "meeting rooms", "calendar booking", "does this new range conflict with any existing one", "stabbing query: which intervals contain point x", "count overlapping intervals".
- Decide first whether intervals are closed `[a, b]` or half-open `[a, b)`. It changes the overlap test (`<=` versus `<`) and is the classic source of off-by-one bugs.
- Neighbouring techniques: if you only need to know the maximum number of overlaps at once, sort the endpoints and sweep (no tree). If intervals never overlap each other, an ordered map by start with floor and ceiling is enough. A segment tree on compressed coordinates handles "add one to a range" and "max overlap so far".
- Output size matters: reporting `k` matches costs O(k + log n) for a balanced tree. A degenerate tree makes it O(n).
- Pitfalls: forgetting to update `maxHi` after insertion, comparing `maxHi` with `r` instead of `l` when pruning the left side, and not rebuilding `maxHi` after a rotation.
--cost
Insert O(h), overlap query O(h + k) for k reported intervals, where h is the tree height (O(log n) when balanced). Space O(n).
--java
static class Node {
    int lo, hi, maxHi; Node left, right;
    Node(int lo, int hi) { this.lo = lo; this.hi = hi; maxHi = hi; }
}

static Node insert(Node t, int lo, int hi) {
    if (t == null) return new Node(lo, hi);
    if (lo < t.lo) t.left = insert(t.left, lo, hi);
    else t.right = insert(t.right, lo, hi);
    t.maxHi = Math.max(t.maxHi, hi);                   // the new interval lives below us
    return t;
}

// add every stored interval overlapping [l, r] to out
static void query(Node t, int l, int r, java.util.List<int[]> out) {
    if (t == null) return;
    if (t.left != null && t.left.maxHi >= l) query(t.left, l, r, out);   // something there may reach l
    if (t.lo <= r && t.hi >= l) out.add(new int[]{t.lo, t.hi});
    if (t.lo <= r) query(t.right, l, r, out);                            // right side starts at lo >= t.lo
}
--py
class Node:
    def __init__(self, lo, hi):
        self.lo, self.hi, self.max_hi = lo, hi, hi
        self.left = self.right = None

def insert(t, lo, hi):
    if t is None: return Node(lo, hi)
    if lo < t.lo: t.left = insert(t.left, lo, hi)
    else: t.right = insert(t.right, lo, hi)
    t.max_hi = max(t.max_hi, hi)                 # the new interval lives below us
    return t

def query(t, l, r, out):
    """Append every stored interval overlapping [l, r] to out."""
    if t is None: return
    if t.left and t.left.max_hi >= l:            # something there may reach l
        query(t.left, l, r, out)
    if t.lo <= r and t.hi >= l:
        out.append((t.lo, t.hi))
    if t.lo <= r:                                # right side starts at lo >= t.lo
        query(t.right, l, r, out)
--cpp
struct Node {
    int lo, hi, maxHi; Node *left = nullptr, *right = nullptr;
    Node(int lo, int hi) : lo(lo), hi(hi), maxHi(hi) {}
};

Node* insert(Node* t, int lo, int hi) {
    if (!t) return new Node(lo, hi);
    if (lo < t->lo) t->left = insert(t->left, lo, hi);
    else t->right = insert(t->right, lo, hi);
    t->maxHi = max(t->maxHi, hi);                      // the new interval lives below us
    return t;
}

// add every stored interval overlapping [l, r] to out
void query(Node* t, int l, int r, vector<pair<int,int>>& out) {
    if (!t) return;
    if (t->left && t->left->maxHi >= l) query(t->left, l, r, out);   // something there may reach l
    if (t->lo <= r && t->hi >= l) out.push_back({t->lo, t->hi});
    if (t->lo <= r) query(t->right, l, r, out);                      // right side starts at lo >= t->lo
}

### mergetree
--idea
A merge sort tree is a segment tree where every node keeps a sorted copy of the items in its range, instead of one summary number. It answers questions like "how many items in positions `l..r` are smaller than `x`?", which a plain segment tree cannot, because "smaller than `x`" depends on `x` and one stored number cannot answer it for every `x`.
Any query range splits into a few tree nodes whose ranges do not overlap. In each of those nodes the items are sorted, so a binary search counts the ones below `x` in log steps. Adding the counts gives the answer. The name comes from how it is built: each node is the merge of its two children's sorted lists, exactly the merge step of merge sort.
--build
1. Build a segment tree over positions `[0, n)`. Node 1 covers everything. A node covering `[lo, hi)` splits at `mid = (lo + hi) / 2` into children covering `[lo, mid)` and `[mid, hi)`.
2. A leaf (`hi - lo == 1`) stores a list with one item.
3. An inner node stores `merge(left list, right list)`, the two sorted lists joined into one sorted list. This is the code piece `u-merge`. Because every level of the tree holds each item once, the whole tree holds `n log n` items.
4. `countLess(l, r, x)`: walk down from the root. If the node's range is outside `[l, r)` return 0. If it is completely inside, return `lowerBound(list, x)`, the number of items smaller than `x` (code piece `u-lower`). Otherwise add the answers of both children.
5. A range `[l, r)` is reached through at most about `2 log n` fully covered nodes, each costing one binary search, so a query costs `log^2 n`.
6. The k-th smallest in `[l, r)`: the answer is the smallest value `v` for which `countLess(l, r, v + 1) >= k`. That is a "first true" search over values, so call `firstTrue(lo, hi, test)` (code piece `u-first`) with that test.
7. This structure does not support changing a value cheaply (every list on the path would need an insert). For updates use a wavelet tree, a Fenwick tree of sorted sets, or sqrt decomposition.
--think
- Signal: a static array and many queries of the form "in positions `l..r`, count the values below `x`, or the k-th smallest, or the closest value to `x`". The word "range" plus a value comparison is the sign.
- Decide first whether the array changes. If not, a merge sort tree, a wavelet tree, or a persistent segment tree all work offline or online. If it changes, think harder (sqrt blocks with sorted lists, or offline divide and conquer).
- Neighbouring techniques: a persistent segment tree answers k-th in a range in O(log n) and uses less memory (O(n log n) nodes holding small numbers). A wavelet tree does the same with bit arrays. Offline, you can sort queries and use a Fenwick tree instead. The merge sort tree wins on simplicity: only a segment tree plus merge.
- Memory is `n log n` integers. For `n = 10^5` that is about 1.7 million numbers, fine. For `n = 10^6` it is 20 million, heavy.
- Pitfalls: confusing count of values `< x` with `<= x` (use `lowerBound` of `x` or of `x + 1`), building with a recursive merge that copies lists (fine, but do not re-sort), and the search range of values in step 6 (use the minimum and maximum of the array).
--cost
Build O(n log n) time and space. Count query O(log^2 n). K-th smallest in a range O(log^2 n x log(value range)).
--java
static class MergeTree {
    int n, minV = Integer.MAX_VALUE, maxV = Integer.MIN_VALUE;
    int[][] t;                                           // t[node] = sorted values of the node's range

    MergeTree(int[] a) {
        n = a.length; t = new int[4 * n][];
        for (int x : a) { minV = Math.min(minV, x); maxV = Math.max(maxV, x); }
        build(1, 0, n, a);
    }

    void build(int node, int lo, int hi, int[] a) {
        if (hi - lo == 1) { t[node] = new int[]{a[lo]}; return; }
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        build(2 * node, lo, mid, a);
        build(2 * node + 1, mid, hi, a);
        t[node] = merge(t[2 * node], t[2 * node + 1]);   // piece u-merge
    }

    int countLess(int node, int lo, int hi, int l, int r, int x) {   // values < x in a[l, r)
        if (r <= lo || hi <= l) return 0;
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r) return lowerBound(t[node], x);        // piece u-lower
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        return countLess(2 * node, lo, mid, l, r, x) + countLess(2 * node + 1, mid, hi, l, r, x);
    }

    int countLess(int l, int r, int x) { return countLess(1, 0, n, l, r, x); }

    int kth(int l, int r, int k) {                       // k-th smallest in a[l, r), k from 1
        return firstTrue(minV, maxV, v -> countLess(l, r, v + 1) >= k);   // piece u-first
    }
}
--py
class MergeTree:
    def __init__(self, a):
        self.n = len(a)
        self.t = [None] * (4 * self.n)       # t[node] = sorted values of the node's range
        self.lo_v, self.hi_v = min(a), max(a)
        self._build(1, 0, self.n, a)

    def _build(self, node, lo, hi, a):
        if hi - lo == 1:
            self.t[node] = [a[lo]]
            return
        mid = (lo + hi) // 2
        self._build(2 * node, lo, mid, a)
        self._build(2 * node + 1, mid, hi, a)
        self.t[node] = merge(self.t[2 * node], self.t[2 * node + 1])    # piece u-merge

    def _count(self, node, lo, hi, l, r, x):          # values < x in a[l, r)
        if r <= lo or hi <= l: return 0
        if l <= lo and hi <= r: return lower_bound(self.t[node], x)     # piece u-lower
        mid = (lo + hi) // 2
        return self._count(2 * node, lo, mid, l, r, x) + self._count(2 * node + 1, mid, hi, l, r, x)

    def count_less(self, l, r, x):
        return self._count(1, 0, self.n, l, r, x)

    def kth(self, l, r, k):                           # k-th smallest in a[l, r), k from 1
        return first_true(self.lo_v, self.hi_v, lambda v: self.count_less(l, r, v + 1) >= k)   # piece u-first
--cpp
struct MergeTree {
    int n, minV, maxV;
    vector<vector<int>> t;                               // t[node] = sorted values of the node's range

    MergeTree(const vector<int>& a) : n(a.size()), t(4 * a.size()) {
        minV = *min_element(a.begin(), a.end());
        maxV = *max_element(a.begin(), a.end());
        build(1, 0, n, a);
    }

    void build(int node, int lo, int hi, const vector<int>& a) {
        if (hi - lo == 1) { t[node] = {a[lo]}; return; }
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        build(2 * node, lo, mid, a);
        build(2 * node + 1, mid, hi, a);
        t[node] = mergeSorted(t[2 * node], t[2 * node + 1]);          // piece u-merge
    }

    int countLess(int node, int lo, int hi, int l, int r, int x) const {   // values < x in a[l, r)
        if (r <= lo || hi <= l) return 0;
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r) return lowerBound(t[node], x);        // piece u-lower
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        return countLess(2 * node, lo, mid, l, r, x) + countLess(2 * node + 1, mid, hi, l, r, x);
    }

    int countLess(int l, int r, int x) const { return countLess(1, 0, n, l, r, x); }

    int kth(int l, int r, int k) const {                 // k-th smallest in a[l, r), k from 1
        return firstTrue(minV, maxV, [&](int v) { return countLess(l, r, v + 1) >= k; });   // piece u-first
    }
};
--uses
u-merge
u-lower
u-first

### ostat
--idea
An order statistic tree is a search tree that can also answer "what is the k-th smallest key?" and "how many keys are smaller than this one?" while keys are being inserted and deleted. A plain sorted array answers both in O(1) and O(log n) but needs O(n) per insert. A plain search tree inserts quickly but cannot count. This structure does everything in about log n.
The one extra fact: every node stores `size`, the number of keys in its subtree. To find the k-th smallest, compare `k` with the size of the left subtree. If `k` is at most that size, the answer is on the left. If `k` is exactly one more, the answer is this node. Otherwise it is on the right, and you have already used up the left subtree and this node, so subtract them from `k`.
--build
1. A node holds `key`, `left`, `right` and `size` (the number of nodes in its subtree, itself included). An empty tree has size 0.
2. Invariant: `size = 1 + size(left) + size(right)` for every node. Every operation that changes the shape of the tree must restore this on the way back up.
3. `insert(t, key)`: recurse like a normal search tree. On the way back up, recompute `t.size = 1 + size(t.left) + size(t.right)`.
4. `kth(t, k)` (k counts from 1): let `s = size(t.left)`. If `k <= s`, go left. If `k == s + 1`, return `t.key`. Otherwise set `k -= s + 1` and go right.
5. `rank(t, key)` (how many keys are smaller than `key`): start at 0. At a node, if `key <= t.key` go left. Otherwise add `size(t.left) + 1` (the left subtree and this node are all smaller) and go right.
6. `erase(t, key)`: as in a normal search tree (zero or one child: replace by the child; two children: copy in the smallest key of the right subtree and erase that from the right). Recompute `size` on the way up.
7. This plain version can become a tall line on sorted input. To keep it fast, apply the same `size` field to a balanced tree (a treap, AVL or red-black tree) and update `size` in every rotation.
--think
- Signal: "k-th smallest of a changing set", "how many elements are less than x so far", "position of x in sorted order", "median of a set with deletions", "rank queries online".
- Decide first whether you need online updates mixed with queries. If the values are known in advance, a Fenwick tree over compressed ranks does the same with much less code: `add(rank, 1)` and a binary descent for k-th.
- Neighbouring techniques: two heaps give only the median, not any k. A sorted list with `bisect` is fine up to about 10^5 items. The tree is for when you need guaranteed log n and also deletions.
- Count of keys in a range `[a, b)` is `rank(b) - rank(a)`. The k-th is the inverse of rank: `rank(kth(k)) == k - 1` when keys are distinct.
- Pitfalls: forgetting to update `size` on the path after an insert that was ignored as a duplicate (do not change anything then), off-by-one between "k counts from 0" and "k counts from 1", and skewed input making the tree a line.
--cost
Insert, erase, kth, rank are O(h), the tree height. O(log n) when balanced, O(n) in the worst case for this plain version. Space O(n).
--java
static class Node {
    int key, size = 1; Node left, right;
    Node(int k) { key = k; }
}
static int size(Node t) { return t == null ? 0 : t.size; }
static void fix(Node t) { t.size = 1 + size(t.left) + size(t.right); }

static Node insert(Node t, int k) {
    if (t == null) return new Node(k);
    if (k < t.key) t.left = insert(t.left, k);
    else if (k > t.key) t.right = insert(t.right, k);
    else return t;                                 // duplicate: nothing changes
    fix(t);                                        // restore the size on the way up
    return t;
}

static int kth(Node t, int k) {                    // k-th smallest, k from 1; k must be in range
    while (t != null) {
        int s = size(t.left);
        if (k <= s) t = t.left;
        else if (k == s + 1) return t.key;
        else { k -= s + 1; t = t.right; }
    }
    throw new IllegalArgumentException("k out of range");
}

static int rank(Node t, int key) {                 // number of keys smaller than key
    int r = 0;
    while (t != null) {
        if (key <= t.key) t = t.left;
        else { r += size(t.left) + 1; t = t.right; }
    }
    return r;
}

static Node erase(Node t, int k) {
    if (t == null) return null;
    if (k < t.key) t.left = erase(t.left, k);
    else if (k > t.key) t.right = erase(t.right, k);
    else {
        if (t.left == null) return t.right;
        if (t.right == null) return t.left;
        Node s = t.right;
        while (s.left != null) s = s.left;         // successor
        t.key = s.key;
        t.right = erase(t.right, s.key);
    }
    fix(t);
    return t;
}
--py
class Node:
    def __init__(self, key):
        self.key, self.size = key, 1
        self.left = self.right = None

def size(t): return t.size if t else 0
def fix(t): t.size = 1 + size(t.left) + size(t.right)

def insert(t, k):
    if t is None: return Node(k)
    if k < t.key: t.left = insert(t.left, k)
    elif k > t.key: t.right = insert(t.right, k)
    else: return t                                 # duplicate: nothing changes
    fix(t)                                         # restore the size on the way up
    return t

def kth(t, k):
    """k-th smallest key, k counts from 1."""
    while t:
        s = size(t.left)
        if k <= s: t = t.left
        elif k == s + 1: return t.key
        else: k -= s + 1; t = t.right
    raise IndexError("k out of range")

def rank(t, key):
    """Number of keys smaller than key."""
    r = 0
    while t:
        if key <= t.key: t = t.left
        else: r += size(t.left) + 1; t = t.right
    return r

def erase(t, k):
    if t is None: return None
    if k < t.key: t.left = erase(t.left, k)
    elif k > t.key: t.right = erase(t.right, k)
    else:
        if t.left is None: return t.right
        if t.right is None: return t.left
        s = t.right
        while s.left: s = s.left                   # successor
        t.key = s.key
        t.right = erase(t.right, s.key)
    fix(t)
    return t
--cpp
struct Node {
    int key, size = 1; Node *left = nullptr, *right = nullptr;
    Node(int k) : key(k) {}
};
int sz(Node* t) { return t ? t->size : 0; }
void fix(Node* t) { t->size = 1 + sz(t->left) + sz(t->right); }

Node* insert(Node* t, int k) {
    if (!t) return new Node(k);
    if (k < t->key) t->left = insert(t->left, k);
    else if (k > t->key) t->right = insert(t->right, k);
    else return t;                                 // duplicate: nothing changes
    fix(t);                                        // restore the size on the way up
    return t;
}

int kth(Node* t, int k) {                          // k-th smallest, k from 1; k must be in range
    while (t) {
        int s = sz(t->left);
        if (k <= s) t = t->left;
        else if (k == s + 1) return t->key;
        else { k -= s + 1; t = t->right; }
    }
    return -1;
}

int rankOf(Node* t, int key) {                     // number of keys smaller than key
    int r = 0;
    while (t) {
        if (key <= t->key) t = t->left;
        else { r += sz(t->left) + 1; t = t->right; }
    }
    return r;
}

Node* erase(Node* t, int k) {
    if (!t) return nullptr;
    if (k < t->key) t->left = erase(t->left, k);
    else if (k > t->key) t->right = erase(t->right, k);
    else {
        if (!t->left) return t->right;
        if (!t->right) return t->left;
        Node* s = t->right;
        while (s->left) s = s->left;               // successor
        t->key = s->key;
        t->right = erase(t->right, s->key);
    }
    fix(t);
    return t;
}

### sqrt
--idea
Square root decomposition cuts an array of `n` items into blocks of about `sqrt(n)` items each, and keeps one summary (a sum, a minimum, a count) per block. A query over a range then touches whole blocks using their summaries, plus a few loose items at the two ends, so it costs about `2 sqrt(n)` steps instead of `n`.
Think of a book split into chapters. To count words from page 12 to page 480, you read the chapter totals for the full chapters in the middle and count the few partial pages at both ends by hand. It is slower than a segment tree (`sqrt(n)` against `log n`) but far easier to write, and it handles odd operations a tree cannot, such as "the most frequent value in a range".
--build
1. Choose the block size `B = sqrtFloor(n)` (code piece `u-sqrt`), at least 1. Block `b` holds positions `b * B` up to `min(n, (b + 1) * B) - 1`, and position `i` is in block `i / B`.
2. Keep three arrays: `a` (the items), `blockSum[b]` (the sum of the items in block `b`, including pending adds), and `lazy[b]` (an amount added to every item of block `b` that has not been written into `a` yet).
3. Build: for every `i`, `a[i] = v[i]` and `blockSum[i / B] += v[i]`. This takes O(n).
4. `rangeAdd(l, r, d)` on `[l, r)`: walk `i` from `l`. Let `b = i / B`, `bs = b * B`, `be = min(n, bs + B)`, `end = min(r, be)`. If `i == bs` and `end == be` the whole block is covered: do `lazy[b] += d` and `blockSum[b] += d * (be - bs)`. Otherwise change items one by one: `a[j] += d` and `blockSum[b] += d` for `j` in `[i, end)`. Then set `i = end`.
5. `rangeSum(l, r)` walks the same way. A whole block adds `blockSum[b]`. A partial block adds `a[j] + lazy[b]` for each `j` in the covered part.
6. Invariant: the true value of item `j` is `a[j] + lazy[block of j]`, and `blockSum[b]` is the true sum of block `b`.
7. Each call touches at most two partial blocks (at most `2B` items) and at most `n / B` whole blocks, so both are about `2 sqrt(n)`.
--think
- Signal: range queries with updates, where a segment tree would need an awkward merge, or the constraints are small (`n` about 10^5, about 10^5 operations: `sqrt(n)` is about 316, so 3 x 10^7 steps). Also "Mo's algorithm" problems, which use the same block idea offline.
- Decide first what one block must remember so that whole blocks can be answered without looking inside, and what lazy mark lets you update a whole block without touching its items.
- Neighbouring techniques: a segment tree with lazy propagation is faster (log n) but longer. A Fenwick tree is faster and shorter when the operation is a prefix sum. Use sqrt decomposition when the per-block information is complicated (sorted lists, hash maps of counts) or you want the simplest correct code.
- You can tune the block size. Updates that touch a whole block cheaply but queries that are costly want a bigger `B`, and the reverse a smaller one. Balance them.
- Pitfalls: the last block is usually shorter than `B` (use `min(n, ...)`), forgetting to apply `lazy[b]` when reading one item of a block, and `B = 0` when `n` is tiny.
--cost
Build O(n). Range add and range sum O(sqrt(n)) each. Space O(n).
--java
static class SqrtArray {
    int n, B; long[] a, blockSum, lazy;

    SqrtArray(int[] v) {
        n = v.length;
        B = (int) Math.max(1, sqrtFloor(n));                // piece u-sqrt
        a = new long[n]; blockSum = new long[(n + B - 1) / B]; lazy = new long[blockSum.length];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { a[i] = v[i]; blockSum[i / B] += v[i]; }
    }

    void rangeAdd(int l, int r, long d) {                    // add d to every item in [l, r)
        for (int i = l; i < r; ) {
            int b = i / B, bs = b * B, be = Math.min(n, bs + B), end = Math.min(r, be);
            if (i == bs && end == be) { lazy[b] += d; blockSum[b] += d * (be - bs); }   // whole block
            else for (int j = i; j < end; j++) { a[j] += d; blockSum[b] += d; }          // loose items
            i = end;
        }
    }

    long rangeSum(int l, int r) {                            // sum of items in [l, r)
        long s = 0;
        for (int i = l; i < r; ) {
            int b = i / B, bs = b * B, be = Math.min(n, bs + B), end = Math.min(r, be);
            if (i == bs && end == be) s += blockSum[b];
            else for (int j = i; j < end; j++) s += a[j] + lazy[b];
            i = end;
        }
        return s;
    }
}
--py
class SqrtArray:
    def __init__(self, v):
        self.n = len(v)
        self.B = max(1, sqrt_floor(self.n))                  # piece u-sqrt
        self.a = list(v)
        nb = (self.n + self.B - 1) // self.B
        self.block_sum, self.lazy = [0] * nb, [0] * nb
        for i, x in enumerate(v):
            self.block_sum[i // self.B] += x

    def range_add(self, l, r, d):                            # add d to every item in [l, r)
        i = l
        while i < r:
            b = i // self.B
            bs = b * self.B
            be = min(self.n, bs + self.B)
            end = min(r, be)
            if i == bs and end == be:                        # whole block
                self.lazy[b] += d
                self.block_sum[b] += d * (be - bs)
            else:                                            # loose items
                for j in range(i, end):
                    self.a[j] += d
                self.block_sum[b] += d * (end - i)
            i = end

    def range_sum(self, l, r):                               # sum of items in [l, r)
        s, i = 0, l
        while i < r:
            b = i // self.B
            bs = b * self.B
            be = min(self.n, bs + self.B)
            end = min(r, be)
            if i == bs and end == be:
                s += self.block_sum[b]
            else:
                s += sum(self.a[i:end]) + self.lazy[b] * (end - i)
            i = end
        return s
--cpp
struct SqrtArray {
    int n, B; vector<long long> a, blockSum, lazy;

    SqrtArray(const vector<int>& v) : n(v.size()) {
        B = max(1LL, sqrtFloor(n));                          // piece u-sqrt
        a.assign(v.begin(), v.end());
        blockSum.assign((n + B - 1) / B, 0); lazy.assign(blockSum.size(), 0);
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) blockSum[i / B] += v[i];
    }

    void rangeAdd(int l, int r, long long d) {               // add d to every item in [l, r)
        for (int i = l; i < r; ) {
            int b = i / B, bs = b * B, be = min(n, bs + B), end = min(r, be);
            if (i == bs && end == be) { lazy[b] += d; blockSum[b] += d * (be - bs); }   // whole block
            else for (int j = i; j < end; j++) { a[j] += d; blockSum[b] += d; }          // loose items
            i = end;
        }
    }

    long long rangeSum(int l, int r) const {                 // sum of items in [l, r)
        long long s = 0;
        for (int i = l; i < r; ) {
            int b = i / B, bs = b * B, be = min(n, bs + B), end = min(r, be);
            if (i == bs && end == be) s += blockSum[b];
            else for (int j = i; j < end; j++) s += a[j] + lazy[b];
            i = end;
        }
        return s;
    }
};
--uses
u-sqrt

### bloom
--idea
A Bloom filter remembers a huge set of items in a tiny amount of memory, and answers "have I seen this?" with either "definitely not" or "probably yes". It never forgets an item that was added (no false "no"), but it sometimes claims to have seen something it has not (a false "yes"). You trade a small, controllable error rate for a very large saving in space. Browsers use it to check a page against a list of bad sites before asking the server.
It is a row of `m` bits, all 0 at first, and `k` different hash functions. To add an item, compute its `k` hash values, each a position in the row, and turn those `k` bits on. To check an item, compute the same `k` positions: if any of those bits is off, the item was never added. If all are on, it was probably added, but other items might have switched those same bits on by chance.
--build
1. Choose `m` (bits) and `k` (hash functions). For `n` expected items, the false-yes rate is about `(1 - e^(-kn/m))^k`, and the best `k` is about `(m / n) * ln 2`. Using about 10 bits per item with `k = 7` gives about 1 percent false yeses.
2. Store the bits packed into integers. Position `p` lives in word `p / W`, at bit `p % W`. (The code uses `W = 30` bits of each `int` to stay clear of the sign bit.) Reading and writing one bit are the pieces `getBit` and `setBit` (code piece `u-bit`).
3. Get `k` hash values from just two hashes by double hashing: `pos_i = (h1 + i * h2) mod m` for `i = 0 .. k-1`. Make `h1` from the string with the rolling hash `buildHash` (code piece `u-hashroll`), taking the hash of the whole string. Make `h2` the same way from the reversed string, and force it odd with `h2 | 1`.
4. `add(item)`: for each `i`, turn on bit `pos_i`.
5. `mightContain(item)`: return false as soon as some bit `pos_i` is off. Return true if all `k` bits are on.
6. Invariant: a bit never turns off, so an added item always finds all its bits on. That is why there are no false "no" answers.
7. You cannot remove an item (turning its bits off could break other items). A counting Bloom filter keeps a small counter per position to allow deletes.
--think
- Signal: "is this URL, username or key in a huge set?", memory is tight, and an occasional false positive is acceptable (the cost of a wrong "yes" is just one extra slow check). Also caches: "skip the disk lookup if the filter says no".
- Decide first the acceptable error rate and the item count, then derive `m` and `k`. Too small `m` for the item count makes the filter useless (almost all bits are on).
- Neighbouring techniques: a hash set is exact but stores every item. A cuckoo filter allows deletes. A count-min sketch estimates counts, not membership. A trie or sorted list is for exact prefix search.
- A Bloom filter cannot list its items or tell how many it holds, and it cannot shrink.
- Pitfalls: a weak or correlated second hash (the two must behave independently), forgetting the modulo, using signed shifts at bit 31, and expecting deletes.
--cost
Add and query O(k + length of the item). Space `m` bits, independent of item size.
--java
static class Bloom {
    static final int W = 30;                                   // bits used per int word
    int m, k; int[] words;
    Bloom(int m, int k) { this.m = m; this.k = k; words = new int[m / W + 1]; }

    long hashOf(String s) { return buildHash(s)[0][s.length()]; }      // piece u-hashroll: hash of the whole string

    int pos(long h1, long h2, int i) { return (int) ((h1 + i * (h2 | 1)) % m); }   // double hashing

    void add(String s) {
        long h1 = hashOf(s), h2 = hashOf(new StringBuilder(s).reverse().toString());
        for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) {
            int p = pos(h1, h2, i);
            words[p / W] = setBit(words[p / W], p % W);                  // piece u-bit
        }
    }

    boolean mightContain(String s) {
        long h1 = hashOf(s), h2 = hashOf(new StringBuilder(s).reverse().toString());
        for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) {
            int p = pos(h1, h2, i);
            if (!getBit(words[p / W], p % W)) return false;              // a missing bit means never added
        }
        return true;
    }
}
--py
class Bloom:
    W = 30                                                   # bits used per int word

    def __init__(self, m, k):
        self.m, self.k = m, k
        self.words = [0] * (m // self.W + 1)

    @staticmethod
    def _hash_of(s):
        return build_hash(s)[0][-1]                          # piece u-hashroll: hash of the whole string

    def _positions(self, s):
        h1, h2 = self._hash_of(s), self._hash_of(s[::-1]) | 1
        return [(h1 + i * h2) % self.m for i in range(self.k)]   # double hashing

    def add(self, s):
        for p in self._positions(s):
            w = p // self.W
            self.words[w] = set_bit(self.words[w], p % self.W)   # piece u-bit

    def might_contain(self, s):
        # a missing bit means never added
        return all(get_bit(self.words[p // self.W], p % self.W) for p in self._positions(s))
--cpp
struct Bloom {
    static const int W = 30;                                  // bits used per int word
    int m, k; vector<int> words;
    Bloom(int m, int k) : m(m), k(k), words(m / W + 1, 0) {}

    static long long hashOf(const string& s) { return buildHash(s).first[s.size()]; }   // piece u-hashroll

    vector<int> positions(const string& s) const {
        long long h1 = hashOf(s), h2 = hashOf(string(s.rbegin(), s.rend())) | 1;
        vector<int> ps;
        for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) ps.push_back((h1 + i * h2) % m);   // double hashing
        return ps;
    }

    void add(const string& s) {
        for (int p : positions(s)) words[p / W] = setBit(words[p / W], p % W);   // piece u-bit
    }

    bool mightContain(const string& s) const {
        for (int p : positions(s))
            if (!getBit(words[p / W], p % W)) return false;    // a missing bit means never added
        return true;
    }
};
--uses
u-hashroll
u-bit

### cms
--idea
A count-min sketch estimates how many times each item has appeared in a huge stream, using a small fixed table, without storing the items themselves. Counting every distinct URL or search term in a hash map would need memory proportional to the number of distinct items. A sketch needs a few thousand numbers, always, at the price of slightly over-counting.
The table has `d` rows and `w` columns of counters. Each row has its own hash function that maps an item to one column. To add an item, increase one counter in every row (the column that row's hash picks). To estimate an item, read its counter in every row and take the smallest. Other items may share a counter and push it up, but never down, so every counter is at least the true count. The smallest of `d` such counters is the closest to the truth.
--build
1. Choose the width `w` and depth `d`. To get an error of at most `epsilon x N` (where `N` is the total number of items seen) with probability `1 - delta`, take `w = ceil(e / epsilon)` and `d = ceil(ln(1 / delta))`.
2. Make a table `cnt[d][w]` of counters, all zero. Use `long` if the stream is long.
3. Row hashes by double hashing: `col_r = (h1 + r * h2) mod w` for row `r`. Take `h1` from the string with the rolling hash `buildHash` (code piece `u-hashroll`, the hash of the whole string) and `h2` the same way from the reversed string, forced odd.
4. `add(item, c)`: for each row `r`, `cnt[r][col_r] += c`.
5. `estimate(item)`: the minimum over the rows of `cnt[r][col_r]`.
6. Invariant: every counter holds the true counts of all items hashed there, so `cnt[r][col_r] >= true count`. The estimate never under-counts, and over-counts by at most `epsilon x N` with high probability.
7. Two sketches with the same size and hash functions can be merged by adding their tables cell by cell, which makes sketches useful across many machines.
--think
- Signal: a stream or huge log, "top frequent items", "how often did this key appear", memory limits, and an approximate answer is fine. Heavy hitters (items above some frequency) are found by keeping a small heap of the largest estimates.
- Decide first the error you can live with relative to the stream length `N`. The guarantee is about `N`, not about the item's own count, so rare items can have large relative error. The sketch is good at finding the heavy items.
- Neighbouring techniques: an exact hash map is fine if the distinct items fit in memory. A Bloom filter answers yes or no only. HyperLogLog counts distinct items. Misra-Gries or space-saving keep an exact small set of heavy hitters.
- The count-min estimate only goes up, so it is biased. A "count-mean-min" variant subtracts the expected noise to reduce bias.
- Pitfalls: using the same hash for every row (then extra rows add nothing), choosing `w` too small for `N`, expecting exact counts, and supporting negative updates (the minimum trick then fails).
--cost
Add and estimate O(d + length of the item). Space O(w x d), fixed.
--java
static class CountMin {
    int w, d; long[][] cnt;
    CountMin(int w, int d) { this.w = w; this.d = d; cnt = new long[d][w]; }

    long hashOf(String s) { return buildHash(s)[0][s.length()]; }       // piece u-hashroll

    int col(long h1, long h2, int r) { return (int) ((h1 + r * (h2 | 1)) % w); }   // row r's own hash

    void add(String s, long c) {
        long h1 = hashOf(s), h2 = hashOf(new StringBuilder(s).reverse().toString());
        for (int r = 0; r < d; r++) cnt[r][col(h1, h2, r)] += c;
    }

    long estimate(String s) {
        long h1 = hashOf(s), h2 = hashOf(new StringBuilder(s).reverse().toString());
        long best = Long.MAX_VALUE;
        for (int r = 0; r < d; r++) best = Math.min(best, cnt[r][col(h1, h2, r)]);   // smallest counter
        return best;
    }
}
--py
class CountMin:
    def __init__(self, w, d):
        self.w, self.d = w, d
        self.cnt = [[0] * w for _ in range(d)]

    @staticmethod
    def _hash_of(s):
        return build_hash(s)[0][-1]                          # piece u-hashroll: hash of the whole string

    def _cols(self, s):
        h1, h2 = self._hash_of(s), self._hash_of(s[::-1]) | 1
        return [(h1 + r * h2) % self.w for r in range(self.d)]   # row r's own hash

    def add(self, s, c=1):
        for r, col in enumerate(self._cols(s)):
            self.cnt[r][col] += c

    def estimate(self, s):
        return min(self.cnt[r][col] for r, col in enumerate(self._cols(s)))   # smallest counter
--cpp
struct CountMin {
    int w, d; vector<vector<long long>> cnt;
    CountMin(int w, int d) : w(w), d(d), cnt(d, vector<long long>(w, 0)) {}

    static long long hashOf(const string& s) { return buildHash(s).first[s.size()]; }   // piece u-hashroll

    vector<int> cols(const string& s) const {
        long long h1 = hashOf(s), h2 = hashOf(string(s.rbegin(), s.rend())) | 1;
        vector<int> cs;
        for (int r = 0; r < d; r++) cs.push_back((h1 + r * h2) % w);   // row r's own hash
        return cs;
    }

    void add(const string& s, long long c = 1) {
        auto cs = cols(s);
        for (int r = 0; r < d; r++) cnt[r][cs[r]] += c;
    }

    long long estimate(const string& s) const {
        auto cs = cols(s);
        long long best = LLONG_MAX;
        for (int r = 0; r < d; r++) best = min(best, cnt[r][cs[r]]);   // smallest counter
        return best;
    }
};
--uses
u-hashroll

### lazy
--idea
Lazy propagation lets a segment tree handle range updates ("add 5 to every item from position 10 to 90") as fast as it handles queries. Without it you would have to change up to `n` leaves and then repair their ancestors. With it, you change only the few nodes that exactly cover the range and leave a note on each: "everything below me still needs +5". The note is the lazy tag.
The tag is only pushed down when someone actually needs to look inside that node. Think of a manager who is told "give everyone in this department a raise". The manager does not phone each employee. He writes it on the department's sheet, and updates the total on the sheet. Only when a question reaches one employee does the pending raise get passed down one level.
--build
1. Start from a recursive segment tree over `[0, n)`. Node `o` covers `[lo, hi)`. Its children `2o` and `2o + 1` cover `[lo, mid)` and `[mid, hi)` with `mid = (lo + hi) / 2`. Arrays: `sum[o]` (the true sum of the node's range, assuming all notes above it are already applied) and `add[o]` (a pending amount to add to every item of the range, not yet given to the children).
2. `apply(o, lo, hi, d)`: the node's whole range gets `+d`. Do `sum[o] += d * (hi - lo)` and `add[o] += d`. This is O(1) and touches no children.
3. `push(o, lo, hi)`: if `add[o] != 0`, call `apply` on both children with `add[o]`, then reset `add[o] = 0`. After this the node has no debts.
4. `rangeAdd(o, lo, hi, l, r, d)`: if `[lo, hi)` does not meet `[l, r)`, return. If it is fully inside, `apply(o, lo, hi, d)` and return. Otherwise `push` first, recurse into both children, then recompute `sum[o] = sum[2o] + sum[2o + 1]`.
5. `rangeSum(o, lo, hi, l, r)`: outside returns 0, fully inside returns `sum[o]`. Otherwise `push` first, then add the answers of both children.
6. Invariant: `sum[o]` is correct whenever all the pending tags on the path from the root to `o` have been pushed. Every walk that goes below a node pushes it first, so the invariant holds along the path it takes.
7. Every query and update visits at most about 4 log n nodes, so the cost stays logarithmic.
--think
- Signal: range update plus range query on the same array. "Add to all items in a range, then ask the sum or the max of a range." Also "set all items in a range to `v`" and "flip all bits in a range".
- Decide first what a tag means and how two tags combine. For "add", tags add up and commute. For "assign", a new tag replaces the old one. For "assign then add" mixes you need an order (a small tuple of multiply and add, applied as `x -> m * x + b`). The tag algebra is the hard part.
- Decide second how a tag changes the node's summary. Sum under add: `sum += d * length`. Maximum under add: `max += d` (no length). Sum under assign: `sum = v * length`.
- Neighbouring techniques: a Fenwick tree handles range add with point query, or range add with range sum using two trees, but not "assign" or "max". Sqrt decomposition handles the same operations in `sqrt(n)`. Offline difference arrays work if all updates come before all queries.
- Pitfalls: forgetting to `push` before descending (stale children), pushing after recursing, using `int` for sums (use `long`), applying `d` without multiplying by the length, and a tag value that cannot be told apart from "no tag" (use a separate flag for assign tags, since assigning 0 is a real tag).
--cost
Build O(n). Range update and range query O(log n) each. Space O(n), two arrays of 4n.
--java
static class LazySeg {
    int n; long[] sum, add;
    LazySeg(int[] a) {
        n = a.length; sum = new long[4 * n]; add = new long[4 * n];
        build(1, 0, n, a);
    }
    void build(int o, int lo, int hi, int[] a) {
        if (hi - lo == 1) { sum[o] = a[lo]; return; }
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        build(2 * o, lo, mid, a); build(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, a);
        sum[o] = sum[2 * o] + sum[2 * o + 1];
    }
    void apply(int o, int lo, int hi, long d) { sum[o] += d * (hi - lo); add[o] += d; }   // note only
    void push(int o, int lo, int hi) {                       // hand the note to the children
        if (add[o] == 0) return;
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        apply(2 * o, lo, mid, add[o]); apply(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, add[o]);
        add[o] = 0;
    }
    void rangeAdd(int o, int lo, int hi, int l, int r, long d) {
        if (r <= lo || hi <= l) return;
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r) { apply(o, lo, hi, d); return; }
        push(o, lo, hi);
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        rangeAdd(2 * o, lo, mid, l, r, d); rangeAdd(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, l, r, d);
        sum[o] = sum[2 * o] + sum[2 * o + 1];
    }
    long rangeSum(int o, int lo, int hi, int l, int r) {
        if (r <= lo || hi <= l) return 0;
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r) return sum[o];
        push(o, lo, hi);
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        return rangeSum(2 * o, lo, mid, l, r) + rangeSum(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, l, r);
    }
    void rangeAdd(int l, int r, long d) { rangeAdd(1, 0, n, l, r, d); }   // on [l, r)
    long rangeSum(int l, int r) { return rangeSum(1, 0, n, l, r); }
}
--py
class LazySeg:
    def __init__(self, a):
        self.n = len(a)
        self.sum = [0] * (4 * self.n)
        self.add = [0] * (4 * self.n)
        self._build(1, 0, self.n, a)

    def _build(self, o, lo, hi, a):
        if hi - lo == 1:
            self.sum[o] = a[lo]
            return
        mid = (lo + hi) // 2
        self._build(2 * o, lo, mid, a)
        self._build(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, a)
        self.sum[o] = self.sum[2 * o] + self.sum[2 * o + 1]

    def _apply(self, o, lo, hi, d):              # note only, children not touched
        self.sum[o] += d * (hi - lo)
        self.add[o] += d

    def _push(self, o, lo, hi):                  # hand the note to the children
        if self.add[o] == 0: return
        mid = (lo + hi) // 2
        self._apply(2 * o, lo, mid, self.add[o])
        self._apply(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, self.add[o])
        self.add[o] = 0

    def _range_add(self, o, lo, hi, l, r, d):
        if r <= lo or hi <= l: return
        if l <= lo and hi <= r:
            self._apply(o, lo, hi, d); return
        self._push(o, lo, hi)
        mid = (lo + hi) // 2
        self._range_add(2 * o, lo, mid, l, r, d)
        self._range_add(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, l, r, d)
        self.sum[o] = self.sum[2 * o] + self.sum[2 * o + 1]

    def _range_sum(self, o, lo, hi, l, r):
        if r <= lo or hi <= l: return 0
        if l <= lo and hi <= r: return self.sum[o]
        self._push(o, lo, hi)
        mid = (lo + hi) // 2
        return self._range_sum(2 * o, lo, mid, l, r) + self._range_sum(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, l, r)

    def range_add(self, l, r, d):                # on [l, r)
        self._range_add(1, 0, self.n, l, r, d)

    def range_sum(self, l, r):
        return self._range_sum(1, 0, self.n, l, r)
--cpp
struct LazySeg {
    int n; vector<long long> sum, add;
    LazySeg(const vector<int>& a) : n(a.size()), sum(4 * a.size()), add(4 * a.size()) { build(1, 0, n, a); }

    void build(int o, int lo, int hi, const vector<int>& a) {
        if (hi - lo == 1) { sum[o] = a[lo]; return; }
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        build(2 * o, lo, mid, a); build(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, a);
        sum[o] = sum[2 * o] + sum[2 * o + 1];
    }
    void apply(int o, int lo, int hi, long long d) { sum[o] += d * (hi - lo); add[o] += d; }   // note only
    void push(int o, int lo, int hi) {                        // hand the note to the children
        if (add[o] == 0) return;
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        apply(2 * o, lo, mid, add[o]); apply(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, add[o]);
        add[o] = 0;
    }
    void rangeAdd(int o, int lo, int hi, int l, int r, long long d) {
        if (r <= lo || hi <= l) return;
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r) { apply(o, lo, hi, d); return; }
        push(o, lo, hi);
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        rangeAdd(2 * o, lo, mid, l, r, d); rangeAdd(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, l, r, d);
        sum[o] = sum[2 * o] + sum[2 * o + 1];
    }
    long long rangeSum(int o, int lo, int hi, int l, int r) {
        if (r <= lo || hi <= l) return 0;
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r) return sum[o];
        push(o, lo, hi);
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        return rangeSum(2 * o, lo, mid, l, r) + rangeSum(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, l, r);
    }
    void rangeAdd(int l, int r, long long d) { rangeAdd(1, 0, n, l, r, d); }   // on [l, r)
    long long rangeSum(int l, int r) { return rangeSum(1, 0, n, l, r); }
};

### treap
--idea
A treap is a binary search tree that stays balanced by using random numbers. Each node holds a key (the search order) and a random priority. The tree obeys two rules at once: keys are in search order (smaller keys on the left), and priorities are in heap order (a parent has a higher priority than its children). The name is "tree" plus "heap".
Given the keys and the priorities, exactly one tree fits both rules, and it is the same tree you would get by inserting the keys in order of decreasing priority. Since the priorities are random, that is like inserting in a random order, and random insertion order gives a tree of height about `2 ln n` on average. So you get balance without rotations rules or colours, just two small operations: `split` and `merge`.
--build
1. A node holds `key`, `pri` (a random integer drawn when the node is created, code piece `u-rand`), `left`, `right`.
2. `split(t, k)` cuts the tree `t` into two trees: `A` with all keys `< k` and `B` with all keys `>= k`. If `t` is empty, both are empty. If `t.key < k`, then `t` and its left subtree belong to `A`: split `t.right` into `(a, b)`, set `t.right = a`, and return `(t, b)`. Otherwise `t` and its right subtree belong to `B`: split `t.left` into `(a, b)`, set `t.left = b`, and return `(a, t)`.
3. `merge(a, b)` joins two trees where every key in `a` is smaller than every key in `b`. If either is empty return the other. If `a.pri > b.pri`, `a` is the root: set `a.right = merge(a.right, b)` and return `a`. Otherwise `b` is the root: set `b.left = merge(a, b.left)` and return `b`. The higher priority always stays on top, which keeps the heap rule.
4. `insert(t, k)`: split `t` at `k` into `(a, b)`, then return `merge(merge(a, newNode(k)), b)`. (Check that `k` is not already there first if you want a set.)
5. `erase(t, k)`: split at `k` into `(a, b)`, split `b` at `k + 1` into `(mid, c)`, throw `mid` away (it holds only the key `k`), and return `merge(a, c)`.
6. Invariant: both the search order and the heap order hold for every node, before and after every operation. `split` and `merge` each walk down one path, so they cost the tree height.
7. Variations: store subtree `size` in each node (updated after every `split` and `merge` step) to get k-th smallest. Split by position instead of key to get an implicit treap, which is a sequence you can cut, join and reverse (the rope idea).
--think
- Signal: you need an ordered set with insert, delete, and also "cut the sequence here and glue pieces together". Split and merge make treaps the simplest balanced tree for problems on sequences: reverse a range, move a block, range operations with lazy tags.
- Decide first whether the tree is ordered by key (a set or map) or by position (an implicit treap, where the key is the index and is never stored). The code is the same, only the split rule changes: "first `k` items" instead of "keys `< k`".
- Neighbouring techniques: AVL and red-black trees give worst-case guarantees but need rotation cases. A skip list is the other randomised structure. In contests with only inserts and finds, a built-in ordered set is shorter.
- Why random priorities matter: if priorities were not random (say, equal to the keys), the treap would be the plain unbalanced tree. The adversary cannot hurt you if they cannot predict your random numbers.
- Pitfalls: recursion depth (fine, the expected depth is logarithmic), forgetting to update sizes or lazy tags inside `split` and `merge`, using a weak random source, and equal keys (decide whether they are allowed, and where they go).
--cost
Expected O(log n) for split, merge, insert, erase and search. Space O(n). The expected height is about 2 ln n.
--java
static class Node {
    int key, pri; Node left, right;
    Node(int k) { key = k; pri = randInt(0, 1_000_000_000); }   // piece u-rand
}

static Node[] split(Node t, int k) {             // {keys < k, keys >= k}
    if (t == null) return new Node[]{null, null};
    if (t.key < k) {
        Node[] p = split(t.right, k);
        t.right = p[0];
        return new Node[]{t, p[1]};
    }
    Node[] p = split(t.left, k);
    t.left = p[1];
    return new Node[]{p[0], t};
}

static Node merge(Node a, Node b) {              // every key in a is smaller than every key in b
    if (a == null) return b;
    if (b == null) return a;
    if (a.pri > b.pri) { a.right = merge(a.right, b); return a; }
    b.left = merge(a, b.left);
    return b;
}

static boolean contains(Node t, int k) {
    while (t != null && t.key != k) t = k < t.key ? t.left : t.right;
    return t != null;
}

static Node insert(Node t, int k) {
    if (contains(t, k)) return t;
    Node[] p = split(t, k);
    return merge(merge(p[0], new Node(k)), p[1]);
}

static Node erase(Node t, int k) {
    Node[] p = split(t, k), q = split(p[1], k + 1);   // q[0] holds only the key k
    return merge(p[0], q[1]);
}
--py
class Node:
    def __init__(self, key):
        self.key = key
        self.pri = rand_int(0, 10**9)             # piece u-rand
        self.left = self.right = None

def split(t, k):
    """Return (keys < k, keys >= k)."""
    if t is None: return None, None
    if t.key < k:
        a, b = split(t.right, k)
        t.right = a
        return t, b
    a, b = split(t.left, k)
    t.left = b
    return a, t

def merge(a, b):
    """Every key in a is smaller than every key in b."""
    if a is None: return b
    if b is None: return a
    if a.pri > b.pri:
        a.right = merge(a.right, b)
        return a
    b.left = merge(a, b.left)
    return b

def contains(t, k):
    while t and t.key != k:
        t = t.left if k < t.key else t.right
    return t is not None

def insert(t, k):
    if contains(t, k): return t
    a, b = split(t, k)
    return merge(merge(a, Node(k)), b)

def erase(t, k):
    a, b = split(t, k)
    _, c = split(b, k + 1)                         # the dropped middle holds only the key k
    return merge(a, c)
--cpp
struct Node {
    int key, pri; Node *left = nullptr, *right = nullptr;
    Node(int k) : key(k), pri(randInt(0, 1000000000)) {}     // piece u-rand
};

pair<Node*, Node*> split(Node* t, int k) {       // {keys < k, keys >= k}
    if (!t) return {nullptr, nullptr};
    if (t->key < k) {
        auto [a, b] = split(t->right, k);
        t->right = a;
        return {t, b};
    }
    auto [a, b] = split(t->left, k);
    t->left = b;
    return {a, t};
}

Node* merge(Node* a, Node* b) {                  // every key in a is smaller than every key in b
    if (!a) return b;
    if (!b) return a;
    if (a->pri > b->pri) { a->right = merge(a->right, b); return a; }
    b->left = merge(a, b->left);
    return b;
}

bool contains(Node* t, int k) {
    while (t && t->key != k) t = k < t->key ? t->left : t->right;
    return t != nullptr;
}

Node* insert(Node* t, int k) {
    if (contains(t, k)) return t;
    auto [a, b] = split(t, k);
    return merge(merge(a, new Node(k)), b);
}

Node* erase(Node* t, int k) {
    auto [a, b] = split(t, k);
    auto [mid, c] = split(b, k + 1);             // mid holds only the key k
    return merge(a, c);
}
--uses
u-rand

### splay
--idea
A splay tree is a binary search tree with no balance information at all. Every time you touch a key (find, insert or delete it), the tree rotates that key all the way up to the root. Keys used often stay near the top, and keys not used for a long time sink. It adapts to the access pattern, like a desk where the paper you just used is always on top.
A single operation can be slow (O(n) on a skinny tree), but rotating the accessed node up also flattens the path it came along. The famous result is that any sequence of `m` operations on a tree of `n` keys costs O(m log n) in total. Each operation is O(log n) on average over the sequence, which is called amortised cost.
--build
1. A node has `key`, `left`, `right`. No colours, heights or priorities.
2. Rotations: `rotR(t)` lifts the left child above `t`: `x = t.left; t.left = x.right; x.right = t; return x`. `rotL(t)` is the mirror.
3. `splay(t, k)` returns a tree whose root is the node with key `k`, or if `k` is missing, the last node on the search path (its neighbour). It works recursively by looking two levels down.
4. Case zig-zig (the key is in the left-left grandchild's range): `t.left.left = splay(t.left.left, k)`, then `rotR(t)`. The second rotation, done after the return, lifts the child above the root. Do the mirror for the right side. Doing the grandparent first is what gives the amortised bound; plain "rotate to the top" does not.
5. Case zig-zag (key is in the left-right grandchild's range): `t.left.right = splay(t.left.right, k)`, then if it exists `t.left = rotL(t.left)`. Mirror for right-left.
6. Finish: after the double step, one more `rotR(t)` (if the left child exists) puts the key on top. Mirror for the right.
7. `contains(k)`: `root = splay(root, k)`, then check `root.key == k`. Searching changes the tree.
8. `insert(k)`: `root = splay(root, k)`. If `root.key == k` stop. Otherwise make a new node `n` with key `k`. If `k < root.key`, `n.right = root`, `n.left = root.left`, `root.left = null`. Else mirror. `n` becomes the root.
9. `erase(k)`: splay `k` to the root. If it is not there stop. If there is no left subtree, the right subtree is the new tree. Otherwise splay the left subtree at `k` (which is bigger than everything in it, so its largest node comes to the top and has no right child), attach the right subtree there.
--think
- Signal: access patterns are skewed (some keys used far more), or you need split and join of ordered sets, or you will build a link-cut tree (which uses splay trees to store paths). "Recently used items are fast" is the hint.
- Decide first whether you need worst-case guarantees. A splay tree gives only amortised ones: a single slow call can happen. In a real-time system or in a persistent setting (old versions are kept), use another tree, because splaying changes the tree even on a read.
- Neighbouring techniques: a treap or AVL for a steady O(log n). A B-tree for disks. A skip list for concurrency. A hash table for plain lookups.
- Invariant to remember: after `splay(t, k)` the search order still holds, since rotations never change the in-order sequence of keys. Only the shape changes.
- Pitfalls: forgetting that reads modify the tree (not safe to share between threads), recursion depth on a skinny tree (the first access after many sorted inserts can be deep, so use an iterative top-down splay in production), and wrong zig-zig versus zig-zag choice.
--cost
O(log n) amortised per operation (any sequence of `m` operations takes O(m log n)), O(n) worst case for a single one. Space O(n), one node per key.
--java
static class Node {
    int key; Node left, right;
    Node(int k) { key = k; }
}

static class SplayTree {
    Node root;

    Node rotR(Node t) { Node x = t.left; t.left = x.right; x.right = t; return x; }
    Node rotL(Node t) { Node x = t.right; t.right = x.left; x.left = t; return x; }

    Node splay(Node t, int k) {                    // key k (or its neighbour) becomes the root
        if (t == null || t.key == k) return t;
        if (k < t.key) {
            if (t.left == null) return t;
            if (k < t.left.key) { t.left.left = splay(t.left.left, k); t = rotR(t); }          // zig-zig
            else if (k > t.left.key) {                                                         // zig-zag
                t.left.right = splay(t.left.right, k);
                if (t.left.right != null) t.left = rotL(t.left);
            }
            return t.left == null ? t : rotR(t);
        }
        if (t.right == null) return t;
        if (k > t.right.key) { t.right.right = splay(t.right.right, k); t = rotL(t); }         // zig-zig
        else if (k < t.right.key) {                                                            // zig-zag
            t.right.left = splay(t.right.left, k);
            if (t.right.left != null) t.right = rotR(t.right);
        }
        return t.right == null ? t : rotL(t);
    }

    boolean contains(int k) { root = splay(root, k); return root != null && root.key == k; }

    void insert(int k) {
        root = splay(root, k);
        if (root != null && root.key == k) return;
        Node n = new Node(k);
        if (root == null) { root = n; return; }
        if (k < root.key) { n.right = root; n.left = root.left; root.left = null; }
        else { n.left = root; n.right = root.right; root.right = null; }
        root = n;
    }

    void erase(int k) {
        root = splay(root, k);
        if (root == null || root.key != k) return;
        if (root.left == null) { root = root.right; return; }
        Node l = splay(root.left, k);              // the largest key of the left part comes to its top
        l.right = root.right;
        root = l;
    }
}
--py
class Node:
    def __init__(self, key):
        self.key = key
