        self.left = self.right = None

def rot_r(t):
    x = t.left
    t.left, x.right = x.right, t
    return x

def rot_l(t):
    x = t.right
    t.right, x.left = x.left, t
    return x

def splay(t, k):
    """Key k (or its neighbour) becomes the root of the returned tree."""
    if t is None or t.key == k: return t
    if k < t.key:
        if t.left is None: return t
        if k < t.left.key:                                    # zig-zig
            t.left.left = splay(t.left.left, k)
            t = rot_r(t)
        elif k > t.left.key:                                  # zig-zag
            t.left.right = splay(t.left.right, k)
            if t.left.right: t.left = rot_l(t.left)
        return t if t.left is None else rot_r(t)
    if t.right is None: return t
    if k > t.right.key:                                       # zig-zig
        t.right.right = splay(t.right.right, k)
        t = rot_l(t)
    elif k < t.right.key:                                     # zig-zag
        t.right.left = splay(t.right.left, k)
        if t.right.left: t.right = rot_r(t.right)
    return t if t.right is None else rot_l(t)

class SplayTree:
    def __init__(self):
        self.root = None

    def contains(self, k):
        self.root = splay(self.root, k)
        return self.root is not None and self.root.key == k

    def insert(self, k):
        self.root = splay(self.root, k)
        if self.root and self.root.key == k: return
        n = Node(k)
        if self.root is None:
            self.root = n; return
        if k < self.root.key:
            n.right, n.left, self.root.left = self.root, self.root.left, None
        else:
            n.left, n.right, self.root.right = self.root, self.root.right, None
        self.root = n

    def erase(self, k):
        self.root = splay(self.root, k)
        if self.root is None or self.root.key != k: return
        if self.root.left is None:
            self.root = self.root.right; return
        l = splay(self.root.left, k)               # the largest key of the left part comes to its top
        l.right = self.root.right
        self.root = l
--cpp
struct Node {
    int key; Node *left = nullptr, *right = nullptr;
    Node(int k) : key(k) {}
};

struct SplayTree {
    Node* root = nullptr;

    Node* rotR(Node* t) { Node* x = t->left; t->left = x->right; x->right = t; return x; }
    Node* rotL(Node* t) { Node* x = t->right; t->right = x->left; x->left = t; return x; }

    Node* splay(Node* t, int k) {                  // key k (or its neighbour) becomes the root
        if (!t || t->key == k) return t;
        if (k < t->key) {
            if (!t->left) return t;
            if (k < t->left->key) { t->left->left = splay(t->left->left, k); t = rotR(t); }   // zig-zig
            else if (k > t->left->key) {                                                       // zig-zag
                t->left->right = splay(t->left->right, k);
                if (t->left->right) t->left = rotL(t->left);
            }
            return t->left ? rotR(t) : t;
        }
        if (!t->right) return t;
        if (k > t->right->key) { t->right->right = splay(t->right->right, k); t = rotL(t); }   // zig-zig
        else if (k < t->right->key) {                                                          // zig-zag
            t->right->left = splay(t->right->left, k);
            if (t->right->left) t->right = rotR(t->right);
        }
        return t->right ? rotL(t) : t;
    }

    bool contains(int k) { root = splay(root, k); return root && root->key == k; }

    void insert(int k) {
        root = splay(root, k);
        if (root && root->key == k) return;
        Node* n = new Node(k);
        if (!root) { root = n; return; }
        if (k < root->key) { n->right = root; n->left = root->left; root->left = nullptr; }
        else { n->left = root; n->right = root->right; root->right = nullptr; }
        root = n;
    }

    void erase(int k) {
        root = splay(root, k);
        if (!root || root->key != k) return;
        if (!root->left) { root = root->right; return; }
        Node* l = splay(root->left, k);            // the largest key of the left part comes to its top
        l->right = root->right;
        root = l;
    }
};

### persist
--idea
A persistent data structure keeps every past version of itself. After an update you still hold the old structure and can query it. A persistent segment tree does this without copying the whole tree on each update: it copies only the one path from the root to the changed leaf (about `log n` nodes) and shares every other node with the older version.
Picture a family tree drawn on paper that you are not allowed to erase. To record one person's change, you write new copies of that person and of each ancestor up to the root, and let all the untouched relatives be shared by pointing at the old ones. Each version is then identified by its own root. Querying version `v` just starts from root `v`.
--build
1. Store nodes in arrays (a pool) rather than as objects: `lc[x]`, `rc[x]` (left and right child ids), `cnt[x]` (a summary for the node, here a count). Node 0 is the shared empty node: `lc[0] = rc[0] = 0`, `cnt[0] = 0`. Every missing subtree points at 0.
2. A node `x` covers a value range `[lo, hi)` that is known from the recursion, not stored.
3. `insert(prev, lo, hi, v)` builds a new version of `prev` that has one more item with value `v`. Allocate a new node `cur` as a copy of `prev` (`lc`, `rc` copied) with `cnt[cur] = cnt[prev] + 1`. If `hi - lo == 1` return `cur`. Otherwise, with `mid = (lo + hi) / 2`, if `v < mid` set `lc[cur] = insert(lc[prev], lo, mid, v)`, else set `rc[cur] = insert(rc[prev], mid, hi, v)`. Return `cur`. The other child stays shared.
4. Build versions of an array `a` of values in `[0, m)`: `roots[0] = 0` (empty) and `roots[i + 1] = insert(roots[i], 0, m, a[i])`. Version `i` then describes the first `i` items.
5. Because `cnt` subtracts, versions `l` and `r` give the contents of the slice `a[l .. r)`: the count in any value range for that slice is `cnt` in version `r` minus `cnt` in version `l`.
6. `kth(l, r, k)` (k-th smallest in `a[l..r)`, k from 1): set `A = roots[l]`, `B = roots[r]`, range `[0, m)`. Repeat until the range has one value: `inLeft = cnt[lc[B]] - cnt[lc[A]]`. If `k <= inLeft` go left in both (`A = lc[A]`, `B = lc[B]`, shrink to the left half). Otherwise `k -= inLeft` and go right. The value reached is the answer.
7. Memory: each insert allocates one node per level, about `log2(m) + 1`. The pool needs `n x (log2(m) + 1) + 1` nodes in total.
8. For a persistent "point assign and range sum" tree the idea is identical: the update copies the path and stores a new sum in the copied nodes.
--think
- Signal: "queries about an old version", "undo", "k-th smallest in a range" (online, no offline trick), "count of values in `[a, b]` among positions `l..r`", "number of distinct values in a range" (with a twist). Any problem where one structure is queried at many different points in its history.
- Decide first what the versions mean. For range queries over an array, version `i` is "the prefix of the first `i` items", and a range is the difference of two versions. For time travel, version `t` is the state after the `t`-th update.
- Neighbouring techniques: a merge sort tree answers similar range questions with `log^2` time and `n log n` memory. A wavelet tree does it with bit arrays. Offline, a Fenwick tree with sorted queries is smaller. Use the persistent tree when queries must be answered online and the structure has many versions.
- Why nodes can be shared: nodes are never changed after creation. All changes create new nodes, so an old root always sees exactly the old data.
- Pitfalls: forgetting that every update copies a node at every level (pool size), changing a node in place (this destroys older versions), using `cnt[0]` before node 0 is set up as the empty node, and an off-by-one between "version `i` has `i` items" and "positions `l..r` inclusive".
--cost
Each update O(log m) time and O(log m) new nodes. Each query O(log m). Total memory O(n log m).
--java
static class PersistentSeg {
    int m, used = 1;                                // node 0 is the shared empty node
    int[] lc, rc, cnt, roots;

    PersistentSeg(int[] a, int m) {                 // values must be in [0, m)
        this.m = m;
        int levels = 1;
        while ((1 << (levels - 1)) < m) levels++;   // nodes created per insert
        int cap = a.length * levels + 1;
        lc = new int[cap]; rc = new int[cap]; cnt = new int[cap];
        roots = new int[a.length + 1];
        for (int i = 0; i < a.length; i++) roots[i + 1] = insert(roots[i], 0, m, a[i]);
    }

    int insert(int prev, int lo, int hi, int v) {   // new version of prev with one more v
        int cur = used++;
        lc[cur] = lc[prev]; rc[cur] = rc[prev]; cnt[cur] = cnt[prev] + 1;   // copy, never edit prev
        if (hi - lo == 1) return cur;
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        if (v < mid) lc[cur] = insert(lc[prev], lo, mid, v);
        else rc[cur] = insert(rc[prev], mid, hi, v);
        return cur;
    }

    int kth(int l, int r, int k) {                  // k-th smallest (k from 1) in a[l, r)
        int A = roots[l], B = roots[r], lo = 0, hi = m;
        while (hi - lo > 1) {
            int mid = (lo + hi) / 2, inLeft = cnt[lc[B]] - cnt[lc[A]];
            if (k <= inLeft) { A = lc[A]; B = lc[B]; hi = mid; }
            else { k -= inLeft; A = rc[A]; B = rc[B]; lo = mid; }
        }
        return lo;
    }
}
--py
class PersistentSeg:
    def __init__(self, a, m):                       # values must be in [0, m)
        self.m = m
        self.lc, self.rc, self.cnt = [0], [0], [0]  # node 0 is the shared empty node
        self.roots = [0]
        for x in a:
            self.roots.append(self._insert(self.roots[-1], 0, m, x))

    def _insert(self, prev, lo, hi, v):             # new version of prev with one more v
        cur = len(self.lc)
        self.lc.append(self.lc[prev]); self.rc.append(self.rc[prev]); self.cnt.append(self.cnt[prev] + 1)
        if hi - lo == 1: return cur                 # copy, never edit prev
        mid = (lo + hi) // 2
        if v < mid: self.lc[cur] = self._insert(self.lc[prev], lo, mid, v)
        else: self.rc[cur] = self._insert(self.rc[prev], mid, hi, v)
        return cur

    def kth(self, l, r, k):                         # k-th smallest (k from 1) in a[l, r)
        A, B, lo, hi = self.roots[l], self.roots[r], 0, self.m
        while hi - lo > 1:
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            in_left = self.cnt[self.lc[B]] - self.cnt[self.lc[A]]
            if k <= in_left:
                A, B, hi = self.lc[A], self.lc[B], mid
            else:
                k -= in_left
                A, B, lo = self.rc[A], self.rc[B], mid
        return lo
--cpp
struct PersistentSeg {
    int m;
    vector<int> lc{0}, rc{0}, cnt{0}, roots{0};     // node 0 is the shared empty node

    PersistentSeg(const vector<int>& a, int m) : m(m) {   // values must be in [0, m)
        for (int x : a) roots.push_back(insert(roots.back(), 0, m, x));
    }

    int insert(int prev, int lo, int hi, int v) {   // new version of prev with one more v
        int cur = lc.size();
        lc.push_back(lc[prev]); rc.push_back(rc[prev]); cnt.push_back(cnt[prev] + 1);   // copy, never edit prev
        if (hi - lo == 1) return cur;
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        if (v < mid) { int c = insert(lc[prev], lo, mid, v); lc[cur] = c; }
        else { int c = insert(rc[prev], mid, hi, v); rc[cur] = c; }
        return cur;
    }

    int kth(int l, int r, int k) const {            // k-th smallest (k from 1) in a[l, r)
        int A = roots[l], B = roots[r], lo = 0, hi = m;
        while (hi - lo > 1) {
            int mid = (lo + hi) / 2, inLeft = cnt[lc[B]] - cnt[lc[A]];
            if (k <= inLeft) { A = lc[A]; B = lc[B]; hi = mid; }
            else { k -= inLeft; A = rc[A]; B = rc[B]; lo = mid; }
        }
        return lo;
    }
};

### dynseg
--idea
A dynamic segment tree is a segment tree over a huge range of positions (say 0 to a billion) that only creates a node when something is stored under it. A normal segment tree over a billion positions would need billions of nodes. If you only ever touch 100,000 positions, a dynamic tree uses about 100,000 times 30 nodes, and the untouched parts simply do not exist.
Treat a missing node as "all zeros" (or whatever the neutral value is). A query that reaches a missing node can return the neutral value at once. An update that reaches a missing node creates it, and its children are created only if the update has to go further down. It is the same tree, drawn lazily.
--build
1. Pick the position range `[0, N)`. A power of two such as `N = 2^30` is convenient. Node 0 is a null marker meaning "does not exist". The root is node 1.
2. Store nodes in growing arrays: `lc[x]`, `rc[x]` (child ids, 0 means none) and `sum[x]` (the sum over the node's range). Start with node 0 and node 1 already in them.
3. `add(x, lo, hi, pos, d)` adds `d` at position `pos` in the node `x` covering `[lo, hi)`: `sum[x] += d`. If `hi - lo == 1` stop. Otherwise find the half `pos` is in. If that child is 0, create a new empty node and store its id in `lc[x]` or `rc[x]`. Recurse into it.
4. `query(x, lo, hi, l, r)` returns the sum over `[l, r)`: if `x == 0` or the node's range does not meet `[l, r)`, return 0. If it is inside `[l, r)`, return `sum[x]`. Otherwise add the answers of the two children.
5. Number of nodes: each update creates at most `log2(N)` new nodes, so `U` updates create at most `U x log2(N)` nodes. Use growable arrays so you never need to guess in advance.
6. Range updates with a lazy tag work the same way: a tag stored at a node, and children created when a tag has to be pushed down. This is how "assign on a huge range" or "add on a huge range" is done.
7. Invariant: a node exists if and only if some update touched a position below it, and `sum[x]` is the sum of every update below it.
--think
- Signal: coordinates up to `10^9` (or `10^18`) with online updates, so you cannot compress them in advance. "Number of points in a range of positions, points arrive online." Also range add and range max over huge index spaces.
- Decide first whether the updates are known in advance. If yes, compress the coordinates and use an ordinary segment tree or a Fenwick tree, which is smaller and faster. A dynamic tree is for when you must answer online.
- Neighbouring techniques: an ordered map handles point updates but not range sums quickly. A persistent segment tree is built the same way (nodes created on demand) but keeps old versions. A treap with sizes also answers rank questions on huge ranges.
- Memory is the usual limit: `30` nodes per distinct updated position, each with three integers. For `10^6` updates that is `3 x 10^7` integers. Estimate it before choosing.
- Pitfalls: forgetting to create the node before descending, reading `sum[0]` (node 0 must stay zero, never modify it), using `int` for positions that need `long`, and an overflow in `lo + hi` (use `lo + (hi - lo) / 2`).
--cost
Update and query O(log N) each. Memory O(U log N) for `U` updates (not O(N)).
--java
static class DynSeg {
    final int N = 1 << 30;                          // positions 0 .. N-1
    int[] lc = new int[1024], rc = new int[1024];
    long[] sum = new long[1024];
    int used = 2;                                   // node 0 = none, node 1 = root

    int newNode() {
        if (used == lc.length) {                    // grow all arrays together
            lc = java.util.Arrays.copyOf(lc, used * 2);
            rc = java.util.Arrays.copyOf(rc, used * 2);
            sum = java.util.Arrays.copyOf(sum, used * 2);
        }
        return used++;
    }

    void add(int pos, long d) {                     // iterative: walk down, creating missing nodes
        int x = 1, lo = 0, hi = N;
        while (true) {
            sum[x] += d;
            if (hi - lo == 1) return;
            int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
            if (pos < mid) { if (lc[x] == 0) { int c = newNode(); lc[x] = c; } x = lc[x]; hi = mid; }
            else           { if (rc[x] == 0) { int c = newNode(); rc[x] = c; } x = rc[x]; lo = mid; }
        }
    }

    long query(int x, int lo, int hi, int l, int r) {   // sum over [l, r)
        if (x == 0 || r <= lo || hi <= l) return 0;        // missing node means all zeros
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r) return sum[x];
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
        return query(lc[x], lo, mid, l, r) + query(rc[x], mid, hi, l, r);
    }
    long query(int l, int r) { return query(1, 0, N, l, r); }
}
--py
class DynSeg:
    N = 1 << 30                                     # positions 0 .. N-1

    def __init__(self):
        self.lc, self.rc, self.sum = [0, 0], [0, 0], [0, 0]   # node 0 = none, node 1 = root

    def _new_node(self):
        self.lc.append(0); self.rc.append(0); self.sum.append(0)
        return len(self.lc) - 1

    def add(self, pos, d):                          # walk down, creating missing nodes
        x, lo, hi = 1, 0, self.N
        while True:
            self.sum[x] += d
            if hi - lo == 1: return
            mid = lo + (hi - lo) // 2
            if pos < mid:
                if self.lc[x] == 0: self.lc[x] = self._new_node()
                x, hi = self.lc[x], mid
            else:
                if self.rc[x] == 0: self.rc[x] = self._new_node()
                x, lo = self.rc[x], mid

    def _query(self, x, lo, hi, l, r):              # sum over [l, r)
        if x == 0 or r <= lo or hi <= l: return 0   # missing node means all zeros
        if l <= lo and hi <= r: return self.sum[x]
        mid = lo + (hi - lo) // 2
        return self._query(self.lc[x], lo, mid, l, r) + self._query(self.rc[x], mid, hi, l, r)

    def query(self, l, r):
        return self._query(1, 0, self.N, l, r)
--cpp
struct DynSeg {
    static const int N = 1 << 30;                   // positions 0 .. N-1
    vector<int> lc{0, 0}, rc{0, 0};                 // node 0 = none, node 1 = root
    vector<long long> sum{0, 0};

    int newNode() { lc.push_back(0); rc.push_back(0); sum.push_back(0); return lc.size() - 1; }

    void add(int pos, long long d) {                // walk down, creating missing nodes
        int x = 1, lo = 0, hi = N;
        while (true) {
            sum[x] += d;
            if (hi - lo == 1) return;
            int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
            if (pos < mid) { if (!lc[x]) { int c = newNode(); lc[x] = c; } x = lc[x]; hi = mid; }
            else           { if (!rc[x]) { int c = newNode(); rc[x] = c; } x = rc[x]; lo = mid; }
        }
    }

    long long query(int x, int lo, int hi, int l, int r) const {   // sum over [l, r)
        if (!x || r <= lo || hi <= l) return 0;                     // missing node means all zeros
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r) return sum[x];
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
        return query(lc[x], lo, mid, l, r) + query(rc[x], mid, hi, l, r);
    }
    long long query(int l, int r) const { return query(1, 0, N, l, r); }
};

### beats
--idea
Segment tree beats handles range updates that are not simple to tag, the main example being "for every item in the range, replace it by `min(item, x)`" (a range chmin). With the ordinary lazy tags of a segment tree this fails, because the new sum depends on how many items were above `x`, and the node does not know.
The trick is to store, for every node, not just the sum but also the largest value `mx`, the second largest value `se` (strictly smaller than `mx`), and `cn`, how many items equal `mx`. If `x` is bigger than `se` but smaller than `mx`, then only the items equal to the maximum change, and all of them become `x`. The sum drops by exactly `(mx - x) * cn`. That is an update that can be applied to the node in O(1) and left as a tag. If `x <= se`, you have to go into the children. Each such descent merges values that were different into equal ones, which can only happen a limited number of times, giving the amortised bound.
--build
1. Build a segment tree over `[0, n)`. In each node `o` store `sum[o]`, `mx[o]` (maximum), `se[o]` (strict second maximum, minus infinity if there is none), `cn[o]` (count of items equal to `mx[o]`).
2. Leaf: `sum = value`, `mx = value`, `se = -infinity`, `cn = 1`.
3. `pull(o)` combines the two children `a = 2o` and `b = 2o + 1`: `sum[o] = sum[a] + sum[b]`. If `mx[a] > mx[b]`: `mx[o] = mx[a]`, `cn[o] = cn[a]`, `se[o] = max(se[a], mx[b])`. If `mx[a] < mx[b]`: the mirror. If equal: `mx[o] = mx[a]`, `cn[o] = cn[a] + cn[b]`, `se[o] = max(se[a], se[b])`.
4. `applyMin(o, x)`: if `x < mx[o]`, then `sum[o] -= (mx[o] - x) * cn[o]` and `mx[o] = x`. The caller guarantees `x > se[o]`, so only the maximum items change. The tag is implicit: the node's own `mx` is the tag.
5. `push(o)`: pass the node's maximum down: `applyMin(2o, mx[o])` and `applyMin(2o + 1, mx[o])`. A child whose maximum is already at most `mx[o]` is unchanged, so this is safe even with no pending update.
6. `chmin(o, lo, hi, l, r, x)`: return if the range misses `[l, r)` or `mx[o] <= x` (nothing is bigger than `x`). If the node is inside `[l, r)` and `se[o] < x`, call `applyMin(o, x)` and return. Otherwise `push(o)`, recurse into both children, then `pull(o)`.
7. Queries are as in any segment tree: `rangeSum` returns `sum[o]` for fully covered nodes, `rangeMax` returns `mx[o]`. Both call `push(o)` before going down.
8. Invariant: `mx`, `se`, `cn` and `sum` of a node are correct for the node's own range assuming everything above has been pushed. The only place that touches a node in O(1) is step 4, and it requires `se[o] < x < mx[o]`, which keeps `se` and `cn` valid.
--think
- Signal: "set `a[i] = min(a[i], x)` for all `i` in `[l, r]`" together with range sum or range max queries. Also range chmax, range "divide by `x`" (floor), "replace by its square root". The word "chmin" or a clamp.
- Decide first which updates you need. Chmin alone with sum and max queries is the base case above. Adding range add as well needs extra tags, and the proof of speed becomes harder (the bound is `O(log^2 n)` amortised instead of `O(log n)`).
- Neighbouring techniques: an ordinary lazy segment tree if the update is add or assign. Sqrt decomposition for odd updates with a weaker but simpler bound. For "floor-divide by x" or "square root" updates, each item can change only a few times (about 6 to 30), so a plain segment tree that recurses to leaves unless the range is already stable also works (a close cousin of beats).
- Why it stays fast: define the potential as the number of distinct values stored in all nodes. Each extra descent in step 6 reduces the number of distinct values in a node (the maximum and second maximum collapse into one). Only updates add distinct values, and they add at most `log n` each.
- Pitfalls: using `se[o] <= x` instead of `se[o] < x` in step 6 (then `cn` and `se` can break), forgetting `push` before recursing, mixing up `-infinity` with a real value (use the smallest integer), and `int` overflow in the sum.
--cost
Chmin, range sum and range max are O(log n) amortised for chmin and queries only (with range add also, O(log^2 n) amortised). Build O(n). Space O(n).
--java
static class Beats {
    static final int NEG = Integer.MIN_VALUE;           // "minus infinity"
    int n; long[] sum; int[] mx, se, cn;

    Beats(int[] a) {
        n = a.length;
        sum = new long[4 * n]; mx = new int[4 * n]; se = new int[4 * n]; cn = new int[4 * n];
        build(1, 0, n, a);
    }
    void build(int o, int lo, int hi, int[] a) {
        if (hi - lo == 1) { sum[o] = mx[o] = a[lo]; se[o] = NEG; cn[o] = 1; return; }
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        build(2 * o, lo, mid, a); build(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, a);
        pull(o);
    }
    void pull(int o) {
        int a = 2 * o, b = 2 * o + 1;
        sum[o] = sum[a] + sum[b];
        if (mx[a] > mx[b]) { mx[o] = mx[a]; cn[o] = cn[a]; se[o] = Math.max(se[a], mx[b]); }
        else if (mx[a] < mx[b]) { mx[o] = mx[b]; cn[o] = cn[b]; se[o] = Math.max(mx[a], se[b]); }
        else { mx[o] = mx[a]; cn[o] = cn[a] + cn[b]; se[o] = Math.max(se[a], se[b]); }
    }
    void applyMin(int o, int x) {                        // only valid when x > se[o]
        if (x < mx[o]) { sum[o] -= (long) (mx[o] - x) * cn[o]; mx[o] = x; }
    }
    void push(int o) { applyMin(2 * o, mx[o]); applyMin(2 * o + 1, mx[o]); }

    void chmin(int o, int lo, int hi, int l, int r, int x) {
        if (r <= lo || hi <= l || mx[o] <= x) return;
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r && se[o] < x) { applyMin(o, x); return; }
        push(o);
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        chmin(2 * o, lo, mid, l, r, x); chmin(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, l, r, x);
        pull(o);
    }
    long rangeSum(int o, int lo, int hi, int l, int r) {
        if (r <= lo || hi <= l) return 0;
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r) return sum[o];
        push(o);
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        return rangeSum(2 * o, lo, mid, l, r) + rangeSum(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, l, r);
    }
    void chmin(int l, int r, int x) { chmin(1, 0, n, l, r, x); }       // on [l, r)
    long rangeSum(int l, int r) { return rangeSum(1, 0, n, l, r); }
}
--py
NEG = float('-inf')

class Beats:
    def __init__(self, a):
        self.n = len(a)
        size = 4 * self.n
        self.sum, self.mx, self.se, self.cn = [0] * size, [0] * size, [NEG] * size, [0] * size
        self._build(1, 0, self.n, a)

    def _build(self, o, lo, hi, a):
        if hi - lo == 1:
            self.sum[o] = self.mx[o] = a[lo]; self.se[o] = NEG; self.cn[o] = 1
            return
        mid = (lo + hi) // 2
        self._build(2 * o, lo, mid, a); self._build(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, a)
        self._pull(o)

    def _pull(self, o):
        a, b = 2 * o, 2 * o + 1
        mx, se, cn = self.mx, self.se, self.cn
        self.sum[o] = self.sum[a] + self.sum[b]
        if mx[a] > mx[b]:
            mx[o], cn[o], se[o] = mx[a], cn[a], max(se[a], mx[b])
        elif mx[a] < mx[b]:
            mx[o], cn[o], se[o] = mx[b], cn[b], max(mx[a], se[b])
        else:
            mx[o], cn[o], se[o] = mx[a], cn[a] + cn[b], max(se[a], se[b])

    def _apply_min(self, o, x):                      # only valid when x > se[o]
        if x < self.mx[o]:
            self.sum[o] -= (self.mx[o] - x) * self.cn[o]
            self.mx[o] = x

    def _push(self, o):
        self._apply_min(2 * o, self.mx[o]); self._apply_min(2 * o + 1, self.mx[o])

    def _chmin(self, o, lo, hi, l, r, x):
        if r <= lo or hi <= l or self.mx[o] <= x: return
        if l <= lo and hi <= r and self.se[o] < x:
            self._apply_min(o, x); return
        self._push(o)
        mid = (lo + hi) // 2
        self._chmin(2 * o, lo, mid, l, r, x); self._chmin(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, l, r, x)
        self._pull(o)

    def _range_sum(self, o, lo, hi, l, r):
        if r <= lo or hi <= l: return 0
        if l <= lo and hi <= r: return self.sum[o]
        self._push(o)
        mid = (lo + hi) // 2
        return self._range_sum(2 * o, lo, mid, l, r) + self._range_sum(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, l, r)

    def chmin(self, l, r, x):                        # on [l, r)
        self._chmin(1, 0, self.n, l, r, x)

    def range_sum(self, l, r):
        return self._range_sum(1, 0, self.n, l, r)
--cpp
struct Beats {
    static const int NEG = INT_MIN;                       // "minus infinity"
    int n; vector<long long> sum; vector<int> mx, se, cn;

    Beats(const vector<int>& a) : n(a.size()), sum(4 * a.size()), mx(4 * a.size()), se(4 * a.size()), cn(4 * a.size()) {
        build(1, 0, n, a);
    }
    void build(int o, int lo, int hi, const vector<int>& a) {
        if (hi - lo == 1) { sum[o] = mx[o] = a[lo]; se[o] = NEG; cn[o] = 1; return; }
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        build(2 * o, lo, mid, a); build(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, a);
        pull(o);
    }
    void pull(int o) {
        int a = 2 * o, b = 2 * o + 1;
        sum[o] = sum[a] + sum[b];
        if (mx[a] > mx[b]) { mx[o] = mx[a]; cn[o] = cn[a]; se[o] = max(se[a], mx[b]); }
        else if (mx[a] < mx[b]) { mx[o] = mx[b]; cn[o] = cn[b]; se[o] = max(mx[a], se[b]); }
        else { mx[o] = mx[a]; cn[o] = cn[a] + cn[b]; se[o] = max(se[a], se[b]); }
    }
    void applyMin(int o, int x) {                         // only valid when x > se[o]
        if (x < mx[o]) { sum[o] -= (long long)(mx[o] - x) * cn[o]; mx[o] = x; }
    }
    void push(int o) { applyMin(2 * o, mx[o]); applyMin(2 * o + 1, mx[o]); }

    void chmin(int o, int lo, int hi, int l, int r, int x) {
        if (r <= lo || hi <= l || mx[o] <= x) return;
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r && se[o] < x) { applyMin(o, x); return; }
        push(o);
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        chmin(2 * o, lo, mid, l, r, x); chmin(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, l, r, x);
        pull(o);
    }
    long long rangeSum(int o, int lo, int hi, int l, int r) {
        if (r <= lo || hi <= l) return 0;
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r) return sum[o];
        push(o);
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        return rangeSum(2 * o, lo, mid, l, r) + rangeSum(2 * o + 1, mid, hi, l, r);
    }
    void chmin(int l, int r, int x) { chmin(1, 0, n, l, r, x); }          // on [l, r)
    long long rangeSum(int l, int r) { return rangeSum(1, 0, n, l, r); }
};

### linkcut
--idea
A link-cut tree maintains a forest (a set of trees) while edges are added (link) and removed (cut), and answers questions about the path between two nodes, such as "are they connected?" or "what is the sum of the values on the path?". Each operation takes O(log n) amortised, even though the trees can be very deep. An ordinary tree stored with parent pointers cannot do this: a path query would walk up to n steps.
The idea is to cut every tree into long vertical paths ("preferred paths") and to keep each path in its own splay tree, ordered by depth. Asking about a path then means rearranging so the path you want is one single preferred path, which is called `access`. Re-rooting a tree is done by reversing a path, which a splay tree can do lazily with a flag.
--build
1. Number the nodes `1..n`. Index 0 means "none". For every node keep `ch0[x]`, `ch1[x]` (left and right child inside its splay tree), `par[x]` (parent, either the splay parent or, for the root of a splay tree, the path-parent in the real tree), `rev[x]` (a pending "reverse this subtree" flag), `val[x]` and `sum[x]` (the sum of `val` over the splay subtree).
2. `isRoot(x)`: `x` is the root of its splay tree if `par[x]` is 0 or `x` is neither child of `par[x]`. (A path-parent link is a `par` pointer that the parent does not point back to.)
3. `pull(x)`: `sum[x] = sum[ch0[x]] + sum[ch1[x]] + val[x]` (with `sum[0] = 0`). `push(x)`: if `rev[x]`, swap its two children, flip `rev` on each existing child, and clear `rev[x]`.
4. `rotate(x)`, as in any splay tree, moves `x` above its parent `y`, fixing the grandparent's child pointer only if `y` is not a splay root, then calls `pull(y)` and `pull(x)`.
5. `splay(x)`: first call `push` on every node from the splay root down to `x` (collect them walking up with `par`, then push from the top). Then rotate `x` up: if both `x` and its parent are same-side children, rotate the parent first (zig-zig), else rotate `x` (zig-zag), and finish with a single `rotate(x)`.
6. `access(x)`: make the path from the real root down to `x` one preferred path with `x` at the bottom. Set `last = 0`. For `y = x`, then `y = par[y]`, and so on while `y != 0`: `splay(y)`, set `ch1[y] = last` (its deeper part becomes the old path we came from), `pull(y)`, `last = y`. Finish with `splay(x)`.
7. `makeRoot(x)`: `access(x)` then `rev[x] ^= 1`. The path to the old root is reversed, so `x` becomes the top.
8. `findRoot(x)`: `access(x)`, then go left (pushing as you go) until there is no left child, and `splay` that node so the cost is paid for. Return it.
9. `link(x, y)` (they must be in different trees): `makeRoot(x)`, then `par[x] = y`. `cut(x, y)` (edge exists): `makeRoot(x)`, `access(y)`, then `x` is `y`'s left child with no right child, so set `ch0[y] = 0`, `par[x] = 0`, `pull(y)`.
10. `pathSum(x, y)`: `makeRoot(x)`, `access(y)`, return `sum[y]`. `connected(x, y)`: `findRoot(x) == findRoot(y)`.
--think
- Signal: a forest that changes online (edges added and deleted) with path queries. "Dynamic connectivity of a forest", "maximum edge on a path while edges change", "network with link and cut commands". If the tree never changes, use heavy-light decomposition or binary lifting instead, which are simpler.
- Decide first what each node stores and how it combines along a path. The sum above is one example, and any associative operation works (max, xor, gcd), as long as the reversal flag also reverses what the node stores if the operation is not commutative.
- Neighbouring techniques: union-find handles only edge additions. Euler tour trees also support link and cut and subtree queries, at a similar cost. Offline dynamic connectivity can use divide and conquer over time with a rollback union-find, with simpler code and `log^2 n` time.
- Edge weights are handled by making each edge a node of its own, placed between its endpoints.
- Pitfalls: forgetting to push tags from the splay root down before rotating (the most common bug), a stack-based push done from the node up instead of from the root down, not handling the `rev` flag in `findRoot`, and cutting an edge that does not exist (check `ch0[y] == x` and `ch1[x] == 0` after `access`).
--cost
Link, cut, access, path query O(log n) amortised. Space O(n).
--java
static class LinkCut {
    int[] ch0, ch1, par; boolean[] rev; long[] val, sum;

    LinkCut(int n) {                                  // nodes 1..n, index 0 means none
        ch0 = new int[n + 1]; ch1 = new int[n + 1]; par = new int[n + 1];
        rev = new boolean[n + 1]; val = new long[n + 1]; sum = new long[n + 1];
    }
    boolean isRoot(int x) { return par[x] == 0 || (ch0[par[x]] != x && ch1[par[x]] != x); }
    void pull(int x) { sum[x] = sum[ch0[x]] + sum[ch1[x]] + val[x]; }
    void push(int x) {
        if (!rev[x]) return;
        int t = ch0[x]; ch0[x] = ch1[x]; ch1[x] = t;
        if (ch0[x] != 0) rev[ch0[x]] ^= true;
        if (ch1[x] != 0) rev[ch1[x]] ^= true;
        rev[x] = false;
    }
    void rotate(int x) {
        int y = par[x], z = par[y]; boolean right = ch1[y] == x;
        if (!isRoot(y)) { if (ch0[z] == y) ch0[z] = x; else ch1[z] = x; }
        par[x] = z;
        if (right) { int b = ch0[x]; ch1[y] = b; if (b != 0) par[b] = y; ch0[x] = y; }
        else       { int b = ch1[x]; ch0[y] = b; if (b != 0) par[b] = y; ch1[x] = y; }
        par[y] = x; pull(y); pull(x);
    }
    void splay(int x) {
        int[] stack = new int[ch0.length]; int top = 0, y = x;
        stack[top++] = y;
        while (!isRoot(y)) { y = par[y]; stack[top++] = y; }
        while (top > 0) push(stack[--top]);               // push tags from the splay root down
        while (!isRoot(x)) {
            y = par[x]; int z = par[y];
            if (!isRoot(y)) rotate((ch0[y] == x) == (ch0[z] == y) ? y : x);
            rotate(x);
        }
    }
    void access(int x) {
        int last = 0;
        for (int y = x; y != 0; y = par[y]) { splay(y); ch1[y] = last; pull(y); last = y; }
        splay(x);
    }
    void makeRoot(int x) { access(x); rev[x] ^= true; }
    int findRoot(int x) {
        access(x);
        while (true) { push(x); if (ch0[x] == 0) break; x = ch0[x]; }
        splay(x);
        return x;
    }
    boolean connected(int x, int y) { return findRoot(x) == findRoot(y); }
    void link(int x, int y) { makeRoot(x); par[x] = y; }                // x and y in different trees
    void cut(int x, int y) {                                            // the edge x-y exists
        makeRoot(x); access(y); push(x);
        if (ch0[y] == x && ch1[x] == 0) { ch0[y] = 0; par[x] = 0; pull(y); }
    }
    void setVal(int x, long v) { access(x); val[x] = v; pull(x); }
    long pathSum(int x, int y) { makeRoot(x); access(y); return sum[y]; }
}
--py
class LinkCut:
    def __init__(self, n):                            # nodes 1..n, index 0 means none
        self.ch = [[0, 0] for _ in range(n + 1)]
        self.par = [0] * (n + 1)
        self.rev = [False] * (n + 1)
        self.val = [0] * (n + 1)
        self.sum = [0] * (n + 1)

    def is_root(self, x):
        p = self.par[x]
        return p == 0 or (self.ch[p][0] != x and self.ch[p][1] != x)

    def pull(self, x):
        self.sum[x] = self.sum[self.ch[x][0]] + self.sum[self.ch[x][1]] + self.val[x]

    def push(self, x):
        if not self.rev[x]: return
        c = self.ch[x]
        c[0], c[1] = c[1], c[0]
        for k in c:
            if k: self.rev[k] = not self.rev[k]
        self.rev[x] = False

    def rotate(self, x):
        y = self.par[x]; z = self.par[y]
        right = self.ch[y][1] == x
        if not self.is_root(y):
            self.ch[z][self.ch[z][1] == y] = x
        self.par[x] = z
        b = self.ch[x][0 if right else 1]
        self.ch[y][1 if right else 0] = b
        if b: self.par[b] = y
        self.ch[x][0 if right else 1] = y
        self.par[y] = x
        self.pull(y); self.pull(x)

    def splay(self, x):
        stack, y = [x], x
        while not self.is_root(y):
            y = self.par[y]; stack.append(y)
        for v in reversed(stack): self.push(v)          # push tags from the splay root down
        while not self.is_root(x):
            y = self.par[x]; z = self.par[y]
            if not self.is_root(y):
                self.rotate(y if (self.ch[y][0] == x) == (self.ch[z][0] == y) else x)
            self.rotate(x)

    def access(self, x):
        last, y = 0, x
        while y:
            self.splay(y); self.ch[y][1] = last; self.pull(y)
            last, y = y, self.par[y]
        self.splay(x)

    def make_root(self, x):
        self.access(x); self.rev[x] = not self.rev[x]

    def find_root(self, x):
        self.access(x)
        while True:
            self.push(x)
            if self.ch[x][0] == 0: break
            x = self.ch[x][0]
        self.splay(x)
        return x

    def connected(self, x, y): return self.find_root(x) == self.find_root(y)

    def link(self, x, y):                               # x and y in different trees
        self.make_root(x); self.par[x] = y

    def cut(self, x, y):                                # the edge x-y exists
        self.make_root(x); self.access(y); self.push(x)
        if self.ch[y][0] == x and self.ch[x][1] == 0:
            self.ch[y][0] = 0; self.par[x] = 0; self.pull(y)

    def set_val(self, x, v):
        self.access(x); self.val[x] = v; self.pull(x)

    def path_sum(self, x, y):
        self.make_root(x); self.access(y); return self.sum[y]
--cpp
struct LinkCut {
    vector<int> ch0, ch1, par; vector<char> rev; vector<long long> val, sum;

    LinkCut(int n) : ch0(n + 1), ch1(n + 1), par(n + 1), rev(n + 1), val(n + 1), sum(n + 1) {}   // nodes 1..n, 0 = none
    bool isRoot(int x) const { return par[x] == 0 || (ch0[par[x]] != x && ch1[par[x]] != x); }
    void pull(int x) { sum[x] = sum[ch0[x]] + sum[ch1[x]] + val[x]; }
    void push(int x) {
        if (!rev[x]) return;
        swap(ch0[x], ch1[x]);
        if (ch0[x]) rev[ch0[x]] ^= 1;
        if (ch1[x]) rev[ch1[x]] ^= 1;
        rev[x] = 0;
    }
    void rotate(int x) {
        int y = par[x], z = par[y]; bool right = ch1[y] == x;
        if (!isRoot(y)) { if (ch0[z] == y) ch0[z] = x; else ch1[z] = x; }
        par[x] = z;
        if (right) { int b = ch0[x]; ch1[y] = b; if (b) par[b] = y; ch0[x] = y; }
        else       { int b = ch1[x]; ch0[y] = b; if (b) par[b] = y; ch1[x] = y; }
        par[y] = x; pull(y); pull(x);
    }
    void splay(int x) {
        vector<int> stack; int y = x;
        stack.push_back(y);
        while (!isRoot(y)) { y = par[y]; stack.push_back(y); }
        for (int i = (int)stack.size() - 1; i >= 0; i--) push(stack[i]);   // push tags from the splay root down
        while (!isRoot(x)) {
            y = par[x]; int z = par[y];
            if (!isRoot(y)) rotate((ch0[y] == x) == (ch0[z] == y) ? y : x);
            rotate(x);
        }
    }
    void access(int x) {
        int last = 0;
        for (int y = x; y; y = par[y]) { splay(y); ch1[y] = last; pull(y); last = y; }
        splay(x);
    }
    void makeRoot(int x) { access(x); rev[x] ^= 1; }
    int findRoot(int x) {
        access(x);
        while (true) { push(x); if (!ch0[x]) break; x = ch0[x]; }
        splay(x);
        return x;
    }
    bool connected(int x, int y) { return findRoot(x) == findRoot(y); }
    void link(int x, int y) { makeRoot(x); par[x] = y; }                 // x and y in different trees
    void cut(int x, int y) {                                             // the edge x-y exists
        makeRoot(x); access(y); push(x);
        if (ch0[y] == x && !ch1[x]) { ch0[y] = 0; par[x] = 0; pull(y); }
    }
    void setVal(int x, long long v) { access(x); val[x] = v; pull(x); }
    long long pathSum(int x, int y) { makeRoot(x); access(y); return sum[y]; }
};

### lichao
--idea
A Li Chao tree maintains a set of lines `y = k x + b` and answers "what is the smallest value among all the lines at the point `x`?" (or the largest, by flipping signs). It lets you add lines in any order and query at any time, each in O(log n), where `n` is the number of possible `x` values. This is the core of the "convex hull trick" in dynamic programming, without needing the lines to arrive sorted.
It is a segment tree over the `x` values where every node stores just one line: the one that is best at the middle of the node's range. When a new line comes in, it is compared with the stored line at the middle point. The loser at the middle can still win on one side, but only one side, because two lines cross at most once. So the loser is pushed down into that one child, and the tree never needs more than one step per level.
--build
1. Choose the integer range `[0, n)` of `x` values you will ever query. Build arrays `K[o]` and `B[o]` for the line at each node `o` of a segment tree over this range (size about `4n`). Initially every node holds an "empty" line `K = 0, B = INF` with `INF` very large (like `4 x 10^18`).
2. `f(K, B, x) = K * x + B`.
3. `add(o, lo, hi, k, b)` with the node covering the inclusive range `[lo, hi]`: let `mid = (lo + hi) / 2`. If the new line is better than the stored one at `mid` (`f(k, b, mid) < f(K[o], B[o], mid)`), swap them. Now the stored line is the winner at `mid`, and `(k, b)` is the loser.
4. If `lo == hi` stop. Otherwise, if the loser beats the stored line at `lo`, it can only win on the left half: `add(2o, lo, mid, k, b)`. Else if the loser beats the stored line at `hi`, it can only win on the right half: `add(2o + 1, mid + 1, hi, k, b)`. Otherwise the loser is worse everywhere in this range and is dropped.
5. `query(x)`: start at the root and walk down to the leaf of `x`, taking the minimum of `f(K[o], B[o], x)` over every node on the path.
6. Invariant: for every `x`, the best line for `x` is stored somewhere on the root-to-leaf path of `x`. Step 4 maintains it because a line that loses at `mid` and at `lo` cannot win anywhere on the left either (lines cross at most once), and symmetric for the right.
7. For the maximum, store negated lines (`-k`, `-b`) and negate the answer, or flip the comparisons.
8. Segments (lines that only exist on an `x` range) are added by splitting the range over the nodes it covers (like a range update in a segment tree) and calling `add` on each, giving `O(log^2 n)`.
--think
- Signal: "minimum over a set of linear functions at a point", DP transitions of the form `dp[i] = min over j of (dp[j] + m_j * x_i + c_j)`, "cheapest option as `x` grows" where each option is a line. The convex hull trick requires slopes sorted; a Li Chao tree does not.
- Decide first the range and type of `x`. For huge or real-valued `x`, use a dynamic tree (create nodes lazily) or ternary search on the hull. Check overflow: `k x + b` must fit in a `long`, and `INF` must still be larger than every real value.
- Neighbouring techniques: the monotone convex hull trick with a deque is simpler and faster when slopes arrive sorted and queries also sorted. Divide and conquer optimisation works for other DP shapes. A kinetic segment tree handles lines that change. A plain segment tree with a lazy tag cannot do this.
- Why the one-crossing fact matters: two different lines intersect at most once, so comparing at `mid`, `lo` and `hi` decides which half (at most one) can still contain a winner. If it were a general function, both halves might have to be searched.
- Pitfalls: inclusive versus half-open ranges (the code here is inclusive, `mid + 1` for the right child), a too-small `INF`, forgetting the empty-line initialisation, and mixing up min with max.
--cost
Insert a line and query a point are O(log n). Space O(n).
--java
static class LiChao {
    static final long INF = 4_000_000_000_000_000_000L;
    int n; long[] K, B;

    LiChao(int n) {                                   // x values 0 .. n-1
        this.n = n; K = new long[4 * n]; B = new long[4 * n];
        java.util.Arrays.fill(B, INF);                // every node starts with the empty line
    }
    long f(long k, long b, long x) { return k * x + b; }

    void add(int o, int lo, int hi, long k, long b) {
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        if (f(k, b, mid) < f(K[o], B[o], mid)) {      // keep the winner at mid in the node
            long tk = K[o], tb = B[o]; K[o] = k; B[o] = b; k = tk; b = tb;
        }
        if (lo == hi) return;
        if (f(k, b, lo) < f(K[o], B[o], lo)) add(2 * o, lo, mid, k, b);          // loser can win on the left
        else if (f(k, b, hi) < f(K[o], B[o], hi)) add(2 * o + 1, mid + 1, hi, k, b);   // or on the right
    }
    void add(long k, long b) { add(1, 0, n - 1, k, b); }

    long query(int x) {                               // smallest value at x
        long best = INF; int o = 1, lo = 0, hi = n - 1;
        while (true) {
            best = Math.min(best, f(K[o], B[o], x));
            if (lo == hi) return best;
            int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
            if (x <= mid) { o = 2 * o; hi = mid; } else { o = 2 * o + 1; lo = mid + 1; }
        }
    }
}
--py
INF = 4 * 10**18

class LiChao:
    def __init__(self, n):                            # x values 0 .. n-1
        self.n = n
        self.K = [0] * (4 * n)
        self.B = [INF] * (4 * n)                      # every node starts with the empty line

    @staticmethod
    def _f(k, b, x):
        return k * x + b

    def _add(self, o, lo, hi, k, b):
        mid = (lo + hi) // 2
        f, K, B = self._f, self.K, self.B
        if f(k, b, mid) < f(K[o], B[o], mid):         # keep the winner at mid in the node
            K[o], k = k, K[o]
            B[o], b = b, B[o]
        if lo == hi: return
        if f(k, b, lo) < f(K[o], B[o], lo):
            self._add(2 * o, lo, mid, k, b)           # loser can win on the left
        elif f(k, b, hi) < f(K[o], B[o], hi):
            self._add(2 * o + 1, mid + 1, hi, k, b)   # or on the right

    def add(self, k, b):
        self._add(1, 0, self.n - 1, k, b)

    def query(self, x):                               # smallest value at x
        best, o, lo, hi = INF, 1, 0, self.n - 1
        while True:
            best = min(best, self._f(self.K[o], self.B[o], x))
            if lo == hi: return best
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            if x <= mid: o, hi = 2 * o, mid
            else: o, lo = 2 * o + 1, mid + 1
--cpp
struct LiChao {
    static constexpr long long INF = 4000000000000000000LL;
    int n; vector<long long> K, B;

    LiChao(int n) : n(n), K(4 * n, 0), B(4 * n, INF) {}      // x values 0 .. n-1, every node starts empty
    static long long f(long long k, long long b, long long x) { return k * x + b; }

    void add(int o, int lo, int hi, long long k, long long b) {
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        if (f(k, b, mid) < f(K[o], B[o], mid)) { swap(K[o], k); swap(B[o], b); }   // keep the winner at mid
        if (lo == hi) return;
        if (f(k, b, lo) < f(K[o], B[o], lo)) add(2 * o, lo, mid, k, b);            // loser can win on the left
        else if (f(k, b, hi) < f(K[o], B[o], hi)) add(2 * o + 1, mid + 1, hi, k, b);   // or on the right
    }
    void add(long long k, long long b) { add(1, 0, n - 1, k, b); }

    long long query(int x) const {                           // smallest value at x
        long long best = INF; int o = 1, lo = 0, hi = n - 1;
        while (true) {
            best = min(best, f(K[o], B[o], x));
            if (lo == hi) return best;
            int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
            if (x <= mid) { o = 2 * o; hi = mid; } else { o = 2 * o + 1; lo = mid + 1; }
        }
    }
};

### wavelet
--idea
A wavelet tree answers questions about a range of an array by value: "what is the k-th smallest in positions `l..r`?", "how many values in `l..r` are below `x`?". It does it by splitting the values, not the positions. The top level splits all values into a small half and a large half. Each position remembers just one bit: which half its value went to. Then each half is split again, and so on, down to single values.
To follow a range down, you only need to know how many items of the range went to the small half (count the zero bits in the range), which a prefix count of the bits gives in O(1). The range then maps to a range in the small half or in the large half. This lesson builds the wavelet matrix, a flat version of the wavelet tree: one array of bits and one prefix count per level, no pointers, same queries and same cost.
--build
1. Values are integers in `[0, 2^B)` (compress values first if they are larger). Process the bits from the top bit `B - 1` down to bit 0. Each bit is one level.
2. At each level, take the current order of the items `cur` (initially the array). For each position `i` in this order, bit `i` is the current bit of `cur[i]`. Store `zc[level][i]`, the number of zero bits among the first `i` positions (`zc[level][0] = 0`), and `nz[level] = zc[level][n]`, the total zeros.
3. Make the order for the next level by a stable split: all items with bit 0 first (keeping their order), then all items with bit 1 (keeping their order).
4. `kth(l, r, k)` (k-th smallest in `a[l, r)`, `k` counts from 0): start with the answer 0. At each level, `zl = zc[level][l]`, `zr = zc[level][r]`, `zeros = zr - zl` (items in the range with bit 0). If `k < zeros`, the answer has bit 0 here: set `l = zl`, `r = zr`. Otherwise it has bit 1: set `k -= zeros`, `l = nz[level] + (l - zl)`, `r = nz[level] + (r - zr)`, and add `2^bit` to the answer.
5. `countLess(l, r, x)` (how many values in `a[l, r)` are below `x`): at each level look at the bit of `x`. If it is 1, all the zero-bit items in the range are smaller, so add `zeros` and go to the ones side (`l = nz + (l - zl)`, `r = nz + (r - zr)`). If it is 0, go to the zeros side (`l = zl`, `r = zr`). At the end the remaining range holds items equal to `x`, which are not counted.
6. Why the mapping of positions works: the stable split keeps items in order, so the items of the range that have bit 0 are consecutive in the next level, starting right after the zeros before `l`. The ones are consecutive after all the `nz[level]` zeros.
7. Memory is `n x B` bits plus the prefix counts (stored as integers here for clarity, so `n x B` integers).
--think
- Signal: static array, many queries of "k-th smallest in a range", "count of values below `x` in a range", "range frequency of a value", "range mode" (harder). Online, with no updates.
- Decide first how to get values into `[0, 2^B)`. Sort and replace each value by its rank if they are large or negative. Then `B = ceil(log2(number of distinct values))`.
- Neighbouring techniques: a persistent segment tree gives the same answers with `O(n log n)` nodes of integers. A merge sort tree is simpler but `log^2` per query. Offline, sort the queries and use a Fenwick tree. A wavelet tree is the leanest in memory with bit-packed arrays.
- The tree form (with explicit nodes and a bitmap per node) additionally allows range-restricted operations like "range reverse" with extra work. For plain counting, the matrix is easier to code.
- Pitfalls: forgetting the stable split (the order must be preserved), mixing up `[l, r)` and `[l, r]`, `k` counting from 0 versus 1, and values outside `[0, 2^B)`.
--cost
Build O(n B). Each query O(B), that is O(log of the value range). Space O(n B) integers, or n B bits when packed.
--java
static class Wavelet {
    int B, n; int[][] zc; int[] nz;

    Wavelet(int[] a, int B) {                           // values in [0, 2^B)
        this.B = B; n = a.length;
        zc = new int[B][n + 1]; nz = new int[B];
        int[] cur = a.clone();
        for (int lv = 0, bit = B - 1; lv < B; lv++, bit--) {
            int[] zeros = new int[n], ones = new int[n]; int z = 0, o = 0;
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                boolean one = (cur[i] >> bit & 1) == 1;
                zc[lv][i + 1] = zc[lv][i] + (one ? 0 : 1);    // zero bits so far
                if (one) ones[o++] = cur[i]; else zeros[z++] = cur[i];
            }
            nz[lv] = z;
            for (int i = 0; i < z; i++) cur[i] = zeros[i];     // stable split: zeros first
            for (int i = 0; i < o; i++) cur[z + i] = ones[i];
        }
    }

    int kth(int l, int r, int k) {                      // k-th smallest in a[l, r), k from 0
        int res = 0;
        for (int lv = 0, bit = B - 1; lv < B; lv++, bit--) {
            int zl = zc[lv][l], zr = zc[lv][r], zeros = zr - zl;
            if (k < zeros) { l = zl; r = zr; }
            else { k -= zeros; l = nz[lv] + (l - zl); r = nz[lv] + (r - zr); res |= 1 << bit; }
        }
        return res;
    }

    int countLess(int l, int r, int x) {                // values < x in a[l, r)
        int cnt = 0;
        for (int lv = 0, bit = B - 1; lv < B; lv++, bit--) {
            int zl = zc[lv][l], zr = zc[lv][r];
            if ((x >> bit & 1) == 1) { cnt += zr - zl; l = nz[lv] + (l - zl); r = nz[lv] + (r - zr); }
            else { l = zl; r = zr; }
        }
        return cnt;
    }
}
--py
class Wavelet:
    def __init__(self, a, B):                           # values in [0, 2^B)
        self.B, self.n = B, len(a)
        self.zc, self.nz = [], []
        cur = list(a)
        for bit in range(B - 1, -1, -1):
            zc, zeros, ones = [0], [], []
            for v in cur:
                one = (v >> bit) & 1
                zc.append(zc[-1] + (0 if one else 1))      # zero bits so far
                (ones if one else zeros).append(v)
            self.zc.append(zc); self.nz.append(len(zeros))
            cur = zeros + ones                              # stable split: zeros first

    def kth(self, l, r, k):                             # k-th smallest in a[l, r), k from 0
        res = 0
        for lv, bit in enumerate(range(self.B - 1, -1, -1)):
            zl, zr = self.zc[lv][l], self.zc[lv][r]
            zeros = zr - zl
            if k < zeros:
                l, r = zl, zr
            else:
                k -= zeros
                l, r = self.nz[lv] + (l - zl), self.nz[lv] + (r - zr)
                res |= 1 << bit
        return res

    def count_less(self, l, r, x):                      # values < x in a[l, r)
        cnt = 0
        for lv, bit in enumerate(range(self.B - 1, -1, -1)):
            zl, zr = self.zc[lv][l], self.zc[lv][r]
            if (x >> bit) & 1:
                cnt += zr - zl
                l, r = self.nz[lv] + (l - zl), self.nz[lv] + (r - zr)
            else:
                l, r = zl, zr
        return cnt
--cpp
struct Wavelet {
    int B, n; vector<vector<int>> zc; vector<int> nz;

    Wavelet(const vector<int>& a, int B) : B(B), n(a.size()), zc(B, vector<int>(a.size() + 1, 0)), nz(B) {   // values in [0, 2^B)
        vector<int> cur = a;
        for (int lv = 0, bit = B - 1; lv < B; lv++, bit--) {
            vector<int> zeros, ones;
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                bool one = cur[i] >> bit & 1;
                zc[lv][i + 1] = zc[lv][i] + (one ? 0 : 1);        // zero bits so far
                (one ? ones : zeros).push_back(cur[i]);
            }
            nz[lv] = zeros.size();
            cur = zeros;                                          // stable split: zeros first
            cur.insert(cur.end(), ones.begin(), ones.end());
        }
    }

    int kth(int l, int r, int k) const {                // k-th smallest in a[l, r), k from 0
        int res = 0;
        for (int lv = 0, bit = B - 1; lv < B; lv++, bit--) {
            int zl = zc[lv][l], zr = zc[lv][r], zeros = zr - zl;
            if (k < zeros) { l = zl; r = zr; }
            else { k -= zeros; l = nz[lv] + (l - zl); r = nz[lv] + (r - zr); res |= 1 << bit; }
        }
        return res;
    }

    int countLess(int l, int r, int x) const {          // values < x in a[l, r)
        int cnt = 0;
        for (int lv = 0, bit = B - 1; lv < B; lv++, bit--) {
            int zl = zc[lv][l], zr = zc[lv][r];
            if (x >> bit & 1) { cnt += zr - zl; l = nz[lv] + (l - zl); r = nz[lv] + (r - zr); }
            else { l = zl; r = zr; }
        }
        return cnt;
    }
};

### kdtree
--idea
A k-d tree (k-dimensional tree) stores points in space so that you can quickly find the point nearest to a query point, or all points inside a rectangle. It is a binary search tree for points: at each level it splits the points by one coordinate, alternating the coordinate level by level (x, then y, then x, and so on). The point at the middle of the split becomes the node, points with a smaller coordinate go left, larger go right.
To find the nearest neighbour, walk down the side of the split the query falls on, since that is probably where the nearest point is. On the way back up, ask one question: could the other side contain something closer? It can only do so if the splitting line is closer to the query than the best distance found so far. If not, the whole other side is skipped. That pruning is what makes it fast.
--build
1. Points are pairs `(x, y)` (the same works for any number of coordinates, cycling through them). A node holds a point `p`, and `left`, `right` children.
2. `build(points, depth)`: if no points, return nothing. Pick the axis `depth % 2`. Sort the points by that coordinate and take the median as the node's point. Build the left child from the points before the median (`depth + 1`) and the right child from the points after it.
3. The tree is balanced because every split is at the median. Sorting at each level gives `O(n log^2 n)` build time. For `O(n log n)` use a linear-time median selection (quickselect).
4. `nearest(node, q, depth)`, with `best` and `bestD` as the best point and its squared distance so far (start with none and infinity): if `node` is empty return. Compute `d = dist2(node.p, q)` (code piece `u-dist2`, the squared distance, which avoids square roots). If `d < bestD`, update `best` and `bestD`.
5. Let `axis = depth % 2` and `diff = q[axis] - node.p[axis]`. The near side is left if `diff < 0`, else right. Search the near side first.
6. Then search the far side only if `diff * diff < bestD`: the splitting line is closer to `q` than the best point found, so a closer point might lie across it.
7. Invariant: when `nearest` returns, `best` is the closest point among all points in this subtree and what was already known. Skipped subtrees cannot beat it because every point there is at least `|diff|` away along the split axis, and `diff * diff >= bestD`.
8. Range search (all points in a rectangle) walks both children but skips a child when the rectangle lies entirely on the other side of the split.
--think
- Signal: nearest neighbour, "k closest points", "points inside a box" among many static points in 2D to about 10D. Many queries against one fixed point set.
- Decide first the dimension. For few dimensions (up to about 10) a k-d tree is good. In high dimensions (50 and up) pruning stops working and you need approximate methods.
- Neighbouring techniques: a grid with buckets is simpler for uniformly spread points. A quadtree splits by space instead of by median. For a one-off closest pair, use divide and conquer. For a few queries a plain scan of all points is `O(n)` per query and may be enough.
- Use squared distances throughout and compare squares: `diff * diff < bestD` avoids any square root and any floating point.
- Pitfalls: pruning with `<=` or `<` is fine but with a wrong sign (comparing `diff` to `bestD` without squaring), searching the far side without checking, duplicate points (fine for nearest, careful for "all points in range"), and deep recursion on large inputs (the tree is balanced, so depth is only `log n`).
--cost
Build O(n log^2 n) with sorting at each level. Nearest neighbour O(log n) on average for well-spread points, O(n) worst case. Space O(n).
--java
static class KdTree {
    static class Node { int[] p; Node left, right; Node(int[] p) { this.p = p; } }
    Node root; int[] best; long bestD;

    KdTree(int[][] pts) { root = build(pts, 0, pts.length, 0); }

    Node build(int[][] pts, int lo, int hi, int depth) {         // builds from pts[lo, hi)
        if (lo >= hi) return null;
        int axis = depth % 2;
        java.util.Arrays.sort(pts, lo, hi, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[axis], b[axis]));
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        Node node = new Node(pts[mid]);
        node.left = build(pts, lo, mid, depth + 1);
        node.right = build(pts, mid + 1, hi, depth + 1);
        return node;
    }

    int[] nearest(int qx, int qy) {
        best = null; bestD = Long.MAX_VALUE;
        search(root, qx, qy, 0);
        return best;
    }

    void search(Node node, int qx, int qy, int depth) {
        if (node == null) return;
        long d = dist2(node.p[0], node.p[1], qx, qy);              // piece u-dist2: squared distance
        if (d < bestD) { bestD = d; best = node.p; }
        int axis = depth % 2;
        long diff = (axis == 0 ? qx : qy) - node.p[axis];
        Node near = diff < 0 ? node.left : node.right, far = diff < 0 ? node.right : node.left;
        search(near, qx, qy, depth + 1);                           // the side the query is on
        if (diff * diff < bestD) search(far, qx, qy, depth + 1);   // the other side only if it can be closer
    }
}
--py
class KdTree:
    class Node:
        def __init__(self, p):
            self.p, self.left, self.right = p, None, None

    def __init__(self, pts):
        self.root = self._build(list(pts), 0)
        self.best, self.best_d = None, float('inf')

    def _build(self, pts, depth):
        if not pts: return None
        axis = depth % 2
        pts.sort(key=lambda p: p[axis])
        mid = len(pts) // 2
        node = KdTree.Node(pts[mid])
        node.left = self._build(pts[:mid], depth + 1)
        node.right = self._build(pts[mid + 1:], depth + 1)
        return node

    def nearest(self, qx, qy):
        self.best, self.best_d = None, float('inf')
        self._search(self.root, qx, qy, 0)
        return self.best

    def _search(self, node, qx, qy, depth):
        if node is None: return
        d = dist2(node.p[0], node.p[1], qx, qy)              # piece u-dist2: squared distance
        if d < self.best_d:
            self.best_d, self.best = d, node.p
        axis = depth % 2
        diff = (qx, qy)[axis] - node.p[axis]
        near, far = (node.left, node.right) if diff < 0 else (node.right, node.left)
        self._search(near, qx, qy, depth + 1)                # the side the query is on
        if diff * diff < self.best_d:                        # the other side only if it can be closer
            self._search(far, qx, qy, depth + 1)
--cpp
struct KdTree {
    struct Node { array<int, 2> p; Node *left = nullptr, *right = nullptr; Node(array<int, 2> p) : p(p) {} };
    Node* root; array<int, 2> best{}; long long bestD = LLONG_MAX;

    KdTree(vector<array<int, 2>> pts) { root = build(pts, 0, pts.size(), 0); }

    Node* build(vector<array<int, 2>>& pts, int lo, int hi, int depth) {     // builds from pts[lo, hi)
        if (lo >= hi) return nullptr;
        int axis = depth % 2;
        sort(pts.begin() + lo, pts.begin() + hi, [axis](const array<int, 2>& a, const array<int, 2>& b) { return a[axis] < b[axis]; });
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        Node* node = new Node(pts[mid]);
        node->left = build(pts, lo, mid, depth + 1);
        node->right = build(pts, mid + 1, hi, depth + 1);
        return node;
    }

    array<int, 2> nearest(int qx, int qy) {
        bestD = LLONG_MAX;
        search(root, qx, qy, 0);
        return best;
    }

    void search(Node* node, int qx, int qy, int depth) {
        if (!node) return;
        long long d = dist2(node->p[0], node->p[1], qx, qy);       // piece u-dist2: squared distance
        if (d < bestD) { bestD = d; best = node->p; }
        int axis = depth % 2;
        long long diff = (long long)(axis == 0 ? qx : qy) - node->p[axis];
        Node *nearSide = diff < 0 ? node->left : node->right, *farSide = diff < 0 ? node->right : node->left;
        search(nearSide, qx, qy, depth + 1);                       // the side the query is on
        if (diff * diff < bestD) search(farSide, qx, qy, depth + 1);   // the other side only if it can be closer
    }
};
--uses
u-dist2

### rope
--idea
A rope is a way to store a very long text (or any sequence) so that you can insert in the middle, delete a stretch, or move a block quickly. In an ordinary array or string, inserting in the middle moves everything after it, which costs O(n). A rope keeps the characters in a balanced tree and glues pieces together by changing a few pointers.
The tree used here is an implicit treap: a treap whose order is the position in the sequence, not a stored key. Each node holds one character and the size of its subtree, so the node's position is found by counting the sizes on the left. Two operations do all the work: `split` cuts the sequence at a position into a left piece and a right piece, and `merge` glues two pieces end to end. Everything else is a few splits and merges.
--build
1. A node holds `ch` (the character), `pri` (a random priority, code piece `u-rand`), `size` (nodes in its subtree), `left`, `right`. The sequence is read left to right by an in-order walk. An empty rope is null.
2. `size(t)` is 0 for null. `upd(t)` sets `t.size = 1 + size(t.left) + size(t.right)`. Call `upd` on a node whenever its children change.
3. `split(t, k)` returns two ropes: the first `k` characters and the rest. If `t` is null, both are null. Let `s = size(t.left)`. If `k <= s`, the cut is inside the left subtree: split `t.left` at `k` into `(a, b)`, set `t.left = b`, `upd(t)`, return `(a, t)`. Otherwise split `t.right` at `k - s - 1` into `(a, b)`, set `t.right = a`, `upd(t)`, return `(t, b)`.
4. `merge(a, b)` joins `a` then `b`. If one is null return the other. If `a.pri > b.pri`: `a.right = merge(a.right, b)`, `upd(a)`, return `a`. Else `b.left = merge(a, b.left)`, `upd(b)`, return `b`.
5. `insert(t, pos, c)`: split at `pos` into `(a, b)`, return `merge(merge(a, newNode(c)), b)`.
6. `erase(t, l, r)` (remove positions `[l, r)`): split at `l` into `(a, rest)`, split `rest` at `r - l` into `(mid, b)`, drop `mid`, return `merge(a, b)`.
7. `charAt(t, i)`: walk down comparing `i` with `size(t.left)`: go left if smaller, return this node's character if equal, else subtract and go right.
8. `moveToFront(t, l, r)` (cut a stretch and put it at the start): split into `a = [0, l)`, `b = [l, r)`, `c = rest`, return `merge(b, merge(a, c))`. Moving a block is just a reordering of three pieces.
9. `toString(t)`: in-order walk, appending each character.
--think
- Signal: a long sequence with many edits in the middle: text editors, "cut and paste a range", "reverse a range" (add a lazy `rev` flag, as in the link-cut tree), "insert at position". Also any problem on a list where positions shift all the time.
- Decide first that the key is the position (implicit), not a value. Then nothing is sorted, and the only rule is that the in-order walk gives the sequence. Splitting by position is `split(t, k)`.
- Neighbouring techniques: a plain array or linked list is better when edits are few or at the ends. A gap buffer (an array with a hole at the cursor) is simpler and good for a text editor with one cursor. A balanced tree such as AVL works the same way but needs rotation code, where the treap needs only split and merge. A skip list can also index by position.
- Cost to remember: `charAt` is O(log n), unlike a linked list, because sizes let you jump. Concatenating two huge ropes is a single `merge`.
- Pitfalls: forgetting `upd` after changing children (sizes go wrong silently), off-by-one in the position inside `split` (the code uses "first `k` characters"), recursion depth (expected logarithmic), and a weak random source.
--cost
Split, merge, insert, erase and `charAt` are O(log n) expected. Building by repeated insertion is O(n log n). Space O(n).
--java
static class Node {
    char ch; int pri, size = 1; Node left, right;
    Node(char c) { ch = c; pri = randInt(0, 1_000_000_000); }      // piece u-rand
}
static int size(Node t) { return t == null ? 0 : t.size; }
static void upd(Node t) { t.size = 1 + size(t.left) + size(t.right); }

static Node[] split(Node t, int k) {                // {first k characters, the rest}
    if (t == null) return new Node[]{null, null};
    if (k <= size(t.left)) {
        Node[] p = split(t.left, k);
        t.left = p[1]; upd(t);
        return new Node[]{p[0], t};
    }
    Node[] p = split(t.right, k - size(t.left) - 1);
    t.right = p[0]; upd(t);
    return new Node[]{t, p[1]};
}

static Node merge(Node a, Node b) {
    if (a == null) return b;
    if (b == null) return a;
    if (a.pri > b.pri) { a.right = merge(a.right, b); upd(a); return a; }
    b.left = merge(a, b.left); upd(b);
    return b;
}

static Node insert(Node t, int pos, char c) {
    Node[] p = split(t, pos);
    return merge(merge(p[0], new Node(c)), p[1]);
}

static Node erase(Node t, int l, int r) {          // remove positions [l, r)
    Node[] p = split(t, l), q = split(p[1], r - l);
    return merge(p[0], q[1]);                      // q[0] is dropped
}

static Node moveToFront(Node t, int l, int r) {    // cut [l, r) and put it first
    Node[] p = split(t, l), q = split(p[1], r - l);
    return merge(q[0], merge(p[0], q[1]));
}

static char charAt(Node t, int i) {
    while (true) {
        int s = size(t.left);
        if (i < s) t = t.left;
        else if (i == s) return t.ch;
        else { i -= s + 1; t = t.right; }
    }
}

static void toText(Node t, StringBuilder sb) {
    if (t == null) return;
    toText(t.left, sb); sb.append(t.ch); toText(t.right, sb);
}
--py
class Node:
    def __init__(self, ch):
        self.ch, self.pri, self.size = ch, rand_int(0, 10**9), 1      # piece u-rand
        self.left = self.right = None

def size(t): return t.size if t else 0
def upd(t): t.size = 1 + size(t.left) + size(t.right)

def split(t, k):
    """Return (first k characters, the rest)."""
    if t is None: return None, None
    if k <= size(t.left):
        a, b = split(t.left, k)
        t.left = b; upd(t)
        return a, t
    a, b = split(t.right, k - size(t.left) - 1)
    t.right = a; upd(t)
    return t, b

def merge(a, b):
    if a is None: return b
    if b is None: return a
    if a.pri > b.pri:
        a.right = merge(a.right, b); upd(a)
        return a
    b.left = merge(a, b.left); upd(b)
    return b

def insert(t, pos, c):
    a, b = split(t, pos)
    return merge(merge(a, Node(c)), b)

def erase(t, l, r):
    """Remove positions [l, r)."""
    a, rest = split(t, l)
    _, b = split(rest, r - l)                      # the middle piece is dropped
    return merge(a, b)

def move_to_front(t, l, r):
    """Cut [l, r) and put it first."""
    a, rest = split(t, l)
    mid, b = split(rest, r - l)
    return merge(mid, merge(a, b))

def char_at(t, i):
    while True:
        s = size(t.left)
        if i < s: t = t.left
        elif i == s: return t.ch
        else: i -= s + 1; t = t.right

def to_text(t, out):
    if t is None: return
    to_text(t.left, out); out.append(t.ch); to_text(t.right, out)
--cpp
struct Node {
    char ch; int pri, size = 1; Node *left = nullptr, *right = nullptr;
    Node(char c) : ch(c), pri(randInt(0, 1000000000)) {}           // piece u-rand
};
int sz(Node* t) { return t ? t->size : 0; }
void upd(Node* t) { t->size = 1 + sz(t->left) + sz(t->right); }

pair<Node*, Node*> split(Node* t, int k) {          // {first k characters, the rest}
    if (!t) return {nullptr, nullptr};
    if (k <= sz(t->left)) {
        auto [a, b] = split(t->left, k);
        t->left = b; upd(t);
        return {a, t};
    }
    auto [a, b] = split(t->right, k - sz(t->left) - 1);
    t->right = a; upd(t);
    return {t, b};
}

Node* merge(Node* a, Node* b) {
    if (!a) return b;
    if (!b) return a;
    if (a->pri > b->pri) { a->right = merge(a->right, b); upd(a); return a; }
    b->left = merge(a, b->left); upd(b);
    return b;
}

Node* insert(Node* t, int pos, char c) {
    auto [a, b] = split(t, pos);
    return merge(merge(a, new Node(c)), b);
}

Node* erase(Node* t, int l, int r) {                // remove positions [l, r)
    auto [a, rest] = split(t, l);
    auto [mid, b] = split(rest, r - l);             // mid is dropped
    return merge(a, b);
}

Node* moveToFront(Node* t, int l, int r) {          // cut [l, r) and put it first
    auto [a, rest] = split(t, l);
    auto [mid, b] = split(rest, r - l);
    return merge(mid, merge(a, b));
}

char charAt(Node* t, int i) {
    while (true) {
        int s = sz(t->left);
        if (i < s) t = t->left;
        else if (i == s) return t->ch;
        else { i -= s + 1; t = t->right; }
    }
}

void toText(Node* t, string& out) {
    if (!t) return;
    toText(t->left, out); out += t->ch; toText(t->right, out);
}
--uses
u-rand

### veb
--idea
A van Emde Boas (vEB) tree stores a set of integers from `0` to `u - 1` and answers "what is the next element after `x`?" (successor), insert, delete and membership in O(log log u) time. That is far faster than the O(log n) of a balanced tree when the universe is not too large: for `u = 2^32`, `log log u` is only 5.
The trick is recursion on the size of the universe. Split the bits of a number into a high half and a low half. The high half says which cluster the number lives in, and the low half says where inside that cluster. Keep a smaller vEB tree for every cluster, plus one more small tree (the summary) that remembers which clusters are not empty. A search then asks the summary one question and one cluster one question, each on a universe whose bit length is half as big. Halving the bits `log log u` times reaches a universe of size 2.
--build
1. A structure for a universe of `bits` bits (`u = 2^bits` values) holds `mn` and `mx` (the smallest and largest element, or -1 if empty). The minimum is stored here and not inside any cluster. If `bits > 1` it also holds `low = bits / 2` (bits for the position inside a cluster), `summary` (a structure over the `bits - low` bits of the cluster number), and an array `cluster` of `2^(bits - low)` structures over `low` bits each. Create sub-structures only when first needed, to save memory.
2. Split a number: `hi(x) = x >> low` (cluster number) and `lo(x) = x & (2^low - 1)` (position in the cluster).
3. `create(universe)`: compute `bits = floorLog2(universe - 1) + 1` (code piece `u-log2`), the number of bits needed to write every value below `universe`.
4. `contains(x)`: true if `x == mn` or `x == mx`. If `bits == 1` false. Otherwise ask `cluster[hi(x)].contains(lo(x))` (false if that cluster does not exist or is empty).
5. `insert(x)`: if empty, set `mn = mx = x` and stop. If `x == mn` or `x == mx` stop (it is already there). If `x < mn`, swap `x` and `mn` (the new minimum is stored here, and the old minimum is the one inserted below). If `bits > 1`: take `h = hi(x)`, `l = lo(x)`. If cluster `h` is empty, insert `h` into the summary and set that cluster's `mn = mx = l` directly, which is O(1), so only one recursive call is made. Otherwise insert `l` into cluster `h`. Finally if `x > mx`, set `mx = x`.
6. `successor(x)` (smallest element greater than `x`, or -1): if `bits == 1`, the answer is 1 when `x == 0` and `mx == 1`, else -1. If `mn != -1` and `x < mn`, answer `mn`. Otherwise let `h = hi(x)`, `l = lo(x)`. If cluster `h` exists and `l < cluster[h].mx`, the answer is in the same cluster: `(h << low) | cluster[h].successor(l)`. Else ask the summary for the next non-empty cluster `sh = summary.successor(h)`. If none, answer -1. Otherwise answer `(sh << low) | cluster[sh].mn`.
7. `erase(x)` (only if present): if `mn == mx`, the set becomes empty. If `bits == 1`, the other of 0 and 1 stays. Otherwise if `x == mn`, find the next smallest element in the clusters (`first = summary.mn`, value `(first << low) | cluster[first].mn`), make it the new `mn`, and continue by erasing that element from its cluster. Erase `lo(x)` from cluster `hi(x)`. If that cluster became empty, erase its number from the summary, and if `x == mx` set the new `mx` from the summary's largest cluster (or to `mn` if none). Otherwise if `x == mx`, set `mx = (h << low) | cluster[h].mx`.
8. Why only one recursive call: inserting into an empty cluster is O(1) because of the stored minimum, and when it is not empty the summary needs no update. So each level makes one real recursive call, and the key length halves every level.
--think
- Signal: predecessor and successor queries over a set of small integers (up to about `2^20` to `2^26` in memory) with many inserts and deletes, where O(log n) is too slow or you want to show the log log bound. Textbook structure rather than a contest staple.
- Decide first whether the universe is small enough. A vEB tree uses memory proportional to `u` in the worst case (clusters of clusters), unless created lazily as here. For `u` up to `2^26` a simple 64-ary bitset tree (levels of 64-bit words with trailing-zero counts) is much easier and just as fast in practice.
- Neighbouring techniques: a balanced tree or sorted set (any universe, O(log n)). A y-fast trie (O(log log u) with O(n) space). A bitset with `nextSetBit` for small `u`. A Fenwick tree with descent if you only need the k-th element.
- The memory-saving trick, storing the minimum outside the clusters, is what makes the recurrence `T(u) = T(sqrt u) + O(1)` and not two recursive calls.
- Pitfalls: forgetting that `mn` is not in any cluster (every cluster query must also check `mn`), not handling an empty cluster separately, off-by-one in the bit split for odd `bits`, and deleting an absent element.
--cost
Insert, delete, membership and successor are O(log log u) each. Space O(n log log u) with lazy creation, O(u) for a full eager tree.
--java
static class VEB {
    int bits, low, mn = -1, mx = -1;
    VEB summary; VEB[] cluster;

    VEB(int bits) {
        this.bits = bits;
        if (bits > 1) { low = bits / 2; cluster = new VEB[1 << (bits - low)]; }
    }
    static VEB create(int universe) { return new VEB(floorLog2(universe - 1) + 1); }   // piece u-log2

    int hi(int x) { return x >> low; }
    int lo(int x) { return x & ((1 << low) - 1); }
    VEB cl(int h) { if (cluster[h] == null) cluster[h] = new VEB(low); return cluster[h]; }
    VEB sum() { if (summary == null) summary = new VEB(bits - low); return summary; }

    boolean contains(int x) {
        if (x == mn || x == mx) return true;
        if (bits == 1 || cluster[hi(x)] == null) return false;
        return cluster[hi(x)].contains(lo(x));
    }

    void insert(int x) {
        if (mn == -1) { mn = mx = x; return; }
