        if (x == mn || x == mx) return;
        if (x < mn) { int t = x; x = mn; mn = t; }            // new minimum stays here
        if (bits > 1) {
            int h = hi(x), l = lo(x);
            VEB c = cl(h);
            if (c.mn == -1) { sum().insert(h); c.mn = c.mx = l; }   // empty cluster: one O(1) step
            else c.insert(l);
        }
        if (x > mx) mx = x;
    }

    int successor(int x) {                                     // smallest element > x, or -1
        if (bits == 1) return (x == 0 && mx == 1) ? 1 : -1;
        if (mn != -1 && x < mn) return mn;
        int h = hi(x), l = lo(x);
        VEB c = cluster[h];
        if (c != null && c.mx != -1 && l < c.mx) return (h << low) | c.successor(l);
        int sh = summary == null ? -1 : summary.successor(h);
        return sh == -1 ? -1 : (sh << low) | cluster[sh].mn;
    }

    void erase(int x) {
        if (!contains(x)) return;
        if (mn == mx) { mn = mx = -1; return; }
        if (bits == 1) { mn = mx = 1 - x; return; }
        if (x == mn) {                                         // pull the next smallest up to be the minimum
            int first = summary.mn;
            x = (first << low) | cluster[first].mn;
            mn = x;
        }
        int h = hi(x);
        cluster[h].erase(lo(x));
        if (cluster[h].mn == -1) {
            summary.erase(h);
            if (x == mx) { int s = summary.mx; mx = (s == -1) ? mn : (s << low) | cluster[s].mx; }
        } else if (x == mx) mx = (h << low) | cluster[h].mx;
    }
}
--py
class VEB:
    def __init__(self, bits):
        self.bits, self.mn, self.mx = bits, -1, -1
        self.summary = None
        if bits > 1:
            self.low = bits // 2
            self.cluster = [None] * (1 << (bits - self.low))

    @staticmethod
    def create(universe):
        return VEB(floor_log2(universe - 1) + 1)             # piece u-log2

    def _hi(self, x): return x >> self.low
    def _lo(self, x): return x & ((1 << self.low) - 1)

    def _cl(self, h):
        if self.cluster[h] is None: self.cluster[h] = VEB(self.low)
        return self.cluster[h]

    def _sum(self):
        if self.summary is None: self.summary = VEB(self.bits - self.low)
        return self.summary

    def contains(self, x):
        if x == self.mn or x == self.mx: return True
        if self.bits == 1 or self.cluster[self._hi(x)] is None: return False
        return self.cluster[self._hi(x)].contains(self._lo(x))

    def insert(self, x):
        if self.mn == -1:
            self.mn = self.mx = x; return
        if x == self.mn or x == self.mx: return
        if x < self.mn: x, self.mn = self.mn, x               # new minimum stays here
        if self.bits > 1:
            h, l = self._hi(x), self._lo(x)
            c = self._cl(h)
            if c.mn == -1:
                self._sum().insert(h); c.mn = c.mx = l        # empty cluster: one O(1) step
            else:
                c.insert(l)
        if x > self.mx: self.mx = x

    def successor(self, x):                                   # smallest element > x, or -1
        if self.bits == 1: return 1 if (x == 0 and self.mx == 1) else -1
        if self.mn != -1 and x < self.mn: return self.mn
        h, l = self._hi(x), self._lo(x)
        c = self.cluster[h]
        if c is not None and c.mx != -1 and l < c.mx:
            return (h << self.low) | c.successor(l)
        sh = -1 if self.summary is None else self.summary.successor(h)
        return -1 if sh == -1 else (sh << self.low) | self.cluster[sh].mn

    def erase(self, x):
        if not self.contains(x): return
        if self.mn == self.mx:
            self.mn = self.mx = -1; return
        if self.bits == 1:
            self.mn = self.mx = 1 - x; return
        if x == self.mn:                                       # pull the next smallest up to be the minimum
            first = self.summary.mn
            x = (first << self.low) | self.cluster[first].mn
            self.mn = x
        h = self._hi(x)
        self.cluster[h].erase(self._lo(x))
        if self.cluster[h].mn == -1:
            self.summary.erase(h)
            if x == self.mx:
                s = self.summary.mx
                self.mx = self.mn if s == -1 else (s << self.low) | self.cluster[s].mx
        elif x == self.mx:
            self.mx = (h << self.low) | self.cluster[h].mx
--cpp
struct VEB {
    int bits, low = 0, mn = -1, mx = -1;
    unique_ptr<VEB> summary; vector<unique_ptr<VEB>> cluster;

    VEB(int bits) : bits(bits) {
        if (bits > 1) { low = bits / 2; cluster.resize(1 << (bits - low)); }
    }
    static VEB create(int universe) { return VEB(floorLog2(universe - 1) + 1); }   // piece u-log2

    int hi(int x) const { return x >> low; }
    int lo(int x) const { return x & ((1 << low) - 1); }
    VEB& cl(int h) { if (!cluster[h]) cluster[h] = make_unique<VEB>(low); return *cluster[h]; }
    VEB& sum() { if (!summary) summary = make_unique<VEB>(bits - low); return *summary; }

    bool contains(int x) const {
        if (x == mn || x == mx) return true;
        if (bits == 1 || !cluster[hi(x)]) return false;
        return cluster[hi(x)]->contains(lo(x));
    }

    void insert(int x) {
        if (mn == -1) { mn = mx = x; return; }
        if (x == mn || x == mx) return;
        if (x < mn) swap(x, mn);                              // new minimum stays here
        if (bits > 1) {
            int h = hi(x), l = lo(x);
            VEB& c = cl(h);
            if (c.mn == -1) { sum().insert(h); c.mn = c.mx = l; }   // empty cluster: one O(1) step
            else c.insert(l);
        }
        if (x > mx) mx = x;
    }

    int successor(int x) const {                              // smallest element > x, or -1
        if (bits == 1) return (x == 0 && mx == 1) ? 1 : -1;
        if (mn != -1 && x < mn) return mn;
        int h = hi(x), l = lo(x);
        const VEB* c = cluster[h].get();
        if (c && c->mx != -1 && l < c->mx) return (h << low) | c->successor(l);
        int sh = summary ? summary->successor(h) : -1;
        return sh == -1 ? -1 : (sh << low) | cluster[sh]->mn;
    }

    void erase(int x) {
        if (!contains(x)) return;
        if (mn == mx) { mn = mx = -1; return; }
        if (bits == 1) { mn = mx = 1 - x; return; }
        if (x == mn) {                                        // pull the next smallest up to be the minimum
            int first = summary->mn;
            x = (first << low) | cluster[first]->mn;
            mn = x;
        }
        int h = hi(x);
        cluster[h]->erase(lo(x));
        if (cluster[h]->mn == -1) {
            summary->erase(h);
            if (x == mx) { int s = summary->mx; mx = (s == -1) ? mn : (s << low) | cluster[s]->mx; }
        } else if (x == mx) mx = (h << low) | cluster[h]->mx;
    }
};
--uses
u-log2

### hll
--idea
HyperLogLog estimates how many distinct items a huge stream contains, using a tiny, fixed amount of memory (a few kilobytes for billions of items, with an error of about 1 to 3 percent). Counting exactly needs a set that holds every distinct item. This needs only a small array of small numbers.
The idea comes from coin flips. If you hash each item to a random-looking bit string, then a string that starts with `k` zeros has probability `1 / 2^k`. If the longest run of leading zeros you have seen among all hashes is `k`, you have probably seen about `2^k` different items. One such number is very noisy, so HyperLogLog splits the items into `m` groups by the first bits of the hash, records the longest run in each group, and combines the groups with a harmonic mean, which keeps the rare huge value from dominating.
--build
1. Choose `p` (here 10) and `m = 2^p` registers, all 0. Each register will hold the largest "rank" seen in its group. The standard error is about `1.04 / sqrt(m)` (3.3 percent for `m = 1024`).
2. Hash the item to 32 well-mixed bits. Take the rolling hash of the string (code piece `u-hashroll`, which gives a number below about a billion) and then scramble it with the `mix` function so that all 32 bits look random. A good mix of the bits matters: a weak hash ruins the estimate.
3. The first `p` bits of the hash give the register number `j`. The remaining `32 - p` bits form `w`. The rank is the position of the first 1-bit counted from the left in those `32 - p` bits, which is `(32 - p) - floorLog2(w)` when `w > 0` (code piece `u-log2` finds the highest set bit) and `32 - p + 1` when `w == 0`.
4. `add(item)`: `reg[j] = max(reg[j], rank)`. Adding the same item again changes nothing, so duplicates are free.
5. `estimate()`: compute `E = alpha * m^2 / sum over j of 2^(-reg[j])`, where `alpha = 0.7213 / (1 + 1.079 / m)`. This is a harmonic mean of the per-register guesses `2^reg[j]`.
6. Small-range correction: if `E <= 2.5 m` and `V` registers are still zero (`V > 0`), use `m * ln(m / V)` instead (this is "linear counting", accurate when few items have been seen).
7. Two sketches with the same `p` merge by taking the maximum in each register, so you can count the distinct items of a union across machines.
8. Why it works: a register that has seen `n / m` distinct items has a max rank near `log2(n / m)`. Across `m` registers the harmonic mean of `2^rank` is proportional to `n / m`, so `m x` that gives `n`. The constant `alpha` fixes the bias.
--think
- Signal: "count distinct users, IPs or queries in a giant log", memory is limited, a 2 percent error is fine. Also approximate unique counts per group (one small sketch per group) and unions of sets.
- Decide first the error you accept and pick `p` from it: error is about `1.04 / sqrt(2^p)`. The memory is `2^p` bytes (registers are small).
- Neighbouring techniques: a hash set is exact and uses memory per distinct item. A Bloom filter tests membership, not counts. A count-min sketch counts frequencies of items. K-minimum-values sketches also estimate distinct counts with simpler analysis. Sort and unique is exact but needs all the data.
- It counts distinct items only: it cannot list them, test if an item is present, or delete one.
- Pitfalls: a hash with poor high bits (the first `p` bits and the zero run must be uniform), forgetting the small-range correction (the raw estimate is badly off for small counts), using a 64-bit shift in a 32-bit field, and expecting an exact number.
--cost
Add O(1) (plus hashing the item). Estimate O(m). Space `m` small integers, for example 1024 bytes for about 3 percent error.
--java
static class HyperLogLog {
    static final int P = 10, M = 1 << P;                // 1024 registers
    int[] reg = new int[M];

    static long mix(long x) {                           // scramble a number into 32 good bits
        x ^= x >>> 16; x = (x * 0x85ebca6bL) & 0xFFFFFFFFL;
        x ^= x >>> 13; x = (x * 0xc2b2ae35L) & 0xFFFFFFFFL;
        x ^= x >>> 16;
        return x;
    }

    void add(String item) {
        long h = mix(buildHash(item)[0][item.length()]);          // piece u-hashroll, then scrambled
        int j = (int) (h >>> (32 - P));                           // first P bits pick the register
        long w = h & ((1L << (32 - P)) - 1);                      // the remaining bits
        int rank = w == 0 ? 32 - P + 1 : (32 - P) - floorLog2((int) w);   // piece u-log2: leading zeros + 1
        reg[j] = Math.max(reg[j], rank);
    }

    double estimate() {
        double alpha = 0.7213 / (1 + 1.079 / M), sum = 0; int zeros = 0;
        for (int r : reg) { sum += Math.pow(2, -r); if (r == 0) zeros++; }
        double e = alpha * M * (double) M / sum;
        if (e <= 2.5 * M && zeros > 0) e = M * Math.log((double) M / zeros);   // few items: linear counting
        return e;
    }
}
--py
import math

P = 10
REGS = 1 << P                                           # 1024 registers (the hash piece already uses the name M)

def mix(x):
    """Scramble a number into 32 good bits."""
    x ^= x >> 16; x = (x * 0x85ebca6b) & 0xFFFFFFFF
    x ^= x >> 13; x = (x * 0xc2b2ae35) & 0xFFFFFFFF
    x ^= x >> 16
    return x

class HyperLogLog:
    def __init__(self):
        self.reg = [0] * REGS

    def add(self, item):
        h = mix(build_hash(item)[0][-1])                # piece u-hashroll, then scrambled
        j = h >> (32 - P)                               # first P bits pick the register
        w = h & ((1 << (32 - P)) - 1)                   # the remaining bits
        rank = 32 - P + 1 if w == 0 else (32 - P) - floor_log2(w)    # piece u-log2: leading zeros + 1
        self.reg[j] = max(self.reg[j], rank)

    def estimate(self):
        alpha = 0.7213 / (1 + 1.079 / REGS)
        e = alpha * REGS * REGS / sum(2.0 ** -r for r in self.reg)
        zeros = self.reg.count(0)
        if e <= 2.5 * REGS and zeros > 0:
            e = REGS * math.log(REGS / zeros)                 # few items: linear counting
        return e
--cpp
struct HyperLogLog {
    static const int P = 10, M = 1 << P;                // 1024 registers
    vector<int> reg = vector<int>(M, 0);

    static uint32_t mix(uint32_t x) {                   // scramble a number into 32 good bits
        x ^= x >> 16; x *= 0x85ebca6bu;
        x ^= x >> 13; x *= 0xc2b2ae35u;
        x ^= x >> 16;
        return x;
    }

    void add(const string& item) {
        uint32_t h = mix((uint32_t)buildHash(item).first[item.size()]);   // piece u-hashroll, then scrambled
        int j = h >> (32 - P);                                            // first P bits pick the register
        uint32_t w = h & ((1u << (32 - P)) - 1);                          // the remaining bits
        int rank = w == 0 ? 32 - P + 1 : (32 - P) - floorLog2(w);         // piece u-log2: leading zeros + 1
        reg[j] = max(reg[j], rank);
    }

    double estimate() const {
        double alpha = 0.7213 / (1 + 1.079 / M), sum = 0; int zeros = 0;
        for (int r : reg) { sum += pow(2.0, -r); if (r == 0) zeros++; }
        double e = alpha * M * (double)M / sum;
        if (e <= 2.5 * M && zeros > 0) e = M * log((double)M / zeros);    // few items: linear counting
        return e;
    }
};
--uses
u-hashroll
u-log2

### dsurb
--idea
A rollback union-find is the ordinary union-find (groups of connected items) with one new power: it can undo its most recent merges, in reverse order. This matters when the problem is a timeline. You add an edge, explore what follows, and then need to go back to the state before that edge was added, as in a depth-first walk over a tree of changes.
Ordinary union-find speeds itself up with path compression, which rewrites many parents during a `find`. Those rewrites cannot be undone cheaply. So the rollback version gives up path compression and keeps only union by size. That alone keeps every tree short (height at most `log n`), so `find` costs O(log n), and each merge changes only two numbers, which are easy to remember and restore.
--build
1. Arrays `p[i]` (parent, starting as `i`) and `sz[i]` (size of the tree rooted at `i`, starting as 1). A `history` stack records each merge.
2. `find(x)` walks up `p` until `p[x] == x`. It does no path compression, because compression changes many parents and could not be undone cheaply.
3. `link(a, b)`: find the roots `ra` and `rb`. If equal, record nothing and return false. Otherwise the smaller tree will be hung under the larger one: `child` is the root with the smaller size (when the sizes are equal, `rb`), and the other is its new parent. Push `child` onto `history`, then perform the merge with the plain `union(a, b)` (code piece `u-dsuunion`), which hangs the smaller tree under the larger and adds the sizes.
4. `snapshot()` returns `history.size()`. It is the marker for "the state right now".
5. `rollback(to)`: while `history.size() > to`, pop a `child`, let `parent = p[child]`, do `sz[parent] -= sz[child]`, and set `p[child] = child`. This exactly reverses the merge, and because merges are undone newest first, each undo sees the same state that the merge saw.
6. Use pattern: take a snapshot, do some `link` calls, answer queries with `find`, then `rollback(snapshot)`.
7. Offline dynamic connectivity: edges that exist during a time interval are placed on the nodes of a segment tree over time (each edge on `O(log T)` nodes). A depth-first walk over the segment tree does `link` for every edge on a node on the way down and `rollback` on the way up. At each leaf (one moment of time) the structure holds exactly the edges alive then, so connectivity queries are answered there.
8. Invariant: the groups after `rollback(s)` are identical to the groups at the moment `snapshot()` returned `s`, because every merge changes only `p[child]` and `sz[parent]`, and both are restored.
--think
- Signal: edges are added and later removed (known in advance, offline), questions like "is the graph connected at time `t`?", "number of components at each moment", or a divide and conquer / depth-first search where each branch adds a few edges and the sibling branch must not see them.
- Decide first whether it is offline. If all edges and queries are known up front, a segment tree over time plus this structure works. If online (edges deleted unpredictably), use a link-cut tree or other heavier methods.
- Neighbouring techniques: plain union-find if you only add edges. Link-cut tree for online link and cut in forests. Rebuilding the union-find from scratch at every query is simple but too slow. Mo's algorithm with rollback also uses this structure, for range queries on a graph.
- Union by size is mandatory. Without it, the `find` cost is not bounded, since you gave up compression.
- Pitfalls: adding path compression "for speed" (breaks undo), rolling back to a stale snapshot taken before a rollback already passed it, recording a merge that did not happen (when the roots were equal), and forgetting that the size must be reduced on the parent.
--cost
`find` O(log n). `link` O(log n). `rollback` of one merge O(1). Space O(n) plus the history.
--java
static class RollbackDSU {
    int[] p, sz; int[] history; int top = 0;
    RollbackDSU(int n) {
        p = new int[n]; sz = new int[n]; history = new int[n];       // at most n-1 merges are ever recorded
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { p[i] = i; sz[i] = 1; }
    }
    int find(int x) { while (p[x] != x) x = p[x]; return x; }        // no path compression: it could not be undone

    boolean link(int a, int b) {
        int ra = find(a), rb = find(b);
        if (ra == rb) return false;                                  // nothing to record
        history[top++] = sz[ra] < sz[rb] ? ra : rb;                  // the root that gets hung below
        union(a, b);                                                 // piece u-dsuunion does the merge
        return true;
    }

    int snapshot() { return top; }

    void rollback(int to) {
        while (top > to) {
            int child = history[--top], parent = p[child];
            sz[parent] -= sz[child];                                 // undo the size change
            p[child] = child;                                        // cut the child loose again
        }
    }
}
--py
p, sz, history = [], [], []                       # module level, as the piece u-dsuunion expects

def init(n):
    global p, sz, history
    p, sz, history = list(range(n)), [1] * n, []

def find(x):
    while p[x] != x:                              # no path compression: it could not be undone
        x = p[x]
    return x

def link(a, b):
    ra, rb = find(a), find(b)
    if ra == rb:
        return False                              # nothing to record
    history.append(ra if sz[ra] < sz[rb] else rb)  # the root that gets hung below
    union(a, b)                                   # piece u-dsuunion does the merge
    return True

def snapshot():
    return len(history)

def rollback(to):
    while len(history) > to:
        child = history.pop()
        parent = p[child]
        sz[parent] -= sz[child]                   # undo the size change
        p[child] = child                          # cut the child loose again
--cpp
struct RollbackDSU {
    vector<int> p, sz, history;
    RollbackDSU(int n) : p(n), sz(n, 1) { iota(p.begin(), p.end(), 0); }
    int find(int x) const { while (p[x] != x) x = p[x]; return x; }   // no path compression: it could not be undone

    bool link(int a, int b) {
        int ra = find(a), rb = find(b);
        if (ra == rb) return false;                                   // nothing to record
        history.push_back(sz[ra] < sz[rb] ? ra : rb);                 // the root that gets hung below
        unite(a, b);                                                  // piece u-dsuunion does the merge
        return true;
    }

    int snapshot() const { return history.size(); }

    void rollback(int to) {
        while ((int)history.size() > to) {
            int child = history.back(); history.pop_back();
            int parent = p[child];
            sz[parent] -= sz[child];                                  // undo the size change
            p[child] = child;                                         // cut the child loose again
        }
    }
};
--uses
u-dsuunion

### dsparse
--idea
A disjoint sparse table answers range queries for any associative operation (sum, product, minimum, gcd, matrix product, string concatenation) in O(1) after an O(n log n) preparation. The ordinary sparse table gets O(1) only for operations where applying an item twice changes nothing (min, max, gcd), because it covers a range with two overlapping blocks. The disjoint version covers every range with two blocks that do not overlap, so it works for sum and non-commutative operations as well.
It does this by making every query cross a "midpoint" that was prepared in advance. At level `h`, the array is cut into blocks of size `2^h`, and each block has a midpoint. For every position left of the midpoint, the table stores the combined value from that position to the midpoint; for every position right of it, the combined value from the midpoint to that position. A query `[l, r]` that straddles the midpoint of some block is just the left piece combined with the right piece.
--build
1. Pad the array length up to a power of two `N = 2^L` (pad with any value, it is never used by a valid query). Make `L + 1` levels `tab[0 .. L]`, each an array of length `N`.
2. For a level `h` from 1 to `L`: cut positions into blocks of length `2^h`. The block starting at `s` has its midpoint at `mid = s + 2^(h-1)`.
3. Left half of the block: set `tab[h][mid - 1] = a[mid - 1]`. Then for `i = mid - 2` down to `s`: `tab[h][i] = op(a[i], tab[h][i + 1])`. So `tab[h][i]` is the combined value of `a[i .. mid - 1]`, with `op` applied in array order.
4. Right half: set `tab[h][mid] = a[mid]`. Then for `i = mid + 1` up to `s + 2^h - 1`: `tab[h][i] = op(tab[h][i - 1], a[i])`. So `tab[h][i]` is the combined value of `a[mid .. i]`.
5. Query `[l, r]` (inclusive): if `l == r`, return `a[l]`. Otherwise find the level at which `l` and `r` first fall in the same block but on different sides of its midpoint. That level is `h = floorLog2(l xor r) + 1` (code piece `u-log2`), because the highest bit where `l` and `r` differ is the bit that separates the left half from the right half of their common block.
6. The answer is `op(tab[h][l], tab[h][r])`: the left piece `a[l .. mid-1]` followed by the right piece `a[mid .. r]`, in order, with no overlap.
7. Each level costs `O(N)` to fill, and there are `log N` levels, so the build is `O(N log N)`. Memory is the same.
8. Invariant: `tab[h][i]` always covers exactly the items between `i` and the midpoint of its level-`h` block, so for any `l < r` the two stored pieces tile `[l, r]` exactly.
--think
- Signal: many range queries on a static array where the operation is associative but not idempotent (range sum without subtraction, range product modulo a number that is not prime, matrix product, "composition of functions", string hashing of a range). Also when you want O(1) queries and a sparse table does not apply.
- Decide first the operation and check it is associative. It does not need to be commutative: the code combines the left piece first, then the right.
- Neighbouring techniques: prefix sums answer sums in O(1) when subtraction is allowed. A sparse table answers min, max, gcd in O(1) with less code. A segment tree handles updates but costs O(log n) per query. A disjoint sparse table is the tool for "static, associative, not idempotent, many queries".
- The xor trick finds the level in O(1) with one bit operation, which is what makes the query constant time.
- Pitfalls: padding to a power of two (the xor trick needs it), answering `l == r` separately (the level would be undefined), mixing up the order of the pieces in a non-commutative operation, and using too much memory for huge `n` (it is `n log n` entries).
--cost
Build O(n log n) time and space. Query O(1).
--java
static class DisjointSparse {
    int n, N, L; long[][] tab; long[] a;
    java.util.function.LongBinaryOperator op;

    DisjointSparse(long[] arr, java.util.function.LongBinaryOperator op) {
        this.op = op; n = arr.length;
        N = 1; L = 0;
        while (N < n) { N <<= 1; L++; }
        if (N == 1) { N = 2; L = 1; }                             // keep at least one level
        a = java.util.Arrays.copyOf(arr, N);                      // padding is never read by a valid query
        tab = new long[L + 1][N];
        for (int h = 1; h <= L; h++) {
            int half = 1 << (h - 1);
            for (int s = 0; s < N; s += 1 << h) {
                int mid = s + half;
                tab[h][mid - 1] = a[mid - 1];
                for (int i = mid - 2; i >= s; i--) tab[h][i] = op.applyAsLong(a[i], tab[h][i + 1]);   // left of mid
                tab[h][mid] = a[mid];
                for (int i = mid + 1; i < s + (1 << h); i++) tab[h][i] = op.applyAsLong(tab[h][i - 1], a[i]);   // right of mid
            }
        }
    }

    long query(int l, int r) {                                    // inclusive
        if (l == r) return a[l];
        int h = floorLog2(l ^ r) + 1;                             // piece u-log2: the level where l and r split
        return op.applyAsLong(tab[h][l], tab[h][r]);              // left piece, then right piece
    }
}
--py
class DisjointSparse:
    def __init__(self, arr, op):
        self.op = op
        n = len(arr)
        N, L = 1, 0
        while N < n:
            N <<= 1; L += 1
        if N == 1: N, L = 2, 1                              # keep at least one level
        self.a = list(arr) + [arr[0]] * (N - n)             # padding is never read by a valid query
        self.tab = [[None] * N for _ in range(L + 1)]
        a = self.a
        for h in range(1, L + 1):
            half = 1 << (h - 1)
            for s in range(0, N, 1 << h):
                mid = s + half
                self.tab[h][mid - 1] = a[mid - 1]
                for i in range(mid - 2, s - 1, -1):         # left of mid
                    self.tab[h][i] = op(a[i], self.tab[h][i + 1])
                self.tab[h][mid] = a[mid]
                for i in range(mid + 1, s + (1 << h)):      # right of mid
                    self.tab[h][i] = op(self.tab[h][i - 1], a[i])

    def query(self, l, r):                                  # inclusive
        if l == r: return self.a[l]
        h = floor_log2(l ^ r) + 1                           # piece u-log2: the level where l and r split
        return self.op(self.tab[h][l], self.tab[h][r])      # left piece, then right piece
--cpp
struct DisjointSparse {
    int n, N = 1, L = 0; vector<vector<long long>> tab; vector<long long> a;
    function<long long(long long, long long)> op;

    DisjointSparse(const vector<long long>& arr, function<long long(long long, long long)> op) : n(arr.size()), op(op) {
        while (N < n) { N <<= 1; L++; }
        if (N == 1) { N = 2; L = 1; }                               // keep at least one level
        a = arr; a.resize(N, 0);                                    // padding is never read by a valid query
        tab.assign(L + 1, vector<long long>(N));
        for (int h = 1; h <= L; h++) {
            int half = 1 << (h - 1);
            for (int s = 0; s < N; s += 1 << h) {
                int mid = s + half;
                tab[h][mid - 1] = a[mid - 1];
                for (int i = mid - 2; i >= s; i--) tab[h][i] = op(a[i], tab[h][i + 1]);          // left of mid
                tab[h][mid] = a[mid];
                for (int i = mid + 1; i < s + (1 << h); i++) tab[h][i] = op(tab[h][i - 1], a[i]);   // right of mid
            }
        }
    }

    long long query(int l, int r) const {                           // inclusive
        if (l == r) return a[l];
        int h = floorLog2(l ^ r) + 1;                               // piece u-log2: the level where l and r split
        return op(tab[h][l], tab[h][r]);                            // left piece, then right piece
    }
};
--uses
u-log2
